Seabirds racing home over the ocean can get jet-lagged
2026-10-31

Seabirds racing home over the ocean can get jet-lagged

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Flying across several time zones often causes jet lag, a feeling when the body insists it’s the middle of the night while the clock says it’s lunchtime. Now it turns out this nuisance is not exclusive to humans. A new Science study on Manx shearwaters, seabirds that can fly hundreds of kilometers a day over the North Atlantic, shows they jet-lag themselves during their excursions. And because these birds navigate by the Sun, that jet lag leaves a measurable mark—it skews their compass. Patrick Lewin is a behavioral and sensory ecologist at the University of Oxford and lead author of the study. “Manx shearwaters are medium-sized birds, about the same size as a pigeon,” Lewin said. “They are white underneath and black on top, but they have very long wings, and they are closely related to albatross.” Manx shearwaters nest in burrows on coastal islands around the North Atlantic. Most breed around the UK and Ireland, with about half of their population living in Wales. From there, they head out to feed anywhere from a few dozen to more than 1,000 kilometers into the Atlantic. To get back, shearwaters are thought to work out where they are located using a map sense, possibly based on smells, and then use the Sun as a compass to set their direction. The trouble with navigating by the Sun, though, is that it moves across the sky. “You can imagine an animal or a human that wants to travel south all day. At sunrise, when the Sun’s in the east, it needs to be traveling 90 degrees clockwise of the Sun, but by midday it needs to be traveling straight towards the Sun, and by sunset it needs to be traveling 90 degrees anticlockwise of the Sun,” Lewin said. Effective navigation by the Sun requires a clock that tells you what time of day it is. Birds, like nearly all animals, have such a clock built in. But the clock in Manx shearwaters is not perfect. The birds recalibrate their internal clock at every dusk and dawn. This means that for every degree of longitude a bird covers between sunrise and sunset, its clock falls four minutes out of sync with local time. In consequence, a Manx shearwater misjudges where the Sun should be and veers off course clockwise when it flies east, or anticlockwise when it flies west. Scientists had already shown something similar in other birds by catching penguins and quickly moving them across lines of longitude, but before Lewin’s study no one had clearly demonstrated it in wild animals moving on their own, mostly because the effect is small, and so requires a lot of data to become apparent. But Lewin’s team happened to have just the right database. Tim Guilford, Lewin’s supervisor at Oxford, and Chris Perrins, a long-time Oxford shearwater researcher, started fitting shearwaters with GPS tags in 2004, when the devices first became small enough for a bird this size. Two decades later, the team had tracks from 3,688 foraging trips made by 1,045 birds from colonies along the UK’s western coasts. Those trips last from one day up to two weeks. From this dataset, Lewin and his colleagues separated homeward flights, which the scientists say was rather simple. Foraging shearwaters make short, wiggly flights between dives. “When they are deciding to go back to their colony, they kind of switch into this mode where they are flying as fast as they can in pretty much a straight line continuously without stopping for really long periods,” Lewin said. For each homing flight, the researchers worked out where the bird last saw the sunrise, at which point its clock should have matched local time. Then, at every GPS fix, they calculated how far east or west it had traveled since, and how much that would bend its Sun compass. Since they knew each bird was headed exactly to its burrow, they could compare those predictions with how the birds actually deviated from their course. The time shifts were tiny. “About an hour is pretty much the maximum that we observed. Most of these are probably 15-minute, 10-minute time differences,” Lewin says. “For us, that would be pretty imperceptible, but it does still have an effect on their compass orientation that we can detect.” Across 335 homing flights made out of sight of land, the birds veered just as the Sun compass predictions said they would. Because the study was not a controlled experiment, though, Lewin argues we need to be careful about causality. Still, the team calculated the biases that crosswinds, the Coriolis effect, or a magnetic compass might introduce, and none of those fit the data well. “Our observed biases match by far the best to the Sun compass biases that we would expect,” Lewin said. The navigational errors apparently caused by jet lag reached up to around 15 degrees, which for birds was way less costly than one might expect. A bird with a map sense can check its position, notice it’s drifting, and correct. The team calculated that for a shearwater flying 1,000 kilometers east in a day, about the maximum the scientists observed, the average error would be roughly seven degrees. If the bird keeps checking where it is, that adds only five kilometers to the trip. The researchers also found that when shearwaters followed coastlines, they still drifted slightly closer to or farther from the shore depending on their expected compass error. Work done in Oxford two decades ago showed that homing pigeons with shifted clocks do the same, even while following a familiar road. This led researchers to hypothesize that birds may run two parallel navigation systems, one based on the compass and one on familiar landmarks, with the balance between them never tipping entirely to one side. An alternative explanation, Lewin suggests, might be that they don’t know exactly where on the coast they are and use the Sun to pick which way to follow it. “We don’t quite know. And I think it’s an interesting question, especially as people try and design bio-inspired autonomous navigation systems,” Lewin says. Lewin’s study could not answer whether Manx shearwaters suffer from other jet lag symptoms like poor sleep or hunger at odd hours. “We’ve not been able to investigate that yet,” Lewin said. The next step for his team would be finding out whether the time shifts affect when birds forage, how much food they bring home, and their breeding success. Another question is whether more animals routinely experience jet lag. Lewin thinks it’s quite likely. The same jet lag effect, Lewin said, could hit any organism with an internal clock that moves far enough east or west in a single day. That mostly means fliers, or animals living near the poles, where lines of longitude are packed closer together. The long-term goal is to find out whether animals that constantly suffer from jet lag evolved some ways to cope with it. “In humans, if you do it a lot, in the long term it has really negative health impacts,” Lewin said. “So maybe we could take some inspiration from these animals that are constantly experiencing jet lag and see if there are ways that we can mitigate jet lag affecting humans.” Science, 2026.  DOI: 10.1126/science.adz2542

The FDA wants to pull its approval of a drug. It’s not so easy.
2026-10-31

The FDA wants to pull its approval of a drug. It’s not so easy.

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
In May 2021, the US Food and Drug Administration released a lengthy briefing document on an experimental medication for a rare autoimmune form of vasculitis that often damages the kidneys and lungs. The agency was on the fence about whether to approve the treatment, and it wanted an independent advisory committee to weigh in. Among the questions at hand: Was the drug meaningfully better than existing therapies? Participants in the study, which had been conducted by the California biotechnology company ChemoCentryx, did appear to have slightly higher rates of symptom remission after one year. But according to the FDA, it was hard to interpret the results because the study’s design had been less than optimal. Some advisory committee members expressed a similar view. The effect of the drug, said one, “is just not well verified by this study.” At the same time, many patients, and some physicians, saw in the data a potentially helpful medication, one that might lessen patients’ reliance on steroids, which are highly effective but often cause severe side effects. The advisory committee ultimately voted narrowly to recommend approval, and the FDA followed that advice, greenlighting the drug on October 7, 2021. The drug, marketed as Tavneos, started making its way into clinics. A year after approval, ChemoCentryx was acquired by Amgen, one of the world’s largest pharmaceutical companies. The cost, even today, isn’t minor: $220,000 per year, according to Drugs.com. In fiscal year 2025, sales of Tavneos reached nearly half a billion dollars. This financial windfall has unfolded despite a long-roiling investor lawsuit—one that incidentally uncovered what the FDA today believes is evidence that ChemoCentryx employees manipulated the data in the pivotal trial submitted to the agency. In April of this year, the FDA issued a letter proposing that Tavneos be withdrawn from the US market, citing reports of serious, sometimes fatal, liver injury, and arguing that the drug now lacks “substantial evidence” that it works. These revelations have triggered a dramatic cascade of events. This summer, the New England Journal of Medicine retracted a paper describing the clinical trial and Tavneos was taken off the market in the European Union. The United Kingdom has announced that it, too, will remove the product from the market. And on October 29, a court hearing is expected to proceed over Zoom to consider a proposed $69 million settlement for the lawsuit. But the fate of Tavneos, at least in the United States, is far from sealed. Amgen has pushed back against its critics, insisting that—whatever the details of ChemoCentryx’s data handling—its drug works as advertised and should stay on the market. In July, the company submitted a 104-page document to the FDA stating that “the totality of the evidence supports the continued availability of TAVNEOS.” Some patients and academic experts also believe the drug is an important treatment option. “This appears to be an efficacious medication,” said Eric Roberts, an epidemiologist at the University of California, San Francisco, who co-authored an independent registry study of Tavneos. The dispute is emblematic of the FDA’s longstanding struggle to regulate drugs of uncertain efficacy, said Reshma Ramachandran, a physician and co-director of the Yale Collaboration for Regulatory Rigor, Integrity, and Transparency. Absent more rigorous studies, either before or after a drug goes on the market, Ramachandran worries, “we’re just going to be living in uncertainty in terms of whether or not these drugs actually work.” For American consumers, the saga of Tavneos also raises potent questions around the safeguards in place to ensure the integrity of drug approvals, particularly when a relatively small company conducts the pivotal trial and has so much riding on the success of a single drug. Chloe Cusano was a 20-year-old college student when she was struck, this past December, with unbearable pain in her legs, as well as bouts of breathlessness from routine tasks like walking upstairs or bending down to retrieve her dog’s water dish. After going to the emergency room, she was hospitalized for more than two weeks, during which she was diagnosed with ANCA-associated vasculitis, or AAV, an umbrella term for a group of rare diseases in which the immune system mistakenly attacks its own blood vessels. When it was first identified, the condition was usually fatal. Over the past four decades, scientists have developed drugs that allow many AAV patients to live a relatively normal lifespan. Because the disease can rapidly lead to organ failure, particularly of the kidneys and lungs, patients are immediately started on a high dose of steroids to rapidly tamp down inflammation. They also typically receive slower-acting medications that target the underlying disease process. Steroids, however, have severe side effects. Within a month and a half of going on the steroids, Cusano recalled, she gained 40 pounds. Her emotions were “just like crazy,” swinging from intense sadness to anger. She had trouble falling asleep, and when she did fall asleep, she was haunted by another side effect: nightmares. “I did not like it at all,” she recalls. AAV patients may need to take steroids for months to years, and Tavneos was supposed to replace or reduce their use. David Pisetsky, a rheumatologist and researcher at the Duke University School of Medicine, was on the FDA advisory committee that considered the ChemoCentryx trial data. He voted against recommending approval, in part because many patients in the control group received just one round of a slower-acting medication — rituximab — in addition to steroids when, in the real world, they would have received two, according to Pisetsky. The trial’s design, he said, made the results difficult to interpret. In an editorial in the journal Rheumatology, Mike Putman, medical director of the Vasculitis Program at the Medical College of Wisconsin, and co-authors would later make a similar case: Patients in the comparison group did not receive treatment consistent with the current standard of care. “It is somewhat debate-able about what the ‘standard of care’ would have been” when the study was done, Putman wrote in an email to Undark. “We had not yet established rituximab as maintenance therapy at that point, though most people were already doing that.” Other members of the advisory committee were persuaded, including Walter Kraft, a clinical pharmacologist and researcher at Thomas Jefferson University. He voted yes to approval. “I felt the evidence, though limited, met the societal and therapeutic goals,” he said during the meeting. Requiring another trial, he added, could delay the drug’s approval by several years. But that vote, he told Undark in a recent interview, was “based upon an assumption that the sponsor was following the rules of the road.” These days, a significant number of drugs approved by the FDA start out at a small company with few or no medications on the market, said Sean Nicholson, an economist at Cornell University. Large companies increasingly recognize that their comparative advantage is not in developing breakthrough science, which is more likely to come from “nimble, small startup biotechs,” so rather than trying to compete, Nicholson said, a big company will look to buy or partner with the smaller company. That’s what happened with ChemoCentryx, which was bought by Amgen for nearly $4 billion one year after the FDA issued its approval of Tavneos. When bringing a drug to market, small companies face a slightly different set of incentives from large companies, said David Ridley, a health economist at the Duke University Fuqua School of Business. A large company with many products in its pipeline may more readily abandon an experimental drug rather than invest in costly late-stage testing. “Small companies have more to lose,” he wrote in an email to Undark. “The entire company can be at stake. That urgency can drive them to work harder and move faster.” “In rare cases,” he added, “they might cut corners.” Back in May 2021, Tavneos’ approval appeared far from certain. Almost immediately after the FDA released its briefing document for the advisory committee meeting, ChemoCentryx’s stock value plummeted, and its shareholders sued for securities fraud. Four years later, as part of that lawsuit, a report was filed, alleging that ChemoCentryx had not, in fact, followed the rules of the road. The report indicated that the company’s own trial analysis originally found that the patients receiving Tavneos had not achieved better one-year remission rates than the patients who received the usual treatment. After company employees unblinded the trial data—meaning they looked to see which study participants had actually received the drug and which had received a placebo—they selected a handful of patients whose remission status would be reassessed. This readjudication, as it is called by the FDA, violated the study’s protocol. What’s more, the agency alleges, the patients were cherry-picked to get the results the company needed for approval. ChemoCentryx did not disclose the original results, nor the fact that it had reassessed the original data, to the FDA. In December 2025, Tracy Beth Høeg became acting director of the FDA’s Center for Drug Evaluation and Research, or CDER. In an email to Undark, Høeg noted that legally, she can’t speak to the specifics of how this issue came to her attention. But, she noted, “One of my key roles as CDER acting director was letting everyone know my door was open, and I encouraged CDER employees to speak openly with me, especially about matters potentially affecting public safety.” “When I learned of this case,” she continued, “I distinctly remember thinking this story had so much intrigue that it could easily be made into a movie.” But the repercussions, Høeg argued with unusual candor, are very real. “Essentially ChemoCentryx swindled billions of dollars by developing a drug that gained FDA approval only because they manipulated the data,” she wrote. Since the drug went onto the market, it has been linked to eight deaths and dozens of hospitalizations. “This can be a deadly drug,” wrote Høeg, “and we don’t know if it works.” Such was the essence of the FDA’s letter sent in April to Amgen, in which the agency proposed to withdraw approval of Tavneos. The retraction of the Tavneos trial data by the New England Journal of Medicine followed two months later, at the request of the study’s two academic authors. “This will be a pivotal moment for rheumatology,” wrote Putman, in a lengthy essay on Substack. It’s a story not just about Tavneos, he argued, but evidence that the normal systems of scientific oversight — including peer review and regulatory approval — “failed to detect a shocking case” of alleged data manipulation. According to Amgen, ChemoCentryx’s actions were less than ideal, but they don’t amount to data manipulation—and they certainly don’t warrant withdrawal of Tavneos. In the 104-page document submitted to the FDA this summer, Amgen said that ChemoCentryx’s re-adjudication doesn’t “align with the approach Amgen would have taken.” Amgen called it “unfortunate” that ChemoCentryx didn’t disclose its actions to the FDA, noting that it, too, had been in the dark about this. Still, Amgen pointed out, none of the underlying data were actually changed, and the reassessment of the data, Amgen maintains, was “conducted to correct a small number of errors.” ChemoCentryx was not legally required to disclose the fact that it revisited the data to the FDA, Amgen maintains. In an emailed response to questions from Undark that Alison Chartan, director of Amgen’s global media relations team, requested be attributed to a company spokesperson, Amgen reiterated many of the points made in the document submitted to the FDA. “As stated in our response to CDER,” the email noted, “the 2019 data readjudication conducted by ChemoCentryx followed a prespecified procedure to correctly and consistently apply prespecified trial rules to adjudicated outcomes. No clinical data were altered.” The email also emphasized the real-world impact a withdrawal would have on those currently taking Tavneos. “Preserving treatment options for these patients,” Amgen wrote, “is central to our mission.” (The company plans to present new data on Tavneos at a conference in November.) The FDA does have the authority to withdraw its approval of a drug, said Patricia Zettler, a law professor at The Ohio State University who formerly served as an attorney for the Department of Health and Human Services. The law requires a stepwise approach. As an opening gambit, the agency must give notice and offer the drug’s manufacturer an opportunity for a public hearing. If the manufacturer can demonstrate a genuine disagreement about the facts, then the hearing may be held. After that, the FDA can withdraw approval of the drug. If Amgen disagrees with the decision, it could sue the agency. All of this leaves patients and physicians in a potentially protracted state of uncertainty about whether Tavneos actually works. Amgen says it does. To make its case, the company has pointed to an independent re-adjudication of its pivotal trial, conducted by researchers at the Duke Clinical Research Institute, part of the Duke University School of Medicine. Although the drug still didn’t meet the FDA’s bar of higher one-year remission rates, the study participants who received the drug fared no worse on this measure. The company also pointed to real-world data, meaning data collected via routine medical care, suggesting that Tavneos can indeed reduce steroid use. Chloe Cusano is among those who would like to see Tavneos retain its approval. She received it while in the hospital and credits it with helping her taper off steroids more rapidly. Roberts, the UCSF epidemiologist, said his team’s registry study found low rates of drug-induced liver injuries among patients in the U.S., a finding he characterized as “incredibly reassuring,” particularly when care is coupled with careful monitoring. It’s important to keep in mind, he said, that the current alternative to Tavneos is “much higher levels” of steroids. Under the law, Tavneos “really shouldn’t remain on the market,” said Joshua Skydel, a clinical fellow in rheumatology at Yale New Haven Hospital and a researcher at the Yale Collaboration for Regulatory Rigor, Integrity, and Transparency. The FDA has standards for drug approval, including substantial evidence of safety and effectiveness. In this case, the pivotal trial has been called into question, and several of the studies that Amgen has pointed to have yet to be published: “I’d at least like to see them go through peer review,” Skydel said, “before hanging my hat on them.” Høeg, meanwhile, is skeptical that clinical evidence gathered since Tavneos was approved will do much to support its case. “I suspect real world data, much of which were already available when we requested withdrawal in April, will not meet the FDA’s evidentiary bar for approval,” she wrote. “We do know the data were insufficient for ongoing marketing authorization [in] the EU and UK.” Skydel additionally noted that at the time of Tavneos’ approval, the FDA requested that additional post-marketing studies be conducted. But Amgen has fallen behind on deadlines for those studies. “This episode really, for me, highlighted the way that drugs are allowed to reach the market with open questions,” Skydel said. “Our capacity to answer those questions is really only as strong as FDA and industry’s collaboration,” he continued, as well as FDA’s willingness to use its authority after a drug has gone on the market. In its April letter, the FDA argued that the stakes of this debate go beyond patients with one rare disease, stating that withdrawing the drug “is critical to protect the public health and the integrity of the drug approval process.” Absent such a consequence, the agency stated, other companies will have an incentive to act deceptively. Walter Kraft, who voted to recommend approving the drug based on what he knew at the time, also worries about the potential for knock-on effects. The clinical trial system “is built on a level of trust,” he said. “And when that trust is put into question for a narrow parochial gain, it puts the whole system at risk for other subsequent actors in the space.” This article was originally published on Undark. Read the original article.

Once again, NASA is asking for ideas to rescue Hubble from certain death
2026-10-31

Once again, NASA is asking for ideas to rescue Hubble from certain death

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
The Hubble Space Telescope still shines in the eyes of astronomers after 36 years in orbit, but it is not as nimble as it once was. Left alone, the observatory will plunge back into the atmosphere in the early-to-mid-2030s, closing the book on one of the most famous scientific instruments of the century. NASA last raised the orbit of Hubble in 2002, when the Space Shuttle Columbia visited the observatory for a servicing mission. The final shuttle servicing flight in 2009 did not reboost the telescope’s orbit, and with the retirement of the shuttle fleet two years later, Hubble has steadily lost altitude with no way to stave off eventual destruction. The space agency released a Request For Information (RFI) on Thursday asking commercial companies for ideas on how to reboost, service, or a controlled disposal of Hubble. Responses are due November 30. Specifically, NASA is looking for information about companies’ current and near-term capabilities for capturing Hubble and boosting it to an altitude of at least 373 miles (600 km), enhancing the telescope’s performance, or guiding Hubble to a controlled reentry. “The requirement includes that the boost and/or servicing mission must not harm the HST and that this mission is expected to occur by 2032,” NASA wrote in the RFI. The contractor would be responsible for providing the launch vehicle, servicing spacecraft, mission operations, and “crew if applicable.” NASA is not committing to buying a Hubble rescue mission, but officials want to know who might be interested in mounting such a mission, how much it would cost, and which companies would have a realistic shot at reaching Hubble before it falls out of orbit and burns up on reentry. Orbital dynamicists currently predict that it will likely happen in 2033 or 2034 without intervention, but reentry forecasts can change based on solar activity. It took 24 years for Hubble, which lacks onboard propulsion, to glide from its 2002 altitude of 362 miles (583 km) to its current orbit some 292 miles (470 km) above Earth. A reboost to NASA’s requirement of 373 miles (600 km) would likely keep Hubble in orbit for at least 25 more years. You’ll be forgiven if you’ve heard this before. Jared Isaacman, a billionaire commercial astronaut, approached NASA in 2022 about a servicing mission to Hubble with a team of astronauts on SpaceX’s Dragon spacecraft. He proposed funding most of the mission, which would have reboosted Hubble by at least 30 miles (50 km). Engineers also studied using Dragon to attach a rescue pod onto Hubble with new gyroscopes to overcome problems with the telescope’s debilitated pointing system. NASA also released an RFI in 2022 for other teams to share how they might boost Hubble’s orbit. The agency received eight responses, including a joint robotic reboost proposal from Astroscale and Momentus to pair their satellite servicing technology and in-space transport capabilities. Agency officials announced in 2024 they would not pursue a reboost mission after reviewing the responses. Mark Clampin, the head of NASA’s astrophysics division at the time, said the servicing plans from Isaacman and SpaceX “were really just notional concepts.” Fast forward to 2026. Isaacman is now NASA administrator, and the satellite servicing industry is maturing. Northrop Grumman launched the world’s most sophisticated robotic servicing satellite earlier this year in a partnership with DARPA, the Pentagon’s research and development agency. Smaller startups like Astroscale and Starfish Space have contracts for servicing and refueling demos with the US Space Force and NASA. So it’s not unrealistic to think the commercial industry could reboost and service Hubble by 2032, given enough time and resources. That was one of the lessons learned from NASA’s last-minute bid to rescue one of its smaller space telescopes, named Swift, from an impending reentry. NASA awarded a $30 million contract to a startup named Katalyst Space Technologies last year and gave the company just nine months to develop a mission to fly up to Swift, grab onto it, and propel it to a higher orbit, extending its useful lifetime. Remarkably, Katalyst built a rescue satellite and launched it by the deadline, and got within about 6 miles (10 km) of Swift. Ground teams decided not to move closer after problems with Katalyst’s power and reaction control systems. The Swift rescue attempt showed a servicing mission doesn’t have to cost billions of dollars, as one of NASA’s now-canceled servicing demo projects was predicted to cost. But Hubble is a significantly more expensive asset than Swift, so if NASA goes forward with a rescue mission, the agency would like to give a contractor more than $30 million and nine months. NASA does currently not have any funding allocated to procuring a Hubble reboost or servicing mission. “Hubble still has important science ahead of it, and we should be looking at every reasonable opportunity to keep it delivering for as long as possible,” Isaacman said in a NASA statement. “If there are innovative and cost-effective ways to extend the life of an asset already on orbit, we owe it to the American taxpayer and the scientific community to explore them. Extending Hubble’s life would mean more discoveries, more science, and more opportunities to expand our understanding of the Universe.” A crewed servicing mission like the one Isaacman envisioned in 2022 is unlikely to happen now. SpaceX aims to retire its Crew Dragon fleet by 2030. In a post on X, Isaacman wrote that he expects the new RFI will “draw interest from robotics companies.” NASA’s newer flagship observatories, sometimes described as successors to Hubble, are not actually one-for-one replacements. Hubble is tuned to observe the Universe in visible and ultraviolet light, while the Webb and Roman telescopes peer deeper into the infrared Universe. Astronomers will likely have to wait for the launch of NASA’s Habitable Worlds Observatory in the 2040s for an improvement on Hubble’s visible and ultraviolet vision. “Commercial industry has rapidly evolved in recent years to offer new capabilities for in-space satellite capture and servicing,” said Nicky Fox, NASA’s associate administrator for science. “Given the time we have until Hubble’s anticipated reentry, we are eager to explore the breadth of possibilities to boost Hubble to a higher orbit while we develop the flagship mission to follow.”

Flesh-eating screwworms spread to third state and are attacking pets
2026-10-30

Flesh-eating screwworms spread to third state and are attacking pets

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
For a genuine fright this Halloween, you can turn your gaze to the southern border, where ravenous, flesh-eating maggots are festering and have taken to eating people’s pets. This week, the country’s New World Screwworm (NWS) infestation spread to a third state, Arizona, which joins New Mexico and Texas in being plagued by the horrifying parasites. To date, 74 animal cases have been identified in the US since the flies re-invaded the country in June. The flies lay hundreds of eggs in wounds and moist orifices of animals, and the resulting voracious maggots feast on their victims. The maggots resemble screws and tortuously bore and twist into living flesh, causing severe, sometimes fatal wounds, before dropping to the ground to develop into adults. Because of their ability to ravage livestock, the flies were eliminated from the country in the 1960s through aerial releases of sterile male flies, which thwart successful breeding. For years, they were held at bay using a biological barrier along the Darién Gap at the border of Panama and Colombia. But the barrier was breached in 2022, and the flies have made their way back to the US through Central America. While they are largely seen as a threat to the livestock industry, they can also devastate wildlife, domestic pets, and in rarer cases, humans. According to the latest data from Mexico’s Ministry of Health, the country has confirmed 806 cases of human screwworm infestations since the flies reemerged. Nine people died from the savage maggots. In the US, most of the 74 confirmed cases have been in livestock. But some of the cases have been in domestic pets, mainly dogs. Just this week, the US Department of Agriculture recorded four new cases in domestic dogs—which includes the new detection in Arizona—as well as a domestic cat in New Mexico. That brings the pet total to 10 cases since the flies reemerged. The US has also detected one case in a wild elk, which was reported last week. Arizona’s agriculture department said this week that the state’s index case was identified in a dog in Cochise County, which borders both New Mexico and Mexico. A local veterinarian examined the poor pup on October 26 and found a wound on the canine’s back that was “heavily infested with maggots consistent with NWS characteristics.” “We remain prepared and vigilant, and we urge all livestock owners, pet owners, and other animal industry partners to continue to monitor animals closely and report any unusual wounds consistent with NWS immediately,” the state said in an announcement. The USDA confirmed the infection and said it was working closely with Arizona on a joint response to the expanded infestation.

Oh captain, our captain: Ars staff remembers Patrick Stewart
2026-10-30

Oh captain, our captain: Ars staff remembers Patrick Stewart

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Along with the rest of the world, we at Ars are mourning Sir Patrick Stewart—the beloved award-winning actor best known as Captain Jean-Luc Picard in Star Trek: The Next Generation and Professor Charles Xavier in the X-Men franchise—who died Wednesday at the age of 86. A statement released by Stewart’s representatives said: We announce with great sorrow the passing of Sir Patrick Stewart yesterday at his home in Los Angeles, in the loving care of his beloved wife Sunny, following a brief and poignant journey with Alzheimer’s Disease and Lewy Body Dementia. He has been held in the warm embrace of family and friends, surrounded by live music, prayer, tears, laughter, and the kind of profound emotion that only he could inspire after a lifetime of devotion to us all. We will forever cherish the peaceful ease and reflection on his face, an enduring reminder of his invincible grace. Through the actor’s X account, the family has requested that, in lieu of flowers, memorial contributions be made to the Johns Hopkins Alzheimer’s Disease Research Center Fund and the Alzheimer’s Foundation of America Caregiver Resources. Born in Yorkshire, England, in 1940, the acting bug bit Stewart at the age of 12, when his English teacher handed out scripts for Shakespeare’s The Merchant of Venice and asked young Patrick to read the part of Shylock—a role the adult Stewart would later play for the Royal Shakespeare Theater (RSC), among many other parts. His signature bald head emerged early, around 19, thanks to alopecia. He briefly worked as a cub reporter and a furniture salesman before winning a scholarship to the Bristol Old Vic Theater School, and he landed his first professional role in 1959, playing a marooned pirate in a production of Treasure Island for the Lincoln Repertory Company. Stewart worked regularly in theater and on British television, and his big break came in his late 40s. The actor was conducting master classes on Shakespeare at the University of California, Los Angeles, when a TV producer developing TNG heard him lecture and thought he would make a perfect captain for the Enterprise-D. Creator Gene Roddenberry wasn’t initially keen on the choice, but Stewart’s audition changed his mind. The rest is television history. On Instagram, fellow thespian Sir Ian McKellen—who first met Stewart when both were young actors with the RSC and co-starred as Magneto in the X-Men films—recalled the day Stewart told him about the Star Trek offer. “’But Patrick, think of your stage career in the UK,'” McKellen recalled saying. “‘Do you really want to risk—getting lost in Hollywood for years and years?’ Well, he took the risk and thank goodness for us all that he did.” Stewart himself never expressed anything but pride in the role. “For huge numbers of people, I will always be Jean-Luc Picard, and that’s fine,” he told The Independent in 2003. “I really believe we created an admirable individual.” TNG ended in 1994, but it wasn’t long before Stewart created another iconic role, starring as Professor Charles Xavier in 2000’s X-Men, alongside McKellen as Magneto. He reprised the role in six more films and voiced the character in multiple video games. He was a serious actor who never took himself too seriously, relishing Shakespeare, Ibsen, and Beckett but also throwing himself into comedic TV appearances on Saturday Night Live, The Daily Show (he was a “special correspondent” for several segments in 2012), and Sesame Street. Who could forget his timeless “Soliloquy on B,” channeling Hamlet? Or his daily readings of Shakespearean sonnets that helped us all get through the COVID-19 pandemic? He voiced Napoleon in 1999’s Animal Farm, CIA Deputy Director Avery Bullock on American Dad and, earlier this year, Sir Nitchfield in the delightful Sheep Detectives. In between acting gigs, Stewart found time for charity work, advocating for such causes as domestic violence prevention and PTSD awareness among members of the armed forces, drawing on his own childhood being raised by an abusive father. All of this made Stewart one of the most beloved actors of our time. Tributes to Stewart are all over social media today, as celebrities and regular folk alike reflect on everything the actor’s work has meant to them over the years, including these heartfelt words from his lifelong friend McKellen: Before everything else, Patrick was defined by being born and bred in Yorkshire, the proud county in the north of England which he loved to the end. You could hear it in his accented voice and in the stern honesty with which he told you what he thought and felt. Yorkshiremen have no truck with half-truths or avoidance of big issues. He wore his heart on his sleeve, which made him a man to rely on, to admire and in my case, love. Ars staffers share their own reminiscences about Stewart and what he meant to them below. I had the good fortune to literally bump into Sir Patrick Stewart outside Bond 45 in Midtown Manhattan (when it was on 45th Street). I was coming out of the restaurant with a couple of colleagues one evening when I turned right into him. When my eyes met his, I entered a state of total confusion. I knew he loved and even played Broadway, but it didn’t register why he was here, right next to me. Fortunately, I recovered—but then, for some unknown reason, I said in a pirate voice, “Scuse muh, Capt’n!” But he didn’t miss a beat, smiled, and took a posture of “after you good sir!” I immediately chuckled like a fool and then added that I loved his work both on and off the stage. He thanked me, and we kept on rolling. I am thankful to this day that I didn’t say something corny like “two to beam up.” What struck me about the exchange was that, as brief as it was, he did not appear annoyed in the slightest. And while I know he is a world-class actor, he also seemed genuinely appreciative of the comment. I am grateful today that I got to share my appreciation with him, awkward though it was. Warmth. Humanity. Decency. Honesty. Integrity. Intellectualism. These are but a few of the traits I associate with Captain Jean-Luc Picard. At a time when our leadership in the United States, and many other countries around the world, regularly showcases the opposite of these characteristics, is it any wonder that we dearly miss the captain of the Enterprise-D? Picard first graced our TV screens in the late 1980s, a super impressionable time for me. As a result, for a time, that is how I expected most leaders would behave. Alas, no. I’m sure everyone has their favorite Picard scenes, but for me it came at the very end of The Next Generation; we had all gotten to know and love the bridge crew so well, and it felt so bittersweet that it all was ending. There was Jean-Luc, who had maintained his professional distance for seven years, joining his crew at the poker table. And now, not just the series is over, but so too is the era in which Patrick Stewart was alive and making the world a better place. It’s a little darker outside. With Patrick Stewart’s death, there will be plenty of people remembering all of the Star Trek: The Next Generation moments when he ably portrayed Captain Picard as the moral and diplomatic core of the show. And for sure, Stewart’s meaty and memorable monologues in episodes like The Drumhead, The First Duty, Darmok, Chain of Command, and more will be long remembered by sci-fi fans and studied by actors honing their craft. But the most lasting Captain Picard moments for me are often the ones where Stewart got to let his hair down (so to speak) and show some rare emotional vulnerability in what was usually such a composed and professional character. I’ll remember watching Picard wrestling with and crying to his brother after the trauma of being overtaken by the Borg, or chasing a dream-world grandson around on his hands and knees. I’ll remember the pain in Stewart’s eyes after a psychic implant revealed his true feelings for Dr. Crusher or when he was forced to struggle with the weight of sending a new romantic interest and subordinate on a dangerous mission. I’ll remember countless small moments like Picard downing a shot of Aldebaran whiskey with a time-shifted Scottie, being duly chagrined by a Captain Picard Day banner during a video call with an admiral, or pretending to be the barber while thwarting smugglers on an otherwise empty ship. It’s these smaller emotional character moments—and Stewart’s ability to make so much of them—that added depth and roundness to Captain Picard’s character over the years. And that continued all the way up to Next Generation‘s very final moments, where Stewart captures the wistfulness and regret of Picard finally deciding to join the senior staff poker game. “Five card stud, nothing wild… and the sky’s the limit.” As a very casual Star Trek viewer, I only really began to grasp the depth of Patrick Stewart’s acting when I became enthralled by the silver screen chemistry between him and co-star Ian McKellen in the X-Men film franchise. The love-hate relationship and emotional vulnerability that Stewart and McKellen instilled in their roles as old-friends-turned-rivals has resonated with me throughout the years, and I still have deep appreciation for how the actors’ real-life friendship grew from that acting experience. I also count myself extremely fortunate to have seen Stewart and McKellen perform together in a Broadway stage revival of the play Waiting for Godot in 2014. I’m sad they won’t be performing together again, but whenever they had the opportunity, they shone brightly indeed. Hope and altruism are central themes in Stewart’s iconic roles, and they resonated most with me during one of my lowest moments. Ever the one to inspire, Stewart’s role as Professor X in many X-Men films encouraged people and mutants to see the best in themselves and in each other. In 2014’s Days of Future Past, his pep talk extended to new ears: a younger, disillusioned version of himself (played by James McAvoy), and he may have well been speaking to me, too. Professor X’s telepathic abilities transcend space, time, and reality—don’t question it too much. Upon the film’s release, I was freshly laid off from my hometown newspaper after the copy desk saw its numbers cut in half. (What was already a skeleton crew became less than that.) It wasn’t lost on me that being a cynic and malcontent was partly why I wasn’t picked to remain at the paper. In my late 20s, I questioned many of the decisions that set me on my journalism path, unsure what would happen to my career. The younger Charles Xavier finds himself in a similar spot: angry with himself, with other people, and with the general state of things—and he’s seemingly out of a job. The older Professor X reminds his younger self that people can overcome their self-defeating flaws. “Just because someone stumbles and loses their way doesn’t mean they’re lost forever.” Younger Chuck laments being tormented by people’s accounts of their pain, something a person who reads the news for a living can relate to. I try to unplug from Cerebro—er, my computer—when I’m off the clock. Xavier doesn’t have that luxury. The constant voices have driven him to despair and left him increasingly jaded. But older Xavier calls him out, saying it’s his own mindset that’s holding him back. “We need you to hope again,” he says. Therein lies the answer to Xavier’s malady: hope. And, as it turns out, the advice was also applicable to a young journalist in a crumbling industry. Incidentally, Stewart also found himself at a career crossroads as a young man, though he wasn’t too far along a chosen path, according to the Hollywood Reporter. At 15, he got a job at the local newspaper, The Dewsbury Reporter, where he was tasked with interviewing bereaved family members when someone in town died. Journalism turned out not to be his passion, and he spent much of his free time with drama groups. His editor finally told him he had to choose between acting and journalism—and he quit on the spot. Maybe if I had an Englishman’s commanding baritone, I would have made the same choice. Ryan Whitwam, senior technology reporter: Patrick Stewart was a prolific actor, but I will always associate him most with Jean-Luc Picard on Star Trek: The Next Generation, a show that made a real impression on me as a nerdy kid. It was refreshing to see a leader who valued science and reason—someone who didn’t immediately reach for their phaser. Despite the sometimes silly sci-fi subject matter, Stewart never seemed like he was too good to be spouting technobabble, even though he was famously skeptical of the role at first. Stewart’s portrayal of Picard elevated the series and helped it to age into a classic that I (and I’m sure many of you) still enjoy. I also appreciate that he took so many different roles in movies and TV during his post-Trek career. Whenever I saw Patrick Stewart pop up on my screen or heard his voice unexpectedly come out of a cartoon character, it was a reminder of how happy TNG made me over the years. I’ll miss that, and I’ll miss knowing that Patrick Stewart is out there being a kind, thoughtful guy off screen. Lee Hutchinson, senior technology editor: The version of Patrick Stewart that lives in my head will most often be the noble starship captain who taught us life lessons every Friday night at 7 pm on channel 39. But while Stewart was obviously fantastic at portraying Picard’s stentorian and often stuffy formality, I was surprised when I discovered that the man behind the uniform was… surprisingly hilarious. I tuned into his early 1994 Saturday Night Live appearance expecting an endless series of Trek riffs, but what I got instead was a sketch about an erotic cake shop run by a weirdly scatological baker. My confusion gave way to giggles—it was a one-note joke, but the image of Stewart in an apron talking about “sexy cakes” has stuck in my head for decades. He committed to it every bit as hard as he committed to following the Prime Directive, and at 16, it made me realize that anyone who could go from “The first duty of a Starfleet officer is to the truth!” to that and sell them both with equal intensity and sincerity was not a one-note actor. This was a man with a hell of a lot of range! The performance that brought me the most joy, though, was his turn on Ricky Gervais’ Extras, where he played a version of himself as an actor, delivering career advice to Gervais. The scene is a masterclass in expectation subversion, something Stewart often seemed to revel in. I won’t spoil it for the two or three readers who still haven’t had the pleasure, but I will say that to this day my wife and I still say, “I’ve seen everything, I’ve seen it all!” at each other regularly in conversation. Farewell, Sir Patrick. You gave us so much. Thank you. Like many Gen Xers, I grew up loving the original Star Trek series. But The Next Generation held a special place in my heart. From the moment it premiered, I was captivated by the mind-bending concepts it explored. Jean-Luc Picard quickly became one of my favorite characters. I admired his intelligence, leadership, and moral integrity. He was the quintessential Starfleet captain, and he made balding cool in a way only Michael Jordan could rival. Years later, I discovered what would become my favorite Patrick Stewart performance: his formidable portrayal of Ebenezer Scrooge on Broadway and in the 1999 adaptation of A Christmas Carol. For me, the movie remains the definitive version of Dickens’ classic, and watching it has become a cherished family holiday tradition. During the COVID lockdowns, we eagerly tuned in to Stewart’s readings of Shakespeare, and of course, his solo multipart rendition of A Christmas Carol. A roaring fire, some warm drinks, and his perfect Shakespearean accent made everything seem right with the world. Sir Patrick Stewart will be greatly missed in the Marlin household. Growing up in the US, raised by Brits, there was something quietly thrilling about seeing a Yorkshireman handed the captain’s chair. Better still, it made TNG legitimate in the eyes of my parents—a classically trained Shakespearean actor wouldn’t be involved in anything unserious, surely. As I came of age in 2000, there he was again as Professor X. Of course he was. Who else could it have possibly been? Beyond the roles, though, he just seemed to be a genuinely good man, the Mr. Rogers and Weird Al tier of human being. And when the world was upside down at the height of COVID, there he was, reading me a daily sonnet.

Supersonic airliner in doubt as company shifts to powering AI data centers
2026-10-30

Supersonic airliner in doubt as company shifts to powering AI data centers

Ars Technica - All content | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Boom Supersonic CEO Blake Scholl recently declared “it’s time for a renaissance in energy and in supersonic flight” as the supersonic airliner company unveiled a Colorado factory dedicated to making gas turbines for AI data centers. But Boom may be facing turbulence after early backers for both ventures have questioned the company’s delivery timeline or backed out entirely. One of the company’s first customers, United Airlines CEO Scott Kirby, has expressed doubts about Boom’s original plan to deliver the first Overture supersonic airliner in 2029. Kirby’s latest frank assessment came during an interview with the Office Hours: Business Edition podcast that appeared on October 28, 2026. “‘29 is their official timeline,” Kirby told podcast host Monica Langley. “It’s not going to happen in ‘29—it’s going to take longer.” Boom first made headlines for flying its XB-1 demonstrator aircraft in 2024 and 2025 to demonstrate a “boomless cruise” concept that harnesses the Mach cutoff phenomenon. Such a feat involves flying just beyond supersonic speeds at higher altitudes under specific atmospheric conditions, so that the aircraft’s shockwaves refract upward and minimize the impact on the ground. The company’s plan to revive supersonic air travel has been backed by the Trump administration via efforts to ease regulations. In June 2026, the Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) proposed a new rule overturning a long-standing ban on commercial supersonic flights over the United States, following an executive order from President Donald Trump. Boom’s press releases have previously described achieving aircraft certification and carrying the first paying customers by 2029. The company has conditional agreements with American Airlines, Japan Airlines, and United Airlines involving non-refundable deposits of unknown amounts that would allow the airlines to fully purchase the Overture aircraft once it becomes commercially available. But United CEO Kirby pointed out that Boom still has “a lot of hurdles to overcome” while developing Overture and getting the supersonic airliner certified by the FAA. “So it’s not a guarantee that they’ll overcome,” he cautioned during the podcast interview, even though he praised Scholl and the company in general. This follows Kirby’s earlier comments back in March that put the chances of Boom’s Overture airliner entering commercial service at “50-50.” Ars has reached out to Boom for comment. Although Boom completed building the factory intended for production of the Overture airliner in 2024, the facility located at Piedmont Triad International Airport in Greensboro, North Carolina, has mostly sat dormant since that time. That has raised questions for North Carolina state leaders who provided more than $50 million in taxpayer funding to help build the Overture factory. Since October 2025, NASA has separately been doing test flights involving the experimental X-59 with a long, needle-like nose designed to reduce the sonic booms from supersonic flight. But this is a completely different approach than Boom’s use of the Mach cutoff flights. Much of Boom’s recent focus has been on developing natural gas turbines to power AI data centers as an additional revenue stream. On October 22, the company unveiled another factory in Centennial, Colorado, dedicated to producing a 42-megawatt natural gas turbine called Superpower for data centers. The Trump administration’s public support of Boom was once again evident as US Secretary of Energy Chris Wright participated in the event alongside Colorado Governor Jared Polis. Boom described its new Superpower factory as making turbine parts while still undergoing construction. It aims to deliver the first gas turbines to AI data center customers in mid-2027 while ramping up annual production to 25 turbines in 2028 and 50 turbines by 2029. Boom’s detour into powering data centers coincides with many tech companies prioritizing natural gas as the primary power source to get their projects up and running. Some data center developers are even using “mobile gas generators strapped to semitrucks” or “aeroderivative turbines originally designed for aircraft and warships,” according to the market intelligence platform Cleanview. However, Boom CEO Scholl also acknowledged the recent loss of a $1.25 billion deal that would have seen data center developer Crusoe purchase 29 of Boom’s gas turbines, in a post on the social media platform X on September 25. Crusoe has been involved in building Texas data centers for Oracle, Google and Microsoft that draw upon various power sources including natural gas and renewables. Scholl said Boom “will be delivering about 250MW of Superpowers next year to other sites” but did not specify the AI data center customers that may be using the gas turbines. Still, Boom has characterized the development of a gas turbine for data centers as being synergistic with its design of the Symphony engine for the Overture airliner. During the October announcement, it also described having completed a prototype turbine core, called Sprint Core, that is intended to eventually power both the Superpower gas turbine and Symphony engine for the airliner. That turbine core is set to undergo testing at the Colorado Air & Space Port, with Boom planning to fire the turbine for the first time before Thanksgiving. That would be a concrete development milestone, but the company still has much to do in proving it can get both its AI data center and supersonic airliner ventures off the ground.

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh is actually managing U.S. debt costs not inflation, economist says, as interest rates threaten to top GDP growth soon
2026-11-01

Fed Chair Kevin Warsh is actually managing U.S. debt costs not inflation, economist says, as interest rates threaten to top GDP growth soon

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Trying to pin down Kevin Warsh as a hawk or a dove has been tricky with his statements and actions puzzling Wall Street. As President Donald Trump’s hand-picked Federal Reserve chairman, Warsh was expected to lean toward looser monetary policy. Then he declared that inflation was a “choice,” suggesting a tougher approach. Over the summer, he suggested looking at alternative inflation gauges and that the bond market would do some of the Fed’s tightening work, hinting as less aggressive policy. But he delivered a hawkish speech at the Jackson Hole conference in August, and led the Fed to a unanimous rate hike in September. Last week, he held off on backing another rate hike, despite inflation heating up again, with his lack of explanation and refusal to offer any hints about the next policy decision producing an overall dovish effect. But Robin Brooks, a senior fellow at the Brookings Institution, said asking whether Warsh is a hawk or a dove is the wrong question. Instead, he views all the mixed messages from the Fed chair through a different lens, arguing the hot inflation readings can’t rationalize Warsh’s statements. “We’re not in hawkish or dovish land. What’s happening is yield curve management, which itself is a function of how precarious the U.S. debt picture is,” Brooks wrote in a Substack post on Thursday. That’s because the surge in Treasury yields this year threatens to worsen the already-grim outlook for U.S. debt, which is now above $40 trillion in gross terms with publicly held debt at 100% of GDP. In fiscal year 2026, which ended in September, the federal government’s debt-interest costs topped $1 trillion, exceeding defense spending. “The bottom line is that inflation and other macro data are secondary,” Brooks added. “The twists and turns in Fed policy are about what’s going on with long-term yields.” He suspects the Fed and Treasury Department are in a de facto accord to keep borrowing costs stable. Brooks pointed to Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent’s surprise announcement to buy back more long-term bonds, briefly lowering yields. Bessent also intervened in the currency market to prop up the yen without Japan having to sell its holdings of U.S. debt, which would have lifted yields. The actual effectiveness of these attempts at managing bond yields is a different story. Expectations for the Iran war to re-escalate have sent oil prices and yields higher. Signs that the U.S. economy is running hot have also raised inflation and rate forecasts. But the U.S. also caught some breaks too. Fears that the AI boom, a major driver of GDP growth, will go bust have taken pressure off yields. France’s political turmoil and fiscal dysfunction have sparked worries that it will trigger a debt crisis that spreads to other parts of the eurozone. Investors have since embraced Treasury debt again as a safe have asset, and the 10-year yield has dipped back below 5% again. “Warsh is neither hawk nor dove,” Brooks declared. “Instead—like Bessent—he’s just trying to keep long-term yields manageable.” Such management risks putting the U.S. on the path toward so-called fiscal dominance, where high debt and deficits override the Fed’s ability to independently manage monetary policy and fight inflation. Economists have pointed out that as long as nominal GDP growth remains above interest rates, then the U.S. can avoid a debt spiral where borrowing costs pile up faster than the economy expands. But bond yields have risen so high and so quickly this year that they have blown past long-term estimates, raising alarms about the debt trajectory. In fact, the Congressional Budget Office was asked to assess scenarios where yields are 150 basis points and 50 basis points higher than the watchdog’s baseline of 4.3%. Under the 150-point scenario, net interest costs go up by $4.9 trillion over the next 10 years, and publicly held debt reaches 133% of GDP by 2036. While yields have pulled back recently, they jumped more than 100 basis points over the CBO’s baseline earlier. Analysts at Bank of America looked at the CBO’s numbers and estimated that in the alternate scenario, the effective interest rate on government debt will exceed long-term nominal GDP growth (assumed to be a 4% pace) as early as fiscal year 2028—which begins October 2027. For now, nominal growth remains robust and was 5.6% in the third quarter, helped by the AI boom and high inflation. “However, both forces are likely to prove more temporary than permanent unless AI adoption ultimately delivers substantial productivity gains,” BofA said in a note on Oct. 19. “Should that occur, concerns around fiscal sustainability would ease materially. Even so, we believe it is optimistic to assume the United States can simply grow its way out of its mounting debt burden.” Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Fortune | FORTUNE
2026 was meant to be the Philippines’ year—yet the country remains stuck in an ‘recurring cycle of underachievement’
2026-11-01

2026 was meant to be the Philippines’ year—yet the country remains stuck in an ‘recurring cycle of underachievement’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
When the Philippines took over from Malaysia as chair of the Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN), expectations ran high that the country could use its one-year term to attract investment and catch up with the more successful neighbors driving the region’s growth story. With two months left in 2026, those hopes have been dashed. The country has been Southeast Asia’s worst-hit by high energy prices since the U.S.-Iran war began in February. “For decades, the Philippines has been described as a country of enormous promise. It has a young population, a large domestic market, a strategic location, and a highly capable workforce,” Ruben Carlo Asuncion, chief economist of the Union Bank of the Philippines, says. “Yet it has often struggled to convert these advantages into sustained gains in investment, productivity, and competitiveness.” He notes that the start of 2026 brought expectations for strong growth supported by easing inflation, lower interest rates, resilient household spending, and infrastructure investment. “Instead, the economy faced a series of shocks that slowed momentum and reignited inflation pressures,” he says. What was meant to be a breakout year, highlighted by the Philippines’ turn as ASEAN chair, has instead become a case study in how the country keeps falling short: its structural advantages are outweighed by corruption, institutional dysfunction, and energy insecurity. Some of the Philippines’ woes—particularly energy prices—are out of the country’s control, and may ease in 2027. In fact, the World Bank expects a rebound to 5.2% in 2027, on higher public spending and easing inflation. Even so, Asuncion sees larger challenges ahead. “The more important question is whether the country can finally break free from its recurring cycle of underachievement.” Peter L. U, dean of economics at the University of Asia and the Pacific in Manila, says the university’s early forecasts put GDP growth above 5% this year, similar to the World Bank’s estimates. However, high oil prices and cuts in public spending—enacted in the wake of a billion-dollar flood control infrastructure scandal last year—have since dragged this forecast down to a meager 3.7%. The one bright spot is the World Bank’s reclassification of the Philippines as an upper-middle-income economy this year. Yet Asuncion calls it a “milestone, not a destination.” “Many countries reach middle-income status. Far fewer successfully make the transition to becoming high-income economies…the challenge for the Philippines is no longer how to achieve growth, but how to sustain and improve the quality of that growth,” Asuncion says. The Philippines’ sluggishness is a contrast to Vietnam, the other Southeast Asian country to get upper-middle-income status this year. Vietnam is expected to grow 7.8% in 2026, according to forecasts from the Asian Development Bank. Exports are also booming, reaching about $375 billion in the first eight months of the year. The Philippines, by comparison, just exported $64 billion. U puts the milestone in more domestic terms. “It’s like a child who turned 13, yet is still the same person with the same maturity they had yesterday. That child will need to continue working hard to continue learning more, acquiring more skills and virtues to become an adult,” he says. One of the biggest obstacles to climbing the income ladder is that investors still struggle to find projects worth backing. Steep electricity costs—some of the highest in Southeast Asia—as well as bureaucracy and corruption, also raise the cost of doing business. The biggest drag on the Philippine economy, however, may be entirely out of its control. Continued tensions in the Middle East will keep energy prices elevated for the foreseeable future, driving inflation in the Philippines. “Oil prices had already begun to come down but spiked again on renewed clashes around a month ago,” U says. Inflation is back at its April peak of 7.2%. The Philippines is still absorbing sharply higher prices of fuel and fertilizer, along with a peso trading at record lows against the dollar. Those pressures feed into prices across the board, says Michael Ricafort, chief economist at the Rizal Commercial Banking Corporation. Ricafort cautions that inflation could pick up further through second-round and pass-through effects, including higher transport costs, businesses pushing for higher prices, rising inflation expectations, and wages. The increase in Metro Manila’s daily minimum wage from 695 to 755 Philippine pesos ($10.91 to $11.85) in September could also lead to pay hikes in other parts of the country, he adds. Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas, the country’s central bank, has also been hiking interest rates to rein in inflation—moves that will dampen the consumption spending that has traditionally driven the economy. On Oct. 22, the BSP raised its benchmark rate by 50 basis points to 5.5%. And more hikes could be coming: Nomura now expects the Philippine central bank to raise rates by another 75 basis points to 6.25% by February. Yet Asuncion argues that these external events exposed the Philippines’ existing weaknesses. “Economies inevitably face external shocks. What differentiates successful economies from struggling ones is the strength of their institutions and their ability to respond effectively,” he says. “The energy shock highlighted the country’s dependence on imported fuel, while the flood control controversy reinforced concerns about governance, accountability, and the efficient use of public resources.” U makes a similar point: the Gulf conflict and last year’s flood-control scandal mirror challenges long familiar to the Philippines. “The Middle East conflict and the resultant oil price spikes have been recurrent going back to the 1970s. Similarly, corruption scandals in the Philippines seem to also be recurrent,” U says. With just a few months left in the year, attention is back on the Philippines to see whether it can turn things around, starting with the ASEAN Summit in November and into 2027. “The ASEAN Chairship has opened important economic opportunities, although many of the benefits will take time to materialise and build on initiatives already underway,” Victor Andres Manhit, president of the Manila-based consultancy group Stratbase, says. He adds that key developments include the signing of the ASEAN Digital Economy Framework Agreement expected during the summit. While the ASEAN Chairship may have provided a platform and opened doors to raise the Philippines’ profile on the investment stage and position it as a key player in the region, “whether the Philippines walks through them depends largely on governance and execution at home,” Asuncion says. The Philippines does have some structural advantages, despite the grim short-term outlook. “The country’s geostrategic location, abundant natural resources, large and young population, and investment opportunities remain important strengths,” Manhit says. Ricafort also sees opportunities in what has been touted as one of the Philippines’ greatest strengths: a young population of more than 115 million, with an average age below 25. “The Philippines has been in a demographic sweet spot since 2015, with the majority of the population already of working age,” he says. Yet this window is also narrowing fast. U warns that declining birth rates and slipping educational quality, as well as the rise of AI, could affect sectors that rely on young workers, like the business process outsourcing industry. And if there’s one insight from the past year, it’s that institutions are still the the foundation of a well-functioning economy. “The lesson from 2026 is straightforward: there is no shortcut around good governance,” Asuncion says. “That is ultimately the difference between an economy that is perpetually promising and one that finally fulfills its promise.” The Fortune Southeast Asia 500 Summit will convene executives from the Fortune Southeast Asia 500, policymakers, and investors to examine the forces reshaping one of the world's most important growth regions—and how business and government can position it for resilient, inclusive growth. As Trump eyes new attack, Iran vows to retaliate beyond the Mideast and target financial assets, while oil markets brace for generational damage
2026-11-01

As Trump eyes new attack, Iran vows to retaliate beyond the Mideast and target financial assets, while oil markets brace for generational damage

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The clock is ticking toward a new round of fighting in the U.S.-Iran war, but this time the damage could be much more widespread, stretching to new regions and different types of targets. President Donald Trump has said he’s contemplating “another little Epic Fury” after the U.S. midterm elections on Tuesday. The U.S. has already sent a third aircraft carrier and Marine expeditionary unit to the Middle East, and told allies in the region to prepare for a possible short but intense renewal of fighting, according to the Wall Street Journal. The Iranian regime similarly stepped up its threats on how it would respond, seeking to deter an attack by threatening disproportionate and no-holds-barred retaliation. On Saturday, Ayatollah Mojtaba Khamenei warned Iran’s response would be “without any geographical limitations,” adding that the “data bank of targets should be updated so that the armed forces could hit all kinds of financial and non-financial assets.” In recent weeks, Iran has warned undersea internet cables and data centers would be targets. The foiled attack on a British air base used by U.S. forces has also been attributed to Tehran. And while North Koreans have been blamed for the $6.3 billion crypto heist at Binance, it shows how vulnerable such assets are to Iran, which is also adept at cyberattacks. At the same time, the Pentagon is investigating whether hackers hit freezers on U.S. military bases. The RAND think tank pointed out last week that hostile Iranian activity “spans the globe,” with proxy Hezbollah in Latin America and reported plots against civilians in Europe. “Unbound and bent on revenge, Iran is unlikely to be contained to just one corner of the globe,” analysts wrote, arguing that the least bad option would be for the U.S. to continue its naval blockade and not launch new attacks. Diplomacy is ongoing with the U.S. and Iran exchanging messages via intermediaries, but neither side has budged from their initial demands, dampening hopes for a breakthrough in the next few days. Given the hardened rhetoric from the regime with threats of unrestricted retaliation, experts now detect a change in Iran’s tactics against the U.S. and see greater odds of the war returning. For Hamidreza Azizi, senior Iran analyst at the Crisis Group, the most important development was the message from Khamenei. “This language suggests a further evolution in Iran’s military strategy,” he posted on X on Saturday. “Since the beginning of the war, Tehran has increasingly moved away from its traditional emphasis on proportionate response and retaliation toward a more offensive approach aimed at imposing costs on adversaries. The latest statements appear to take this logic a step further.” Azizi also cited remarks from another top official who indicated Iran could stage a preemptive strike if preparations for hostile action were confirmed. In addition, Khamenei insisted Iran would determine when and how any renewed conflict ends. Iran is also wary of further diplomatic efforts, especially since previous attacks came even in the middle of talks with the U.S. early this year and last summer. “Iran appears increasingly determined to prevent a repetition of previous scenarios in which negotiations or temporary ceasefires were followed by renewed military conflict,” Azizi added. “From Tehran’s perspective, the objective is to make another attack so costly and unpredictable that Washington would have to reconsider the option altogether.” Meanwhile, Iran still has thousands of missiles and drones in its arsenal that can inflict catastrophic damage to its neighbors in the Persian Gulf area. That’s as stockpiles of U.S. interceptors have already been depleted to critically low levels, while newer Iranian missiles has demonstrated the ability to evade air-defense systems. Considering how much damage the region’s energy infrastructure sustained earlier in the war, it can’t absorb another round of fighting, according to Amir Handjani is a board member at the Quincy Institute for Responsible Statecraft. Indeed, even U.S. military bases across the Middle East have been heavily damaged by Iranian drones and missiles. “Gulf producers spent five decades persuading the world that their supply could withstand regional wars. Eight months of fighting on two fronts have badly weakened that case. A negotiated end to the war would give the region time to rebuild it,” Handjani wrote. “Another round, on top of the Saudi-Houthi war, would leave damage to infrastructure and to importers’ confidence that markets are likely to carry for a generation.” Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Soaring health insurance costs are quietly preventing wages from rising higher, while workers also shoulder a larger share of premiums, economist says
2026-11-01

Soaring health insurance costs are quietly preventing wages from rising higher, while workers also shoulder a larger share of premiums, economist says

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Open enrollment season for health care plans is about to begin, and many Americans may get sticker shock after seeing how much premiums will rise. But the rising cost of insurance is also quietly eroding wage gains, preventing workers from getting bigger raises than they would otherwise receive. Health insurance costs are a major issue in the midterm election. In addition to higher gas prices and utility bills, insurance has stoked voter anxiety about the rising cost of living. So despite consistently solid wage gains and a labor market that’s at full employment, consumer confidence gauges have tumbled as overall affordability deteriorates. Fresh economic data illustrate why workers aren’t feeling upbeat about more pay. In the third quarter, wages and salaries increased 3.1% from a year ago, according to the employment cost index released by the Bureau of Labor Statistics on Friday. But at the same time, employer health insurance contributions jumped 6.4%, matching the fastest pace in two decades, according to economist Guy Berger, senior fellow at the Burning Glass Institute. In a Substack post, he calculated that if employer contributions had instead grown at 3%, which was the pace a few years ago, wage growth would be about 0.2-0.3 percentage points faster today. “That’s not a lot, but not nothing either,” Berger wrote. With many Americans resorting to buy now, pay later plans to buy essentials, even those incremental gains could make a big difference. Berger added that the data likely understates the squeeze workers a facing, as employer contributions might not be keeping pace with health insurance costs. “In other words, employees are paying a bigger share of premiums,” he explained. On top of that, the insurance plans themselves are covering less, forcing workers to pay more expenses out of pocket, Berger said. Americans who get health insurance through their employers aren’t the only ones feeling the pain. The cost of coverage via Affordable Care Act plans has also be surging. In 2027, premiums will rise by 13%, according to health-research nonprofit KFF, which drew on federal data released Friday. That’s the second biggest hike since 2018 and follows a 26% jump in 2026. The latest increase translates to a $704 average monthly premium for a 40-year-old purchasing a mid-tier Obamacare plan. Such “benchmark” premiums were $624 in 2026 and $497 in 2025. While spending on drugs and hospital care is going up, the ACA market has come under particular strain since 2025, because Congress didn’t renew the enhanced subsidies that were enacted during the pandemic. As a result, ACA enrollment has dropped by millions as people balked higher premium expenses. Since most who opted out were healthier, those remaining tend to be sicker and drive up premiums. As of February, enrollment in ACA plans had plunged by 12% from a year earlier, or around 19.2 million people, and the industry has indicated more have dropped out since then. Meanwhile, insurers are choosing not to participate in ACA marketplace, narrowing the choices for consumers. According to KFF, there were 31 state-level departures by insurers as of late October versus eight additions. “Looking ahead to 2027, several insurers have said they expect the market to continue to deteriorate in 2027 as a continued result of the enhanced premium tax credits ending,” KFF said on Friday. “Some enrollees who were able to keep coverage in 2026 may no longer be able to make ends meet.” Jason Ma is the weekend editor at Fortune, where he covers markets, the economy, finance, and housing. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. All Rights Reserved.
