A one-person quorum: no discussion, no dissent, no disclosure at the SEC
2026-10-26

A one-person quorum: no discussion, no dissent, no disclosure at the SEC

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-10-26

Visit Original Website
On September 30, the Securities and Exchange Commission quietly rewrote the rule that decides the number of commissioners who must be present to act on agency business. Under the amended Rule 200.41, whenever the other sitting commissioner is disqualified from a matter, the one remaining commissioner will now constitute a quorum. The agency that was created to protect investors and the public market made this change without public comment, calling it internal “housekeeping” that does not “substantially affect the rights or obligations” of anyone. Congress designed the SEC as a five-member body, with no more than three commissioners from the president’s party, precisely so that no single political party, and certainly no single person, could steer America’s capital markets alone. Today the Commission has no Democratic members and with Commissioner Hester Peirce’s departure on October 2, it is down to two members. A recusal is all it will take for a single individual to wield the full authority of the Commission. One person is not a regulator; it is a dictator. Nearly every action or proposed action by the SEC this term either reduces investor information or silences their voice. The SEC has moved to rescind the Climate Disclosure rule, Say on Pay, and 14a-8’s shareholder proposal rule. It plans to slash financial reporting from quarterly to twice a year and plans a sweeping rewrite of Regulation S-K, the backbone of what material information public companies must provide investors. The SEC has even cleared the way for companies to force shareholder complaints into private arbitration, where there is no jury and no public record. The direction is unmistakable: companies will keep the privilege of raising money from public markets while shedding their traditional obligations. Publicly traded companies now resemble private equity: access without accountability, capital without candor. Let’s call it what it is: corporate authoritarianism. The SEC has granted itself power without oversight and has, rule by rule, gifted the same to the CEOs it was meant to regulate. Top beneficiaries include the same corporate giants that wrote seven-figure checks to the president’s campaign and inauguration. A CEO freed from disclosure and an agency Commission freed from dissent are two sides of the same coin. Each unilateral action is defended as reducing corporate burden, but a stock price is only as accurate as the public information behind it. Markets depend on the free flow of information as surely as free societies depend on freedom of the press and the right to peaceful protest. Authoritarian systems like the one the SEC is building depend on the opposite: power that answers to no one. For the pension funds, endowments, and asset managers that steward the retirement savings of more than a hundred million Americans, reduced information flow is a serious problem. Fiduciaries are bound to act prudently on behalf of their beneficiaries. Strip away the information they need to make informed decisions, and prudence becomes virtually impossible. Undisclosed risk tends to surface all at once. Meta’s shareholders recently learned that lesson the hard way. Mark Zuckerberg spent years overriding the warnings and requests of his own investors to reduce or eliminate the harmful and addictive content leading to immense public harm. Now, the same shareholders who were proven right, are on the hook for a $17 billion settlement. America’s capital markets have been the envy of the world for one reason: TRUST. Our founders feared concentrated, unchecked power, and Congress built the SEC to guard against it after the 1929 collapse. Investors from London to Singapore accept American valuations because they believe our disclosures are complete and because our rules are stable. If we continue to erode that trust by capriciously changing the rules, capital will do what it always does: move to where it is respected. A Commission of one is not a Commission at all. A CEO who answers to no one is not running a public company. Investors and lawmakers must demand that the empty Commission seats be filled, that the definition of a quorum of one be reversed, and that information previously provided to the market be restored before capital is driven from American capitalism. The opinions expressed in Fortune.com commentary pieces are solely the views of their authors and do not necessarily reflect the opinions and beliefs of Fortune. Andrew Behar is the Board Chair and President of As You Know and the CEO of As You Sow, the nation’s leading non-profit practitioner of shareholder advocacy and engagement. He is on the board of the Responsible Sourcing Network and a member of the XPrize Brain-Trust for Abundant Energy. His book, The Shareholders Action Guide: Unleash Your Hidden Powers to Hold Corporations Accountable, was published in November 2016 by Berrett-Koehler. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. ‘The best thing that you can do for your career is to make yourself redundant’: Inside Stellantis’ AI push after the ‘starting gun went off’
2026-11-01

‘The best thing that you can do for your career is to make yourself redundant’: Inside Stellantis’ AI push after the ‘starting gun went off’

Fortune | FORTUNE | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The Grand Cherokee is lined with wood paneling, its windshield is cracked, and chunks are cut out of its side-view mirrors like pierced ears. That’s where the cameras go, a grinning Joydip Saha tells me from behind the wheel. The manager of Stellantis’ hands-free driving project lovingly taps what he calls “the most broken-up car,” jerry-rigged with a yellow box sitting on the dashboard that lets a human grab control. It’s an industry-standard “kill switch” used in many prototype vehicles. In technical terms, the safety driver can hit the kill switch to disconnect power to the advanced driver assist system module, stopping the ADAS system from controlling the vehicle. Then, the driver has full control over the vehicle. Another piece of equipment connected to the kill switch — the STLA AutoDrive L2++ system embedded in the vehicle — beeps when it needs him to take control, a bit like a dog wearing a smart collar. And it’s confused. We are in the bowels of the Chrysler Technology Center in suburban Auburn Hills, Mich., navigating through the rabbit warren of a parking structure on the campus built by Lee Iacocca, the former Fortune posterboy who broke ground on the 500-acre site in 1986 and dedicated it in 1991, at a cost the company puts at about $1 billion. With the headquarters tower added in 1996, it spans 5.4 million square feet, the second-largest building in the U.S. by floor space, behind only the Pentagon. Stellantis still runs its North American engineering out of Iacocca’s building, and the people who work there still say “all under one roof.” Inside, it’s a pure shot of early ‘90s design. Skinny escalators climb skyward around an octagonal skylight, like a Clinton-era MC Escher drawing. The diamond-shaped parking garage attached to it is unique and confounding. Saha’s development mule heads toward the wrong exit and Saha has to put his hands on the wheel Even he finds the garage complicated. Auburn Hills’ suburban roads are much better for a test drive and I watch Saha’s mule smoothly navigate an intersection where two Michiganders decide to both make illegal multi-lane changes within seconds of each other. Saha, the global engineering manager who serves as the owner of Stellantis’ L2++/hands-free door-to-door supervised automated driving system, has been at this longer than most. He worked on the Chrysler Pacifica that Stellantis supplied for Waymo’s first robotaxi in Arizona, then managed a group that integrated Level 4 autonomy with partners including Aurora and Lyft. He still test-drives every new software build himself, as often as twice a week, because he considers any problem a customer faces problems  to be his responsibility. He said his team built the Cherokee’s hands-free system in six weeks with the help of AI tools, when a similar project used to take a year or even 18 months. He has a rule for AI, though: “I will not let people use AI to architect stuff because they will forget how to debug stuff,” he said. In a safety-critical business, he adds, engineers have to be able to answer every question. Across two days of interviews at the technology center, engineers told Fortune that AI has turned yearlong projects into weeks and overnight simulations into minutes. Stellantis wants that speed to cut development time on its new computing and cockpit software, STLA Brain and STLA SmartCockpit as part of a general push to speed vehicle development from four years to 24 months, starting with two models in 2027. But nearly everyone who described a speed-up also described checking the machine’s work. Stellantis also has problems bigger than time savings. The company has 14 brands, a European business that is losing money, and a second-quarter adjusted operating margin of 1.8%, while CEO Antonio Filosa has set a margin target of 7% for 2030. Wall Street isn’t waiting. Stellantis is the member of Detroit’s Big Three most exposed to Europe, which accounted for about 38% of its 2025 revenue. (GM sold its European brands, Opel and Vauxhall, in 2017 to PSA, one of the companies that later merged to form Stellantis.) Europe also leans much harder toward electric vehicles than truck-happy America: : battery-electric cars made up 17.4% of new EU registrations last year, according to ACEA, compared with 7.8% of U.S. sales, according to Cox Automotive. Stellantis had also bet heavily on EVs in North America, and it took the biggest hit in Detroit after the $7,500 federal EV tax credit ended on Sept. 30, 2025. In February, Stellantis wrote down roughly €22.2 billion (about $26.5 billion), more than Ford’s $19.5 billion charge in December or the $7.6 billion GM took across two charges. Stellantis shares fell 25% that day, and the reverberations are still being felt, with the stock down nearly 60% year-to-date. Mel Roberts, the director of North American infotainment, who plans the features inside Stellantis vehicles five to eight years out, said AI was not her biggest surprise of the last several years. “What I couldn’t anticipate is just the complete collapse of the electrification ambitions and how that really did affect all of our future plans,” she said. “It trickled into everything.” Stellantis is the only one of Detroit’s Big Three to be legally headquartered abroad, in the Netherlands. It’s the result of a pandemic-era merger in 2021 between Fiat Chrysler and the PSA Group that brought iconic European brands such as Peugeot and Citroën together with Fiat, Jeep and Ram. That gives it a distinctly international air, as Fortune met executives born in France, Mexico and Bangladesh within minutes of each other, along with a Michigander back from a few years stationed in Paris. The mergers also left behind a machine built by committee. Ben Zawodni, manager of software engineering, is working to fold the hundreds of electronic control units, each a small computer with its own job, into a central “brain” called, naturally, STLA Brain. Over the decades, he says, the car’s architecture had evolved over time to really mirror the supply chain. “We were bolting on features.” Amanda French, who leads the company’s platform teams, is tackling the same problem one layer down. Stellantis historically built separate platforms, each to a fixed size. Its new platform, STLA One, is built to stretch from small B-segment cars to midsize D-segment vehicles. The company says it is designed to grow into a mega platform supporting 30+ models and targeting more than 2 million units by 2035. Asked whether building it felt like playing with Legos, French responded, “Yeah, with a lot more people and emotions involved.” Upstairs at the technical center, Chief Engineering & Technology Officer Ned Curic is a self-described “intense” but talkative and warm presence. He came to Stellantis by way of Amazon, with previous stints at Microsoft and Toyota. He explained that Stellantis is not where it needs to be. Some teams show “phenomenal successes,” he said. In building that culture, he said, “as a whole organization, we are not there.” Curic sounds exasperated with an industry that has piled on complexity. Cars now come with “five screens, like digital noise, like 350 features,” he said, and automakers have historically tended to “checklist the heck out of the features” without measuring how people actually use them. He said he learned from Toyota that “the highest quality part that you have in a car is the part that you don’t have in a car.” In Stellantis’ research on drivers, for instance, he said most people preferred a mechanical stick to a button on a screen. At Amazon, Curic received Jeff Bezos’ notorious emails forwarding on customer complaints. The body of each email was a single question mark. “I never found it stressful,” Curic said. “Never.” One executive would handle the problem, he explained, and the others would each write a couple of paragraphs to help. From Amazon and Toyota, Curic also brought small teams from the “two-pizza rule” and single owners for each system, so engineers who once sat in separate groups can’t blame each other when something breaks. “If you see something that’s wrong, you just pick it up,” he said. “I own it.” The hardest idea he’s selling is that “the best thing that you can do for your career is to make yourself redundant.” He has done it himself several times by getting a team in good shape, leaving, and starting something new. For that to work, Curic said, the company has to make sure there is always a new opportunity for those people, and that leaders need maturity and confidence. Asked whether Detroit’s long run of hard times makes people defensive about change, he replied that while there is always a bit of a fear, change is a lot more accepted, ”and actually sought after.” The man telling engineers to make themselves redundant is also hiring them. At the Paris auto show in October, Curic told Bloomberg that Stellantis would add “multiple hundreds” of engineers in Europe and the U.S. this year. The pressure lands on engineers like Zawodni and French. Both of them are tackling Stellantis’ tricky margins issue by simplifying the wiring for each vehicle to bring the manufacturing into alignment, cutting costs and boosting Stellantis’ profits back to where they need to be. “The AIs aren’t going to tell us what the new platforms are,” Zawodni said. Zawodni said he started working on consolidating the cars’ central computing during COVID, when engineers went fully remote and Stellantis set up labs in India, Poland and North America that could be logged into from anywhere. A proof of concept for a global observability platform, which monitors software deep inside the car’s computers, took a week. Before agentic coding tools, he remembered something like that would take six months. And defects that took two to three weeks to fix shrank to two to three days. “We knew that we had to really reimagine all of our time,” Zawodni said. Did that scare him? “How exciting, right?” French, a cheerful engineer from southern Indiana, lit up when the conversation turned to AI, though hers was a more measured take than Zawodni’s. She said human judgment “cannot be replaced,” but AI helps her pull together requirements and bills of materials stored in regional systems that were never built to talk to each other, because Stellantis is “made up from a number of different companies.” She compared AI to an interactive form of Cliffs Notes she wished for in school. Zawodni said his team was not all as excited about the AI breakthroughs as he was. “I think there was a starting gun, and that starting gun went off,” he said. “Some people heard it, and some people didn’t.” Of his own engineers, he calls it a mixed bag. “Engineers are really afraid of losing their core skills,” he said. They want to know why a semicolon in a coding language does what it does. He called that “a valid curiosity,” but argued that it doesn’t get Stellantis to its goals. He wants them to become architects, and eventually chief architects. The tools haven’t lightened Zawodni’s own load. “Agentic tools, they’ve made our work so much easier,” he said. “So why am I working so much harder?” He estimates he works at least twice as hard as before. “I feel a tremendous sense of urgency.” He said, “I really hope that we can transition responsibly … especially our people.” Anne Laliron, a French engineer who runs Stellantis’ roughly 400-person tech research group, gave up her office after COVID so her team can always find her, and she lights up talking about their energy. Her job is deciding which big bets to make. Stellantis can’t afford to do everything, so it designs battery cells in-house and partners with startups for the rest. “If you want to embrace everything,” she said, “you are at risk of spreading your effort.” For Stellantis, partnering is a way to punch above its weight. The company brings in Wayve, a British startup, for the perception software in its hands-free driving, works with the French startup Tiamat on sodium-ion batteries, with Zeta Energy on lithium-sulfur batteries, and owns a stake in the Chinese EV maker Leapmotor, selling its cars outside of China through the joint venture Leapmotor International. Laliron said the habit runs deep at Stellantis and is less common at other automakers. Curic wants a culture where “it doesn’t have to be invented here,” and he said the learning with Leapmotor runs both ways: “We learned a lot… we also taught them a lot.” Stellantis runs batteries the same way, Laliron said: design in-house, partner with startups, and leave mass production to its gigafactory partners. Their biggest bet sits on a stage nearby: a battery-electric Dodge Charger prototype carrying a solid-state battery from Factorial, a Massachusetts startup Stellantis has worked with since 2021. The two companies run it as one team, and Factorial co-founder Siyu Huang says the startup has done it with $250 million in capital, a fraction of what some competitors have spent. The car worked the first time they turned the key. But Huang puts the technology three to five years from automotive production, and Laliron cuts about 10% of her group’s projects every year when they don’t pan out. “It’s always painful,” she said. Elsewhere, the speed-ups are real, but still small next to Stellantis’ problems. Arturo Guzman was among the first to bring AI into the company’s aerodynamics work, about five years ago, after a startup struggled to explain it to him in a side conversation at a conference. Now he explains it to vice presidents, and he starts by taking the mystery out. “There’s no miracles. There’s no magic,” he said. “For us being engineers, all this is a Python code.” Run through his team’s model, a design change to a Ram pickup’s front splitter returns a drag estimate in about three minutes. The full simulation used to take 14 to 24 hours. He tells his directors it’s like an intern. His younger engineers get a different metaphor: R2-D2 from Star Wars. “You just give it a task and come back and see what it did.” Guzman said the time his team saves goes to “other stuff.” His team now generates new guidelines, takes requests from across the company, and mines its simulation data for patents. “Now I tell them, just send me the data,” he said. Through the doors to the wind tunnel, the giant equipment runs at pretty much the same pace it always has. It still tests “typically, one car a day,” said Steve Kubiak, a deadpan Michigander who started at Chrysler in 1992 and has supervised the tunnel for about 10 years. Asked whether the process has changed over time, he said, “No, it’s always been the same.” The analysis is speeding up even if the testing isn’t. A colleague, Todd Lounsberry, gives AI the data from the last 20 runs of a test and asks what it sees. Asked how someone becomes the manager of a wind tunnel, Kubiak responded, “First you get a wind tunnel.” Other engineers are keeping AI on a short leash. Mohammed Malik, who runs Stellantis’ driving simulators from the Automotive Research & Development Centre in Windsor, Ontario, said his group is “keeping it in the box,” but even so, technology has transformed his work. Malik at work is like a life-size, immersive game of Mario Kart, the classic racing game where Nintendo characters pick their car and track then try to dodge obstacles to the finish line. His job has been transformed by his simulator more than by AI. Engineers used to tune a vehicle on a computer, build a prototype and only then find out how it felt. Now a driver can feel the computer model before anything is built, and Malik can swap springs on the fly. He can test 10 tires in an hour, something no prototype can do. His team has scanned about 20 roads, from racetracks to local streets, in 10-centimeter squares, and it trades models with colleagues in Brazil and Italy instead of shipping cars across oceans. Malik plays me a video of a full-size Stellantis body that lacks any wheels — instead it’s suspended on what appear to be mechanical spider-legs, bobbing up and down, back and forth in front of a giant TV screen with a track. Asked whether he ever played Mario Kart, he said he has a track of his own, built by a student. “You can drive the Rainbow Road if you want,” he beams from behind his bushy mustache. AI’s role is narrower. A simulator run produces thousands of channels of data, and when a car hits a bump and “swirls a little bit,” Malik asks AI which channel to look at. His ambition is much bigger: feed an agent every test driver’s scorecards from every region, ask it “what does it take to make a Jeep,” and let it drive the simulator itself until the answer feels right. For now, he said, the company is moving at a very slow pace and wants some form of control. “Some of the stuff we want to do, we can do today,” he said. “And some of the stuff we want to do, we cannot.” The bigger surprise has been people’s willingness, or lack thereof, to adapt. Veteran test drivers who spent 10 or 20 years in physical prototypes were slow to move to the simulator. Young engineers with racing setups in their basements took to it right away. Laliron has seen the same thing. Experienced engineers carry old failures with them, she said, and sometimes “their brain is closing doors.” Some people have made peace with work that is better but not faster. Roberts said her AI research tool returns 10 to 15 sources, and she has to check every one. “I don’t think it saves time,” she said, though it makes her work more “robust.” Alison Rahm, the chief vehicle engineer for midsize and large SUVs, has seen this before. She is moving back to Michigan after two years running Stellantis’ European car lineup from Paris, and she remembers a staff meeting in 1997, when her boss told the room they were getting something new called email. “All of us were in a total panic,” she said. They went back to their desks and tried it anyway. AI now runs checks that people used to do by hand, Rahm said, like going down a list of about 1,000 engineering design practices. That frees engineers to ask whether the rules themselves are right. But she isn’t ready to stop double-checking. For years after virtual crash testing arrived, she notes, engineers still crashed real cars into the walls to convince themselves the situations were right. Asked how long it will take to trust AI the same way, Rahm paused. “I think we need another life cycle to prove to ourselves that our assumptions were right.” While cautioning that different products have different life cycles, she deliberated. “I would say four years.” That would land in 2030, the year Filosa has promised a 7% margin. Saha drives us back into Iacocca’s garage, and puts his hands back on the wheel of the Cherokee as the diamond-shaped parking structure looms. The robot needs a guiding hand coming home. Saha takes a long view on when the AI game will shake out. As we roll into a smooth parking job, he turns to me and says he’s a Game of Thrones fan (his favorite character is Robb Stark). While he works in logic, he explains, he likes fantasy because he finds he needs “to be a little bit philosophical” with the other side of his brain. “Creativity is very important.” Saha takes a long view on when the AI game will shake out. As we roll into a smooth parking job, he turns to me and says he’s a Game of Thrones fan (his favorite character is Robb Stark). While he works in logic, he explains, he likes fantasy because he finds he needs “to be a little bit philosophical” with the other side of his brain. “Creativity is very important.” Humans have adapted and thrived to date by figuring out creative solutions, he said. “We were never the strongest animal in the world,” he said, riffing a bit in the style of Game of Thrones writer George R.R. Martin. He suggests that several hundred years of industrialization led people sometimes to get used to process-oriented thinking. “My understanding is we’re going to leave behind the process-oriented world because AI is going to help us. And my personal belief is that’s actually good.” “There will be some loss,” Saha said, as there is with any technological shift. But he pointed out that humans have adapted to the natural world through creativity over and over again. “People are going to figure out how to tame AI, too.” [This report has been updated to clarify Ned Curic and Joydip Saha’s job titles with more specificity: Chief Engineering & Technology Officer and owner of L2++/hands-free door-to-door supervised automated driving, respectively.] Nick Lichtenberg is business editor and was formerly Fortune's executive editor of global news. © 2026 Fortune Media IP Limited. Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network
2026-10-30

Ireland joins European Lab for Learning and Intelligent Systems network

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Alongside Norway, it is Ireland’s first time being included in the pan-European network. Ireland has joined the European Laboratory for Learning and Intelligent Systems (ELLIS) network after officially launching ELLIS Unit Ireland. The new unit marks the first time Ireland has joined the network, as ELLIS welcomed other new units in Austria, France, Germany and fellow first-timer Norway. The network has 49 units across Europe and aims to propel the continent as a leader at the forefront of AI research. The units operate as localised hubs whereby researchers can work together, share ideas across a range of disciplines and carry out long-term research. The unit is part of Rinn AI, which is Ireland’s national centre for research and innovation in data science and AI and is funded by Research Ireland. Prof Noel O’Connor, Rinn AI’s director, will lead ELLIS Unit Ireland, which will focus on advancing fundamental AI research in core machine learning and related areas such as computer vision, natural language processing and information retrieval. The unit will also work closely with domain experts, policymakers and societal stakeholders, as a means of addressing real-world challenges. There will be a focus on connecting researchers across the country and training the next generation of ML and AI researchers. Commenting on Ireland’s inclusion to the network, O’Connor said: “Joining ELLIS is a significant moment for AI research in Ireland. Through Rinn AI, we have brought together researchers from every university in the country and ELLIS Unit Ireland now connects that community directly to Europe’s leading network in machine learning. “It opens new doors for collaboration, gives our researchers and students a platform on the European stage and helps ensure the AI we develop is shaped by the people it serves.” SiliconRepublic.com recently spoke with Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork, about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Silicon Republic
Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares
2026-10-30

Spooky science: How your brain conjures up night-time scares

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
University of Bristol’s Prof Michelle Spear looks at how your brain could be responsible for things that go bump in the night, not ghosts. A version of this article was originally published by The Conversation (CC BY-ND 4.0) It’s three in the morning. You’ve woken from a dead sleep because you’ve heard a thud in the darkness or a floorboard creaking downstairs. Or perhaps you’ve woken because you have the unmistakable sensation you’re not alone. During daylight, these phenomena might be dismissed as the heating, your neighbours or the sounds of an old house settling. But in the middle of the night, they can feel a tad more sinister. Your mind might immediately start reeling, jumping to the worst-case scenario – wondering if you’ve been broken into, or if you’re being haunted by some supernatural force. In reality, the explanation is far more simple. Things really do go bump in the night. But occasionally, the culprit is closer to home than we might imagine. Falling asleep is not the neurological equivalent of switching off a light. The brain moves gradually from wakefulness into sleep, accompanied by changes in sensory processing, muscle activity and awareness of the world around us. But these changes don’t always occur in perfect synchrony. So for brief periods, characteristics of sleep and wakefulness can overlap. The results can be surprisingly spooky. If you’ve ever been on the verge of sleep only to feel that you’ve fallen off a kerb, missed a step or dropped through the bed, you’ve probably experienced a hypnic jerk. Also known as a sleep start, this is a sudden involuntary contraction of one or more muscles as we move into the earliest stages of sleep. It’s an example of how the transition between wakefulness and sleep isn’t always a smooth one. Hypnagogic experiences can also involve vivid sounds, images or sensations. So as you’re on the edge of sleep, you may think you hear footsteps or whispers – or you may even think you see a figure in the room. Exploding head syndrome – perhaps the most alarming name for a harmless sleep condition – is another example of how the boundaries between sleep and wakefulness can become blurred. As someone is falling asleep, or occasionally waking, they suddenly perceive an enormous noise: an explosion, gunshot or slamming door – yet nothing in the outside world has made a sound. Its precise cause remains uncertain. But the phenomenon demonstrates something important: hearing a sound and a sound actually occurring are not quite the same thing. The conscious experience of hearing is constructed by the brain. So neural activity generated without an external sound can still produce an auditory experience that seems entirely real. The brain doesn’t even need to be asleep for this to happen. If you’ve ever thought you’ve heard someone call your name – only to turn around and find no one there – you’ve been tricked by your brain. Another possible explanation for the sounds we think we hear at night involves source monitoring. This is the brain’s ability to distinguish events generated internally from those coming from the outside world. Thoughts, memories and imagined speech are internally generated, yet normally retain the quality of being ours. But occasionally, that attribution goes awry – allowing something generated within the brain to feel as though it came from somewhere else. Around sleep, that boundary becomes particularly interesting. Another common – but frightening – experience that can happen at night is waking up with the sudden feeling that someone else is in the room. This experience is usually the result of sleep paralysis. During rapid eye movement (REM) sleep, when most of our vivid dreaming occurs, the brain temporarily suppresses activity in most muscles. This protective mechanism, known as REM atonia, prevents us from physically acting out our dreams. But during sleep paralysis, our waking awareness returns while this physical paralysis persists. You know where you are, but cannot move. Yet elements of dream perception can remain active. This is why you may have experienced the sensation that someone – or something – is in the room with you, despite clear sensory evidence of the contrary. One clue as to why this happens comes from experiments examining how the brain distinguishes itself from others. In one study, participants controlled a robotic device positioned in front of them, while a second robot, which was placed behind them, reproduced these movements by touching the participants’ backs. But when researchers introduced a slight delay between the movement and the touch, some participants began to feel as though somebody else was touching them. Some even sensed another person standing behind them. While this experiment doesn’t explain every felt presence that can happen during sleep paralysis, it does reveal something remarkable: the distinction between ourselves and others is something the brain has to construct. Usually, movement, touch and our sense of body position agree about what belongs to us. But if that relationship is disrupted, an internally generated sensation can begin to feel like it originated elsewhere. Now picture this happening in a dark bedroom, when you’re partly awake yet still on the edge of dreaming and unable to move. The sensation that somebody else is there becomes rather less mysterious. Perception is not a passive recording of the world. It combines incoming sensory information with expectation and previous experience to construct the most likely explanation. But at the boundary between sleep and waking, this process can become less reliable. We may hear sounds that were never made, experience fragments of dreams intruding our bedroom or interpret sensations arising from our own bodies as the presence of somebody else. Michelle Spear is a professor of anatomy at the University of Bristol. Her research focuses on the architecture of synovial joints, drawing on her experience in histopathology to examine clinical and functional aspects of tissue-based research. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. Silicon Republic
Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland
2026-10-30

Rinn Semiconductors director’s vision for the sector in Ireland

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Prof Paul Townsend tells us about his plans for the semiconductors research centre that sits within Research Ireland’s new Rinn Network. Over the summer, seven new Research Ireland centres were launched to address key research areas deemed pertinent to the country’s scientific, medical, economic and technological development. The centres belong to a national research network called ‘Rinn’ and between them are set to receive €460m in Government investments over the next eight years, supporting 577 research positions, developing more than 800 PhDs and collaborating with 17 research-performing organisations. The seven Rinn research centres – dedicated to the fields of advanced therapies, AI, energy, medical devices, pharma and biopharma, quantum, and semiconductors – began their work in July, with each being led by directors who are experts in their sectors. Rinn Semiconductors is set to receive nearly €71m of Government funding overall and is headed by Prof Paul Townsend, a photonics specialist in the School of Physics at University College Cork (UCC) and with Tyndall National Institute who was born and grew up in the UK. “I studied physics at university, gaining a PhD from the University of Cambridge on the novel properties of polymers that behave as one-dimensional semiconductors,” he tells SiliconRepublic.com. His subsequent work in industry included time spent at British Telecom on quantum cryptography, the then-emerging concept of exploiting quantum physics to secure information. This work, he explains, was ancestral to “the quantum information and computing revolution that we frequently read about” today. “In 2003, I had the opportunity to move to Ireland with my family and colleagues to found a new group at UCC and Tyndall, working on optical fibre communication systems with funding from Science Foundation Ireland,” says Townsend. Tasked then with supporting the development of the wider photonics research ecosystem and engagement with industry in Ireland, this has since become the focus of his time working with Tyndall as its head of photonics research. “My ambition is to take this an exciting further step forward with Rinn Semiconductors,” says Townsend. The centre is set to focus on research and innovation in heterogeneously integrated semiconductor systems, and aims to lead Ireland’s response to the point at which traditional improvements in computer chips diminishes (the end of Moore’s Law scaling) by pioneering modular integrated circuit-based – or chiplet-based – semiconductor design and integration. Chiplet architecture is a method of using multiple smaller modules to build a single larger processor for a more powerful end result. The Rinn Semiconductors team will seek transformative advances in these technologies and to apply them in three main areas: future internet, sustainable energy and the environment, and digital healthcare. “Today, semiconductor chips touch nearly every aspect of our daily lives; they are in phones, computers and medical technology,” notes Townsend. “Almost any problem you can think of – societal or technological – requires the use of semiconductors to provide a solution. “This is also a technology where Ireland plays a leading role. We are home to a world-class semiconductor industry sector – with a 50-year operating history – that employs 20,000 people in more than 100 companies. “The national semiconductor strategy, Silicon Island, aims to build upon this to position Ireland internationally as a hub of semiconductor excellence and to contribute to the EU Chips Act goal of growing the EU’s proportion of global semiconductor manufacturing output.” Key elements of the Government’s Silicon Island strategy, he notes, include strengthening research capacity, supporting start-ups and spin-outs, and building a future-ready talent pipeline through education and skills programmes. “Rinn Semiconductors is extremely well-positioned to make major contributions to the achievement of these goals,” he says. Another “key objective” of the centre, according to Townsend, is “to foster deep collaboration across the seven universities and institutions involved” by leveraging the “world-class” semiconductor fabrication facilities and infrastructure at Tyndall. The centre plans for what it calls “radical collaboration” with and between Dublin City University, Munster Technological University, Trinity College Dublin, Tyndall, UCC, University of Galway and University of Limerick. This, it is hoped, will lead to “a cohesive ‘atom to systems’ value chain in Ireland, connecting everything from basic materials research through to complete technology solutions”, says Townsend. The Rinn Network, in his view, offers the opportunity to leverage coordinated research at scale, which is “critical” to the development of complex technologies such as semiconductors. “The significant scale of the industry collaborative research projects we have established in just the first two months of operation gives me great confidence that this vision can be realised.” As centre director, Townsend is responsible for ensuring that his programme is “suitably prioritised, innovative, impactful, internationally leading in its focus areas and, critically, delivers on its mission and goals”. He is also in charge of budgeting, and overseeing grant compliance and reporting. “In addition, I take the lead on stakeholder and external relations management, and act as an advocate for the centre and its mission,” he says. Another important part of his leadership role is “to ensure the centre is a beacon for attracting and developing talent at all levels from PhD students to senior principal investigators (PIs)” in order to “address the demand from industry for highly-skilled scientists and engineers, as well as to sustain the academic research ecosystem needed to supply these trainees and keep Ireland at the forefront of semiconductor research”. Apart from the development and implementation of the centre’s scientific and operational strategy, Townsend – whose personal research background is in optical fibre communications and sensing – gets some hands-on time at Rinn Semiconductors, too. “I am also an active PI in the centre, where my group focuses on developing new integrated semiconductor solutions for two main application areas: energy-efficient interconnect technologies for AI clusters in data centres, with the goal of constraining the overall power consumption of AI; and distributed acoustic sensing in optical fibre cables, where we target improved monitoring of undersea communication cables and offshore windfarm power cables to ensure resilience and protection.” Moving forward, Townsend says his Rinn centre is targeting progress in four main areas: research breakthroughs in semiconductor chiplet integration and packaging technologies; translation of these ideas into new manufacturable technologies and products; acting as a catalyst for talent development; and driving engaged research programmes with stakeholders for impact on policy and society. The latter, he hopes, could include “addressing the early stages of the talent supply problem by working with teachers and policymakers on how to encourage more school and university students, particularly women, to take up physics and engineering subjects that can lead to a career in the semiconductor industry”. Townsend notes, too, the potential of the overall network for fostering collaboration and shared progress across the seven cornerstone areas, and the prospect of creating “greater value and impact than could be achieved by a group of individual centres working in a less connected way”. “The wider Rinn Network plan adds the exciting possibility for centres to work together on complex problems that can only be addressed through an interdisciplinary approach. “I think the scale and ambition of the overall programme will also be a great attractor for external talent and a fantastic springboard for researchers already in Ireland to advance their careers. An especially important goal to him on an individual level, he adds, would be “the development of the next generation of exciting, talented researchers who will take Ireland on to even greater success in the future”. “I am very fortunate and proud to have had the opportunity to work in Ireland and to contribute at least somewhat to the development of the national research ecosystem. “On the personal side, my wife and I and our two now-grown-up daughters all live happily in and around Cork – and wouldn’t want to be anywhere else.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Intel shares drop after weak forecast over global PC slowdown

‘We see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,’ said Intel’s CFO. Intel shares dropped by nearly 24pc in pre-market trading today (30 October) after the chipmaker gave a weak fourth-quarter forecast over a slowed PC market. The company expects to bring in between $15bn and $16bn in revenue in the upcoming quarter. The figures are lower than reported analyst expectations for Q4 revenue of around $17.1bn. The US semiconductor giant announced a third-quarter revenue of $16.5bn – up 21pc over last year, in line with its expectations. Revenue from its client computing and physical AI division dropped by 5pc to $8.1bn while data centre and AI products gained 78pc to $7.3bn. CEO Lip-Bu Tan noted that the growth reflects “improving execution” and “meaningful progress expanding supply”, and said that demand for its CPUs remain “robust” despite “near-term headwinds across the PC industry”. Soaring memory prices – driven up by the continued unprecedented demand for AI chips – has had a notable negative impact across the consumer devices market, including personal computers. According to data from Counterpoint Research, global PC shipments fell 21pc in Q3 compared to last year. The report also forecasts a 27pc year-over-year drop in sales for the upcoming quarter. “Looking ahead, we see the PC slowdown continuing at least through the first half of next year,” noted Intel chief financial officer David Zinsner. “We remain optimistic about the opportunities across our broader portfolio, and we will continue making disciplined investments in capacity and working capital to increase supply and support future growth.” Just days ago, Intel announced plans to purchase Israeli quantum company Classiq to create a full-stack quantum computing platform. The company has been investing in the technology since 2015. In August, Intel completed a $23bn public equity offering to continue funding future growth opportunities. Intel shed about 1,200 employees between late June and September this year, its filings further showed. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal
2026-10-30

Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell awarded UCD Ulysses Medal

Silicon Republic | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
Jocelyn Bell Burnell. Image: Chris Bellew, Fennell Photography The astrophysicist has a long legacy of scientific research, discovery and supporting the wider STEM community. Astrophysicist Jocelyn Bell Burnell has been awarded the UCD Ulysses Medal for her outstanding scientific achievements and ongoing commitment to developing the next generation of researchers. The Ulysses Medal is awarded to those who have contributed something of immense importance on a global scale. Previous recipients include author Margaret Atwood, former Irish President Mary McAleese and the ‘Godfather of AI’, Prof Geoffrey Hinton. Raised in Northern Ireland, Burnell previously described her early STEM education as challenging. Despite her assurance that she wanted to study the stars surrounding our planet, her gender precluded her from engaging with STEM subjects in school, as they were aimed primarily at young men. She persevered, becoming one of only three young girls taking science in her secondary school, where she topped the class in physics and then continued on to the University of Glasgow and then Cambridge, where she undertook a PhD. It was at Cambridge where she worked on the development of a radio space telescope designed to explore the furthest reaches of space and where she discovered a “scruff” on one of the charts she was analysing. This was the moment she identified what would become known as a pulsating radio star, or pulsar. Despite her work however, Burnell was notably excluded when the discovery was recognised by the Nobel Prize in Physics in 1974. The prize was instead awarded to her PhD supervisor, Antony Hewish and a fellow Cambridge professor, Martin Ryle. Her exclusion stands as a prime example of how the achievements of women were so often disregarded in favour of their male co-workers and supervisors. In 2018, she donated £2.3m to start the Bell Burnell Graduate Scholarship Fund, a fund supporting students coming from underrepresented backgrounds. The money was part of the Special Breakthrough Prize in Fundamental Physics that she had earned for her previous discovery and five decades of leadership in the field. Prof Lorraine Hanlon , a full professor of astronomy at UCD, said Burnell had been “peerless in how she has deployed her brilliance, transforming her groundbreaking discovery into lifelong advocacy for curiosity-driven research, equity and global science diplomacy. “The achievements of all the Bell Burnell scholars stand as living proof of Dame Jocelyn’s enduring legacy in recognising and empowering the next generation of scientific leaders.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Futurism
Prisons Using AI to Predict When Incarcerated People Will Commit Future Crimes
2026-11-01

Prisons Using AI to Predict When Incarcerated People Will Commit Future Crimes

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Michigan is set to deploy a major new AI system to prisons across the state, meant to classify incarcerated people based on “how dangerous they are.” That’s according to Detroit channel Fox2, which reports that the Michigan Department of Corrections is plowing ahead with an inmate categorization algorithm that will sort people into various housing levels. In Michigan, incarcerated people are separated into five housing levels according to their security classification. In the past, screenings were performed by a committee of staff appointed by the Warden at each facility. Now, it seems the state wants to outsource that decision — or at least have it be heavily informed by — a non-profit tech company called Recidiviz. Recidiviz began as a data organization project at Google, meant to “uncover how technology could standardize criminal justice data and improve the system nationwide.” Its AI-integrated software platform is said to perform features like identifying individuals eligible for state-offered programs, reclassification, or release, and coordinate access to resources like transitional housing and employment. In a press release, MDOC said the Recidiviz software will be used to “optimize placement of incarcerated individuals” starting with a pilot program in a number of men’s facilities beginning in December. The state agency notes that the AI tool is expected to “improve MDOC’s ability to more accurately predict incarcerated individuals’ risk of future violence by approximately 30 percent,” though how the state arrived at that vague figure is unclear. For incarcerated people, housing designations are extremely consequential, determining which programming and resources an incarcerated person is eligible for, and what kind of population they’ll be housed with for the duration of their sentence. Setting the state’s rationale aside, the MDOC-Recividiz partnership leaves some major questions unanswered. What kind of data will be collected, for example, will matter a great deal with regard to the AI system’s accuracy. As a ProPublica investigation into a similar risk-analysis program in Louisiana found, the kind of data being used to classify incarcerated people contained inherent racial and socioeconomic bias. As a result, Louisiana’s carceral system has become even more cruel and harmful to those earnestly working toward rehabilitation. In one case reported by ProPublica, a blind and wheelchair bound 70-year-old was determined to be a “moderate risk” of reoffending despite having a clean disciplinary record — a classification which results in an automatic loss of eligibility for parole. While Michigan’s system is geared toward housing assignments rather than parole decisions, it seems highly unlikely that software alone will solve the state’s ongoing corrections crisis — and if Louisiana’s embrace of AI is any example, it could very well make matters far, far worse. Michigan Department of Corrections was contacted for comment. More on incarceration: What Are the Ethics of Strapping VR Headsets on Inmates in Solitary Confinement?

Microsoft Patents an AI That Can Literally Put Words in People’s Mouths in Real Time
2026-11-01

Microsoft Patents an AI That Can Literally Put Words in People’s Mouths in Real Time

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Microsoft just filed a patent for an AI product that would be able to transform live video in real time to alter speakers’ words and faces. The patent, as first flagged by patent-tracker Patentlyze, is described by its inventors as a “near real-time artificial intelligence (AI) enhancement of video or augmented reality (AR) presentations of live events.” Using AI agents, the system would identify and alter or remove certain words, while editing a speaker’s face and mouth to match. The agents would rely on set parameters or user profiles to make the changes, and would also edit the video and audio just before and after a removed or replaced word to allow for “smooth transitions.” In an era of media and the internet increasingly defined by AI-fueled deepfakes, disinformation, and information-muddling slop, the system described in this patent may represent a new world of filtering tools that subtly alter reality as it takes place, in this case on a highly-personalized level. Viewers, the patent suggests, could consume whatever live event they want to without hearing what they don’t want to hear. The potential use cases floated in the patent vary. One possible application is real-time translation, with the system allowing for broadcasts and other live footage to be both heard and seen in a viewer’s preferred language. The patent also posits that such a tool could be used to remove filler words like “um” or “ah” from live speech, or to erase stutters or speech impediments. Other possible use cases, though, go much further. According to the patent, the system could be used to cut down on “personal words or phrases” like “coined terms,” as well as “colloquial terms” like the term “y’all” or the phrase “bite the bullet.” The patent also claims that the system could eliminate “mumbled words, mispronounced words, misused words, misspoken words, offensive words, insensitive words, or inappropriate words.” Replacement words, it adds, may come from an “internet search.” In other words, live broadcasts may not be so live anymore after all. Microsoft didn’t immediately respond to a request for comment. Dubbing has existed for ages, and video footage is edited all the time, both for normal and for insidious reasons. Automatically editing live videos in this way with the help of AI agents, however, could change how audiences fundamentally engage with live footage. As it stands, if people don’t like what they hear, they can simply turn something off; the system outlined by Microsoft describes an environment where people can instead adjust someone else’s speech to their personal taste. Language translation aside, does using a tool that eliminates errors, or erases words and phrases associated with a speaker’s local dialect, move us further into personalized bubbles? What’s so bad about hearing someone from the South say “y’all?” To that end, the patent does mention that an event’s original live video and audio may be recorded and stored for the sake of “ground truth,” nodding to potential misuse and confusion that could arise from such a tool. The kinds of live footage that could be transformed by the system include lectures, presentations, a range of television productions — awards ceremonies, game and cooking shows — and live sports, among several others. More on Big Tech patents: Meta Patents Technology to Detect Your Mood by Constantly Analyzing Your Tone of Voice

New Data Centers in the US Set to Wipe Out 15 Years of Greenhouse Gas Reductions
2026-11-01

New Data Centers in the US Set to Wipe Out 15 Years of Greenhouse Gas Reductions

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Nearly 15 years worth of hard-won pollution reduction efforts are set to be undone in the United States, as a wave of data centers powered by gas plants enters construction. That’s according to a new analysis by the Sierra Club, first reported by Bloomberg, which notes that the US energy grid is expected to belch out 2.1 billion metric tons of carbon dioxide by 2030, assuming the country’s currently-planned AI developments enter operation as scheduled. That’s a massive rise compared to even 2025 — itself a near-record year for US carbon emissions. If the AI industry and its data center developers get their way, the analysis found, emissions will likely skyrocket by 42 percent over the five-year period, an expected increase of 618 million tons compared to 2025. “The numbers continue to sort of boggle our minds,”  the Sierra Club’s principal advisor for climate and energy Jeremy Fisher told Bloomberg. Due to major bottlenecks in the electrical grid, many data center developers have opted for on- or near-site power plants, which are overwhelmingly fueled by noxious fossil fuels. Projects like Elon Musk’s 1.2 gigawatt data center near Memphis are already choking out residents with fleets of methane generators, which legal experts say flaunt all kinds of local permitting and federal environmental regulations. Still, for anybody banking on having clean air to breathe, there is some hope. The number of data center projects at risk of cancellation is rising precipitously as time goes on — driven by grass roots organizing and increasing financial pressures. So if you’re one of the many activists mounting opposition to data centers in your community, just know that the benefits of a cancelled hyperscale facility extend far beyond your backyard. More on data centers: Thieves Are Making a Fortune Heisting AI Data Center Equipment
Futurism
Former Crypto Regulator Insists That Gambling on the Weather Is Inherently a Good Thing and “Human Nature”
2026-11-01

Former Crypto Regulator Insists That Gambling on the Weather Is Inherently a Good Thing and “Human Nature”

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
In a live appearance on Bloomberg TV, James Christopher Giancarlo, former chairman of the US Commodity Futures Trading Commission which regulates digital commodity and digital asset trading, insisted that America’s growing addiction to prediction markets is a net benefit for society as a whole. During the interview, Bloomberg anchor Tim Stenovec challenged the former watchdog around the “existential question” posed by prediction markets. “I have people getting in touch with me on the Bloomberg terminal all the time who are like, ‘I do not want my kids exposed to this stuff,’ like the gamification of everything in society,” Stenovec said. “They don’t see that as a good thing.” Giancarlo — who now has a cushy life advising the companies he used to regulate, including Polymarket — replied that “like it or not, people like to gamble.” “Human nature is human nature,” Giancarlo said. “I think there’s a social benefit in prediction markets.” For the former regulator, that benefit evidently comes from the fact that people are gambling billions of dollars on things like weather patterns and oil prices. “People like certainty. Why, during a weather condition, is everybody listening to Bloomberg’s weather? Because they want some predictability of the weather,” Giancarlo enthused. “Heretofore there’s been no predictability in things that are actually more important than the weather — interest rates, gas prices, the outcome of elections — those are important things to a lot of people.” Though Giancarlo said he’s in favor of strong federal regulations around betting markets, he said allowing them to proliferate provides useful data: the so-called “wisdom of the crowd.” “If you can pull the wisdom of the crowds of tens of thousands or hundreds of thousands, it’s a lot better than your average poll which polls maybe 800 people,” he continued. “I think there’s a social benefit in this, whether you participate in them or not, you may like the data that comes out.” Of course, Giancarlo’s effusive praise for the gamification of weather patterns deserves some scrutiny. For starters, as the sitting chair of Polymarket’s advisory board, the prediction market guru is about the farthest thing from a neutral messenger. Nevermind the fact that the “wisdom of the crowd” phenomenon is hotly contested, with critics arguing prediction markets are actually driven by a tiny number of bettors, are heavily weighted toward high rollers, and incentivize insider trading and mass disinformation campaigns. A recent case-in-point came during the recent presidential elections in Brazil, where far-right candidate Flávio Bolsonaro was overwhelmingly pegged as the favorite on Polymarket — confidence which held up until the moment Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva emerged as the victor. Innocent People Are Being Accused of Shoplifting by Faulty AI-Facial Recognition Systems at an Alarming Rate
2026-11-01

Innocent People Are Being Accused of Shoplifting by Faulty AI-Facial Recognition Systems at an Alarming Rate

Futurism | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
From New York City to the streets of Tokyo, creepy AI-facial recognition security systems are quickly becoming a common sight across the world. Unfortunately for many, the tech is nowhere near ready for the responsibility of live use as the troubling rise of false-positive matches makes clear. In the United Kingdom, AI surveillance systems in retail stores have led to a surge in customers being wrongly accused of shoplifting. TechRadar highlighted a number of recent cases throughout the country, where shoppers say false accusations have left them shaken, with a feeling that their privacy had been horribly violated. As one woman identified as Mary told the publication, her husband has been socially “isolated” after a quick jaunt into a Home Bargains store for vitamins went awry. When the stores’ system flagged the anonymous man as a shoplifter, the couple was forced to leave by management, seemingly without any chance to refute the accusation. “I was scared to go into a shop,” the man told TechRadar. Another man was said to be “shaking from head to toe” following his wife’s removal from a Budgens chain store for the crime of stealing “loads of meat” days before — an accusation complicated by the fact that they hadn’t set foot in the store for months. In these and other cases, the publication reports a dizzying labyrinth of corporate bureaucracy and vague insinuations that leave innocent people unable to access their local businesses, and with little recourse to clear their name. In the UK, individuals can file Subject Access Requests, or SARs, which are like US-Freedom of Information Act requests but for private companies. Though the avenue technically exists for those wrongly accused of shoplifting, it’s a massive undertaking just for the hope of clearing your name — and requires handing over even more private information to private companies. As head of advocacy at civil liberty group Big Brother Watch Jack Coulson told TechRadar, it’s “the stuff of Kafkaesque nightmares to be trapped in a maze of bureaucracy and forced to hand over even more personal data just to be told the accusation.” More on facial recognition: Innocent Man Freed After Spending Over 50 Days in Jail Due to Horribly Inaccurate AI Facial Recognition Tech

AI can make tech CEOs’ lives easier. What about everyone else?
2026-11-01

AI can make tech CEOs’ lives easier. What about everyone else?

TechCrunch | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Dazzle AI CEO Marissa Mayer recently became the latest tech CEO to face criticism over seemingly out-of-touch comments extolling the usefulness of AI. In Mayer’s case, that involved asking an AI tool for help with a school-assigned description of her daughter. For OpenAI CEO Sam Altman, that’s included suggesting that parents create personalized podcasts for their kids’ drive to school and also declaring that he could not imagine parenting without ChatGPT. Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg has even suggested that it will become increasingly acceptable to meet your social and romantic needs through AI. This all led TechCrunch’s Amanda Silberling to wonder if AI executives are simply too out-of-touch to build products for normal people — a crucial question with Anthropic set to go public just as Wall Street begins losing its appetite for heavy AI spending. On the latest episode of the Equity podcast, Kirsten Korosec, Sean O’Kane, and I chatted with Amanda about why industry leaders can sometimes seem so tone deaf. Kirsten wondered if this is more of a marketing problem or a product problem. Amanda’s answer: “A little bit of both.” She also argued that the tech industry is increasingly pushing a future “where basic human interaction gets more and more optimized out of actually occurring,” adding, “And call me old school, but I think that humans being able to talk to each other is probably a net positive for society.” Keep reading for a preview of our conversation, edited for length and clarity. Anthony Ha: There’s a lot of societal skepticism about AI, this feeling that the people running these companies don’t have our best interests at heart, that they’re out of touch. And so, you would just think, “Maybe don’t make statements like that,” because of course, people are going to see them very skeptically. I think [CEOs] are trying to walk this delicate balance. On the one hand, there’s a tremendous financial incentive for them to make the case that you should use this technology in every aspect of your lives. But when you make that case, it actually [doesn’t paint] a very appealing picture. In some cases, it’s downright dystopian. Kirsten Korosec: Anthony, if they took your advice, we would have nothing to talk about on this show. But I will say this: There’s this whole idea of ChatGPT and other AI tools making your life easier when it comes to personal relationships. And in many of these cases, it just seems to overcomplicate what would be, like, observing your child. That might be hard for some people, the people who are building these products, and it doesn’t necessarily relate to everyone else out there in the world. It can just seem so incredibly disconnected when you see these quotes. To me, it always comes back to — who are they speaking to? Are there people out there who can relate? Maybe there are. Amanda Silberling: Other use cases we’ve seen people mention are, like, the Bumble CEO Whitney Wolf Heard has made comments about, “Well, what if we had AI concierges, your AI dates the other person’s AI. And then if the AIs have a good date together, then they introduce the people.” Or in the New York Times article, Marissa Mayer mentioned, “Well, what if my AI could coordinate with my husband’s AI” and stuff like that. And I think that it feels like people are trying to over-optimize out of just having conversations with people. I’ve already seen that happen in my generation, where I do notice in myself that if I’m ordering takeout, it feels weird to call a place. I’m just so used to, “Well, obviously you can do it online and you don’t have to talk to anyone. And I feel like there’s just going to be more of these things, where basic human interaction gets more and more optimized out of actually occurring. And call me old school, but I think that humans being able to talk to each other is probably a net positive for society. Sean O’Kane: Yeah, but what’s the ROI, Amanda? How much revenue can you generate off of a conversation that somebody’s having in meatspace? Amanda: That is the thing, though. Dating is really inefficient. It sucks that you have to go on a bunch of dates with different people. You might waste an evening and be like, “Well, that was a bust.” And so you can see why people are [wondering,] “What if you didn’t have to go on dates? What if your AI could go on dates for you?” But there’s just something not human there. And then also, you are outsourcing the decision of who you are going to date to a computer that you are trusting knows you better than yourself, which I don’t think is true. Kirsten: One thing that I think is worth going back to, and I sort of hinted about this earlier: Who are they talking to? Who is their audience for all these optimizations, in your view? Is it a product problem, or is it a messaging problem? Are they just talking about it in the wrong way, and actually, there’s this really great value in here and it just seems out of touch with reality and humanity, but if they just talked about it differently, it would be better? Or is it actually a product problem, and to your point, that we’re focusing on optimizing sort of the wrong things with AI tools? Amanda: I think it’s a little bit of both, but I think that the marketing problem comes from when you have these high-up people at these massively valuable companies in a room together, talking about the pain points in their lives, and [they focus on] making dinner reservations, booking flights. Everybody does those things, but I’m not booking flights once a week. I’m not making dinner reservations all the time. And when I do, those aren’t really things that I find to be a particular challenge in my life. I mean, obviously people find value from things like ChatGPT and Claude, but I think the value that the average person is finding isn’t necessarily like booking a flight for me. Anthony:  One of the things I’ve also been thinking about is, there are companies like Uber that, when it first launched, it was targeting this tech early adopter set in San Francisco, and I remember being very confident [that] this will never catch on more broadly. This is just for a very narrow audience of young urban millennials who have money to spare on cars, and it works [uniquely] well in San Francisco. For a lot of things, the tech industry has succeeded by betting that, “People have not caught up with us, but we are the leading edge, and we reflect what the consumer of the future looks like, the family of the future looks like.” And in this case, it just seems like that bet is not borne out yet — for a variety of reasons, partly because the society that this technology is being promoted into is very different. People are starting to say, “I don’t love the influence that this technology is having in my life. I don’t love that you’re pitching this as something where every experience in my life should be optimized out of. But actually, I want to live my own life. I actually want to parent my own children.” Amanda: I think that it’s not a coincidence that at the same time we see this over-optimization happening among people in tech, that there’s also a movement of people that are reverting back to wanting to use flip phones, which I’ve talked about on Equity before, or people who are buying CDs because they’re tired of an algorithm telling them what they want to listen to. And it is just an interesting cultural tension that’s happening, where we haven’t found the balance of what optimization actually helps us and what optimization just makes us feel like we aren’t as human as we want to be. TechCrunch Mobility: A new, bigger AV boom is here

Welcome back to TechCrunch Mobility, your hub for the future of transportation and now, more than ever, the role AI is playing in it. To get this in your inbox, sign up here for free — just click TechCrunch Mobility! I’ve joked here and on the Equity and Autonocast podcasts that the autonomous vehicle industry feels a lot like 2016, at least in terms of the buzziness surrounding the sector. But a recent conversation with Trucks VC partner Puneeth Meruva has me rethinking that comparison. There’s evidence that 2026 is even hotter — at least by certain measures — than those frothy dealmaking days of 2016 and 2017. Trucks VC, which specializes in early-stage investing, has fielded pitches from 81 new autonomous vehicle startups so far in 2026 — two-thirds more than last year and enough to surpass the boom years of a decade ago.  The VC firm argues in a new blog post that we’re in the midst of a second, even bigger AV wave fueled by technological breakthroughs. Yes, I’m talking about AI. “For a long time AV companies were trying to go from what was AV 1.0 to AV 2.0, which is kind of this holy grail end-to-end architecture for AVs,” Meruva told me. “And I think the foundation models now provide a technical architecture that makes those end-to-end systems possible.” These foundational models are particularly good at generalizing across different environments and scenarios, potentially making AVs cheaper and faster to deploy. The arrival of powerful open source models like Nvidia’s Alpamayo and Qwen’s general-purpose vision-language-action models has made it even easier for startups to get off the ground. The barrier to entry is lower than ever before. Meruva said Trucks is consistently seeing startups deploy within a few months and with less than $1 million raised. (And while Meruva wouldn’t name names, the firm has invested in some of them.) Of these 81 startups that pitched Trucks VC, I mistakenly assumed most would be applying their AV tech to construction, warehouse, agriculture, and mining. And yes, those were in the mix. But what surprised me was the growing number of startups pitching pure-play robotaxi businesses. And many are positioning themselves as direct competitors to Waymo. As wild and galaxy brain-thinking as that may sound, Meruva thinks there is room for smaller players. “In this world, smaller, more niche autonomy companies can compete — maybe they’re not competing with Waymo on a global scale, but maybe they’re competing in specific regions or specific types of routes,” he said. And Meruva doesn’t see the pace flattening. He expects even more AV startups to pitch the firm next year. Regular readers of this newsletter know we’ve been closely following Slate Auto — breaking the original story on Jeff Bezos’ investment way back in April 2025. But the company it spun out of is what got our attention this week. Re:Build, a Massachusetts-based manufacturing accelerator, raised $2.1 billion to “expand Re:Build’s capacity to manufacture hard tech products and launch new industrial startups.” The investment was led by General Catalyst and JPMorganChase’s Strategic Investment Group. Other investors weren’t disclosed. The company, which was co-founded by Amazon’s former commercial CEO Jeff Wilke, has billed itself as “America’s answer to Shenzhen.” Bedrock Robotics raised $600 million in a funding round that values the autonomous vehicle technology startup at $4.1 billion. CapitalG and Emergence Capital co-led the Series C round. New investors US Innovative Technology Fund (USIT), Bessemer Venture Partners, and Temasek participated in the round, alongside existing investors 8VC, Georgian, Valor Equity Partners, Xora, Eclipse, Perry Creek Capital, and Nvidia. DoorDash has agreed to acquire Instacart for $17 billion. Meanwhile, DoorDash noted in its third-quarter earnings that it has, so far, only received a few hundred orders from AI agents. PreScalar, a San Francisco-based startup developing software and testing infrastructure for factories, raised $7 million in seed funding co-led by Dynamo Ventures and BMW i Ventures, with participation from Blue Wire Capital, Uplift Ventures, and Antler. The startup was founded last year by a trio who had stints at Cruise, Google, Rivian, and Waymo. Elroy Air, a startup developing autonomous heavy-cargo drones for defense, rapid response, and commercial logistics, hired Jeremy Hux as its CFO in preparation for going public. Ford announced two new EV chargers that will allow its forthcoming Fathom pickup truck to serve as a battery capable of delivering power to the home or grid. GM is embracing hybrids now. Times sure have changed! The U.S. automaker is also trying to move past the driver data-selling controversy from several years ago. GM agreed to a $220 million settlement with 36 states (its settlement with California and the Federal Trade Commission happened earlier) to settle allegations that it illegally sold private driving behavior and location information to data brokers. Kodiak AI will deliver 75 self-driving trucks to freight company DTL Transport starting in 2028. It’s the company’s first binding commercial deal for driverless big rigs that will run on public highways. Rivian reported its third-quarter earnings, and a few items stood out to us. For one, the new R2 has been successful enough for the company to raise its 2026 delivery guidance by a couple thousands vehicles. Rivian, which had previously expected to deliver between 65,000 and 70,000 EVs this year, bumped up that floor to 67,000. But here is another potentially lucrative development: The $5.8 billion joint venture between Rivian and Volkswagen Group is expanding to include AI assistants. No word yet on whether that means VW will invest even more money into Rivian. Tesla turns to Turo to boost adoption of Full Self-Driving (Supervised). Waymo and Toyota have locked in a manufacturing partnership, officially moving them past the “we’re just talking” phase. Toyota will manufacture AV-ready bZ4X vehicles — and integrate Waymo’s self-driving system — at its factory in Japan. The vehicles will be used for robotaxi services in Japan, the U.K., and other international markets. Importantly, the companies hinted that this could eventually lead to a licensing deal in which Waymo AV tech is in personally owned vehicles.
