Binance resumes withdrawals following $6.3 billion breach
2026-11-01

Binance resumes withdrawals following $6.3 billion breach

TechCrunch | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Cryptocurrency exchange Binance announced early on Sunday that it would begin allowing withdrawals in a phased process. The company froze all deposits and withdrawals after hackers stole $6.3 billion worth of crypto from Binance wallets. In its latest update, Binance said it has now covered 95% of the stolen assets, and it’s still working to buy the “remaining affected assets,” which are “less liquid in the market and take longer time to source at the required scale.” “Our commitment remains unchanged: Binance will bear the financial impact of the stolen funds,” the company added. “These losses will not be passed on to users.” As for the withdrawal freeze, Binance said it did not make the decision lightly, as it understands “how important reliable access to withdrawals is for our users.” Nonetheless, the company said this was a necessary “precaution while our teams worked to contain the incident loss, assess its impact, restore affected services, and complete security checks.” Binance will resume supporting withdrawals in phases, depending on network and asset type. As of Sunday morning, the company’s network status page showed a mix of normal, partially suspended, and fully suspended statuses — for example, Bitcoin withdrawals and deposits are still listed as partially suspended, while Ethereum deposits are normal and withdrawals are partially suspended. The company also said users should expect “temporary delays or longer processing times due to traffic volume.” “Resuming withdrawals is a major milestone, but our work continues,” Binance said. “We will continue strengthening our safeguards, completing the recovery process, and providing updates as progress continues.” Binance subsequently shared that between 6am and 3:30pm Coordinated Universal Time, it processed $4.6 billion in withdrawals and $1.0 billion in deposits, for a net outflow of $3.6 billion — 2.1% of its total assets under management. The company also said it will publish a more in-depth incident report when its investigation is complete. Binance previously blamed North Korea hackers for the breach. On Saturday, the FBI said that a North Korean group known as TraderTraitor was responsible, and that the group was converting the assets primarily into Ethereum, with further laundering likely. This post has been updated with information about net outflows during the initial withdrawal period. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Pixel 11 Pro Fold: Sturdy rectangle in a wide wide world
2026-11-01

Pixel 11 Pro Fold: Sturdy rectangle in a wide wide world

TechCrunch | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. It’s strange to write this review when the world is focused on the passport-sized iPhone Duo, and China-based companies are busy releasing wide foldables with a similar design. Google, however, stuck to its guns this year and produced a Pixel 11 Pro Fold, which is pretty much similar to last year’s foldable. The changes involve a lighter design, a brighter display, better wireless charging, and an updated camera sensor. The new olive color looks subtle but gorgeous, so that’s a bonus. However, with all the options out there, the device might not attract people who might be looking to buy their first foldable — unless they’re serious Pixel fans. Nonetheless, I have enjoyed using the device because the Pixel 11 Pro Fold’s cameras are competent enough, even if they aren’t a match for the Pixel 11 Pro’s setup, and the screen is nice to use despite the very visible crease. The Pixel 11 Pro Fold is 5mm thick when unfolded (vs 5.2mm of the Pixel 10 Pro Fold) and 10.1mm thick when folded (vs 10.8 mm of the Pixel 10 Pro Fold). This one is for the spec sheet because you might not feel as much difference. Despite this, the phone is not as thin as a device like the Honor V6 Magic. The difference you might notice — if you hold both this year’s and last year’s foldables — is that the new device is 19 grams lighter. But if you are buying a new device or switching from another foldable, it’s barely noticeable. The Pixel 10 Pro Fold was already a sturdy device. With this year’s version, the company claims to have increased durability by three times. Plus, there is a new glass-ceramic cover and a glass-fiber composite back cover to take up any impact. My Pixel 10 fell a few times from the table and from my pocket, but there wasn’t any visible dent or scratch. Well done, Google. One drawback of a strong hinge is that you can’t unfold the 11 Pro Fold with one hand, and that’s by design. The outer display is ever so slightly bigger, but the more observable change is the 3,600-nit display that is an upgrade from last year’s 3,000-nit peak display. This means that you can use the phone in bright conditions better. Google’s foldable still has a very visible crease in an age where phone makers like Oppo and Honor have managed to reduce the visibility significantly. The crease is more pronounced in bright light, given there is no reflective display on the device. On the back, there is the new HiLight indicator, which still lights up only for calls from your favourite contacts, and when you are talking with Gemini hands-free. There isn’t much to talk about this feature beyond that. Reading and watching videos on a foldable is still fun, though a wide foldable might show an edge on videos. The multitasking is good, but you can only open two apps side by side. Some apps that are tuned for foldable are great to use. For instance, Gmail, being a Google app, lets you see your inbox on one side and a particular email on the other. This can help you get through emails faster. However, Slack doesn’t have this split view, and it’s frustrating because we have been through many cycles of Android foldables with apps still not suited for the expanded screen. Given how quick Apple developer system moves, Android might feel it’s lagging in foldable experiences after the iPhone Duo is out. The camps are divided as to how apps look on Duo’s unfolded phone, but developers are rapidly updating their apps to fit the unfolded screen. The bubbles, which let you access apps in floating windows and minimize them in a taskbar, are a good idea. That way, you can temporarily look at information in an app without having to make it a full-sized window. Gemini updates this year were muted. The company built on its idea of Magic Cue to give timely suggestions in different apps. As I mentioned in my Pixel 11 Pro XL review, Rambler was my favorite feature to use in the new Pixel lineups. I could easily reply to emails and texts, and take notes, without involving texting on a wide keyboard. Foldables have mostly had inferior cameras to their candybar counterparts for years now. While this year is no different, Google continuing to use the “Pro” moniker for its foldable feels like a disservice. The Pixel 11 Pro Fold cameras are good, but they aren’t Pixel 11 Pro XL good. There are a few positives with the new version. There is an updated main sensor, still a 48-megapixel one, that lets more light in for photos with better detail. Google has also updated its models to support 30x Super Res Zoom, up from 20x Super Res Zoom. Even if Google wouldn’t include the same ultrawide and telephoto sensors as the slab Pro devices, I wish they would use the same main sensor on the Fold as the rectangle phone. The telephoto and ultrawide sensors are similar to last year’s device, so there is barely any improvement in images taken from them. In terms of software features, there is Looks, which can change the tone of an image with different presets, and Magic Capture, which can pick the best frames from a video — useful for when you have pets or kids. At $1,899, the Pixel 11 Pro Fold is for someone who likes Pixels and wants a foldable, given it’s barely $100 less than the new iPhone Duo and the same price as the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8, which are both wide-format phones. In the U.S., the taller Galaxy Z Fold 8 Ultra starts at $2,099. In Europe, companies like Xiaomi and Honor are going to release their foldables, too. So Google’s foldable is going to face a lot of competition. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. This doesn’t affect our editorial independence. Here’s what ‘renoa’ — the first new fiber in 25 years — feels like
2026-11-01

Here’s what ‘renoa’ — the first new fiber in 25 years — feels like

TechCrunch | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Buy One Founder Summit Ticket, Get a Second for Free. Register now. Founder Summit begins Nov. 4, 9:00 a.m. ET. Get your pass savings & deals before it starts. REGISTER HERE. Sometimes it seems like everyone — and every occasion — demands cashmere these days. Numerous brands sell cashmere sweaters, from Bruno Cucinelli’s $7,900 cable knit cardigan to Quince’s $50 crewneck. Menswear company Loro Piana has made cashmere a cornerstone of its lineup, helping to drive the LVMH division’s valuation above €11 billion. There are even cashmere running shirts. But as cashmere has spread throughout the fashion industry, it’s not for everyone. It’s still relatively expensive, hard to care for, and environmental fraught. The goats that produce cashmere have been trashing rangelands across Central Asia. That may change, though. I recently experienced an alternative fiber called renoa, developed by a startup called Everbloom, that’s washable and promises to be more affordable. It could bring the cashmere experience, via a substitute material, to more products and people. Renoa is made from sources of waste protein, including cashmere and wool scraps as well as down and feather “lint” leftover from the bedding industry. Everbloom processes the waste into a powder and blends it with a biodegradable biopolymer, and the combination is then extruded to the right length and diameter to mimic specific natural fibers. Renoa was recently approved as the first new fiber classification in 25 years by the U.S. Federal Trade Commission, which regulates fiber labeling in the U.S. Everbloom has been developing the new fiber for three years, and is currently in the process of scaling up. The startup developed its own AI model for material science, and a cashmere substitute is the first product. Last month, I stopped by Everbloom’s headquarters in New York City, which sits 15 floors above 5th Avenue, kitty corner from Bryant Park. There, co-founder and CEO Sim Gulati walked me through a range of products made with its company’s cashmere-like renoa, including a crewneck sweater, a pair of knit gloves, and a scarf. The sweater and gloves were soft and light, but the scarf was the most notable. It didn’t look like much, but it had been through the kind of hell and back that a pure cashmere scarf wouldn’t survive. It was a custom item that Everbloom had knit to test renoa, and Gulati had been wearing and washing it for months, including a trip through the tumble dryer before I saw it. It wasn’t pilled, and Gulati said it hadn’t shrunk, either. Everbloom is currently working with a range of companies to get renoa into consumer products. The first are likely to feature renoa as a blended fiber, making up around 20% of the material in a cashmere sweater, for example. The first product is expected to hit the market in the second quarter of 2027 Gulati said. The 80/20 cashmere-renoa blend that I felt was extremely close to the real thing, and Gulati said that additional finishing that accompanies commercial garments should eliminate any differences without compromising the washability and stability of the garment. After my visit, I kept thinking about various items in my closet that could benefit from those features. When renoa lands on shelves next year, I’ll be keeping an eye out for it. Top Trump Officials Are Hitting the Campaign Trail Ahead of the Midterms. Are They Allowed To?
2026-11-01

Top Trump Officials Are Hitting the Campaign Trail Ahead of the Midterms. Are They Allowed To?

TIME | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump and Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum look on as Secretary of Commerce Howard Lutnick speaks during a campaign rally at Curtis D. Menard Memorial Sports Center in Wasilla, Alaska, on October 13, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Top Trump Administration officials have taken to the campaign trail to boost Republican candidates ahead of the pivotal midterm elections as the party battles to retain control of Congress. Last month alone, Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, Federal Communications Commission Chair Brendan Carr, and Interior Secretary Doug Burgum appeared alongside President Donald Trump to support Republican Sen. Dan Sullivan in his tight reelection fight against Democrat Mary Peltola at a rally in Wasilla, Alaska, on Oct. 13. Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth appeared in Wisconsin alongside GOP Rep. Derrick Van Orden on Oct. 20 to speak to manufacturing workers during a Pentagon tour highlighting U.S. defense production. Last Monday, Vice President JD Vance and Burgum spoke at a rally in Topeka, Kansas. And on Tuesday, Secretary of State Marco Rubio briefly joined Trump on stage, at the President’s urging, during a rally in Corpus Christi, Texas. Cabinet members and other leading Trump officials have been wading into the midterms in these and other ways for months. In August, for instance, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy endorsed Republican Senate candidate Mike Rogers in Michigan. Legal experts and advocacy groups contend that in making such moves, members of the Administration have violated a decades-old law: the Hatch Act, passed by Congress in 1939, which places restrictions on certain political activities for federal executive branch employees. The Administration has argued that the officials have participated in political events in a strictly personal capacity, which is permitted under the Hatch Act, and that their actions have been cleared by lawyers and ethics officials. But several top Trump aides—including at least nine of his 15 Cabinet secretaries—have been hit with formal complaints that their actions have violated the law. “The number of Hatch Act violation complaints is extreme and abnormal for election time, especially for complaints against senior officials,” Kedric Payne, the head of ethics at the Campaign Legal Center, a legal non-profit that has filed a complaint against the Trump Administration for using taxpayer money to fund political ads, tells TIME. “We are seeing with the Trump Administration why the Hatch Act is so important,” Payne says, “because when it is not enforced quickly, it can be abused.” Read more: How Trump Could Interfere With the Midterms on Election Day, and the Days After Here’s what to know about the Hatch Act, and the accusations that Trump officials have violated it. The Hatch Act is an 87-year-old federal law that prohibits certain government employees from using their “official authority or influence for the purpose of interfering with or affecting the result of an election,” among other limits it imposes on officials’ political activity. It states that such interference applies to “an activity directed toward the success or failure of a political party” or candidate. The law was established to ensure that “federal programs are administered in a nonpartisan fashion, to protect federal employees from political coercion in the workplace, and to ensure that federal employees are advanced based on merit and not based on political affiliation,” according to the Office of Special Counsel (OSC), which enforces it. Read more: The Key Congressional Races to Watch on Election Night Many of the Hatch Act complaints against Trump officials on the campaign trail have centered around one specific aspect of the law that says federal employees cannot use or identify with their official title while engaging in political activity. In a handful of such complaints against members of the Administration, legal and press advocacy groups have pointed out that they were introduced at rallies by their Cabinet positions. “Political activity must be conducted purely in your personal capacity, without using your official title, without implying endorsement of the United States government. In your personal time at your own personal expense,” Richard Painter, the former chief White House ethics lawyer under George W. Bush and a professor at the University of Minnesota law school, tells TIME. The Trump Administration has pushed back on the accusations, saying that its members were acting in a personal capacity at the times in question. A number of complaints have been filed against the Trump Administration for violating the Hatch Act in recent months. Two advocacy non-profits, The Democracy Defenders Fund and Reporters Without Borders, filed a complaint in October over the rally in Alaska that featured Trump, Lutnick, Burgum, and Carr. The groups claimed that Carr “impermissibly mixed his official work into his personal remarks” and requested that the OSC open an investigation into each Trump official’s conduct at the rally. In September, the two advocacy groups also filed a complaint against Duffy for his appearance in Michigan and his endorsement of Rogers. Chief Counsel at the Democracy Defenders Fund said in a statement that Duffy’s conduct “crossed the legal and ethical line” and more broadly stated that it was “part of a broader pattern of this administration playing fast and loose with the law.” Spokespeople for the Interior and Transportation departments have denied wrongdoing on the part of their respective leaders to media outlets. The FCC and Commerce Department did not immediately respond to TIME’s request for comment. Read more: The 7 Key Governor Races to Watch in the Midterm Elections These complaints are among many more that have been filed over Administration officials’ participation in campaign activities. “We haven't seen since the first Trump Administration this number of complaints against officials, and I've lost track, but I do think it's well over 20 complaints just in recent weeks,” Payne says. In addition to complaints over officials’ appearances at campaign rallies, others have similarly alleged that Trump officials used their official titles in efforts to influence the midterms through social media and television network appearances. CREW, a nonprofit government watchdog organization, has made several such claims. In one complaint from earlier this month, it argued that White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller violated the Hatch Act during an interview on Fox News in which he spoke against specific Democratic candidates running for office and urged voters to support a “pro-American Trump agenda.” The group has also filed complaints against Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy, Jr. and Federal Bureau of Investigation Director Kash Patel for comments made while speaking to Fox. “These accusations are baseless and amount to a manufactured political attack," the Department of Health and Human Services said in a statement. "Secretary Kennedy takes his ethical obligations seriously, follows federal ethics laws, and consults HHS career ethics officials to ensure his activities comply with the standards governing public service.” The Department of Department of Justice, which houses the FBI, did not immediately respond to TIME’s request for comment regarding the accusations against Patel. When asked about the various Hatch Act complaints against Administration officials, White House spokeswoman Lauren Bis tells TIME that “it has long been established that senior administration officials are allowed to discuss current events, policies, and the candidates involved with those policies in order to advance the agenda of the administration.” Top Trump officials have additionally been accused of violating the Hatch Act through other means than direct statements and appearances in support of—or opposition to—midterm candidates that complaints allege may also affect the outcome of upcoming elections. Payne and his colleagues filed a complaint accusing several Administration officials of using taxpayer money for political ads which the complaint describes as “touting partisan and political rhetoric” to influence the outcome of the midterms. The complaint cites three ads: one that promotes Trump’s actions in Iran, another that decries socialism, and a third that features Trump speaking at Mount Rushmore. The Campaign Legal Center in its complaint quotes parameters set by the OSC that say the Hatch Act is designed “to prevent the use of federal power and taxpayer resources to shape electoral outcomes and to preserve public confidence in the nonpartisan administration of the law.” “Voters deserve to know that elected officials are campaigning using their own money and not taxpayer dollars,” Payne says. “Clearly these advertisements that were paid for by U.S. taxpayer dollars were intended to promote the Republican Party and influence the upcoming election.” Read More: Trump's Taxpayer-Funded Ads Draw Bipartisan Criticism The complaint specifically requests an investigation into Trump officials including Office of Management and Budget Director Russell Vought, Department of Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin, White House Chief of Staff Susie Wiles, and White House Appointments Secretary Natalie Harp for their involvement in putting together the ads. “There’s a statute that prohibits use of government funds for publicity or propaganda, unless expressly permitted by Congress,” Painter says. “Those ads that Trump was paying for with taxpayer dollars that said ‘paid for by the White House,’ that was propaganda. That violated that statute.” Trump has defended the ads by saying he was “only promoting the country,” rather than specific candidates. “That’s an ad for the spirit of our country,” he said when asked about the ads in the Oval Office in September. A Hatch Act complaint was also filed against the Administration by the Democracy Defenders Fund over the raid federal agents conducted at Democratic Rep. Ilhan Omar’s home in Minnesota in October. The raid was reportedly part of an ongoing Department of Justice investigation into potential immigration fraud. Omar has denied all wrongdoing. “The timing of this outrageous action against a long-time target of Trump’s evinces illegal political intent,” Norm Eisen, the co-founder of Democracy Defenders Fund, said in a statement. “Staging a public raid on the home of a sitting member of Congress weeks before a consequential election is an abuse of power.” Trump has maintained that he has “nothing to do” with the investigation, but has continued to levy public attacks against Omar, including calling her “crooked.” The flurry of recent complaints against the Trump Administration are far from the first instances in which the President and his aides have come under fire for alleged Hatch Act violations. An OSC report from 2021, for instance, found that in 2020, at least 13 senior Trump Administration officials violated the Hatch Act. Following an investigation by the OSC, disciplinary measures for Hatch Act violations are determined by the Merit Systems Protection Board (MSPB), an independent judicial agency within the Executive branch. For Cabinet or senior officials, OSC refers its findings to the President, who determines disciplinary action. “The potential of those consequences happening under this President are small because it is up to the President usually to hold members of the administration accountable when they violate the Hatch Act,” Payne says, noting that in some cases, officials may be required to only issue a public apology for their violation, or face no consequence at all. Federal officials who have previously been found to have violated the Hatch Act under Trump largely have not faced repercussions because the President either declined to punish them or because the OSC reported the violations after Trump and the officials in question left office. Read more: How Trump’s Approval Compares to Other Presidents Just Before the Midterms After one OSC report from 2018 found Kellyanne Conway, a former counselor to Trump, had violated the Hatch Act, for example, Trump declined to fire her, telling Fox & Friends at the time that “it looks to me like they’re trying to take away her right of free speech and that’s just not fair.” The OSC itself has meanwhile acknowledged that it was reporting other violations at a point when disciplinary action could not be taken. “Though discipline is no longer possible once subjects leave government service, OSC is issuing this report to fully document the violations, highlight the enforcement challenges that OSC confronted in investigating the violations, and to deter similar violations in the future,” one OSC report from 2021 reads. Federal employees can face several civil penalties if they are found to have violated the Hatch Act, however, including a fine, a suspension, or a letter of reprimand. For a criminal violation, up to three years in prison may be imposed as a penalty if an official is found to have “intimidate[ed], threaten[ed], command[ed], or coerce[ed] … any employee of the Federal Government … to engage in, or not to engage in, any political activity.” “If President Trump or anyone overreaches the point of trying to coerce someone in the government to engage in partisan conduct, that's a violation of the criminal provision of the act, but all the rest of it's civil,” Painter explains.
TIME
Why Doctors Are Last to Know What’s in the Drug Supply
2026-11-01

Why Doctors Are Last to Know What’s in the Drug Supply

TIME | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Drug tests used to detect fentanyl and xylazine in New York City on May 25, 2023. The illicit drug supply has changed rapidly over the last few months, with nitazenes, medetomidine, cychlorphine, and other novel synthetic substances appearing in rapid succession. Rapid tests for emerging substances are being developed and marketed with impressive speed, and these can return results within minutes. Law enforcement, medical examiners, employers, community organizations and even the general public can use them, if their states have decriminalized them. Physicians treating overdoses cannot. The contrast is hard to ignore: A test can be used to fire someone or support a criminal case, yet it  remains unavailable to the physician in an emergency room trying to save that person’s life, when minutes are essential. The divide is largely regulatory. Many tests for emerging drugs are labeled for forensic use only, and clinical tests for living patients require approval by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) as a diagnostic tool. As such, they cannot legally be used by medical clinics that adhere to federal law and funding policies. Tests used in patient care must meet FDA requirements for clinical diagnostics. Point-of-care testing—testing performed at or near the bedside—also requires an additional determination that the test is simple and low-risk enough to qualify for waived status under the Clinical Laboratory Improvement Amendments (CLIA). There is a legitimate reason for that regulatory distinction. Tests used to make medical decisions should be accurate, reliable, and interpreted appropriately. A rapid screen is not definitive toxicology, and it should not be treated as such. Fentanyl shows why speed matters. In early 2023, we pointed out that rapid fentanyl tests already existed outside clinical medicine, yet hospitals relied on expensive spectrometry testing that could take an hour or longer, if the equipment was available onsite at all. By the time FDA-cleared and CLIA-waived rapid fentanyl tests arrived, the illicit market had already changed again. Starting in 2020, xylazine spread through the fentanyl supply, introducing a new clinical hurdle: naloxone reverses opioids, but not xylazine. Patients can remain dangerously sedated or unstable even after naloxone is given. Further, xylazine is associated with necrotic skin wounds that increase risk of sepsis, and withdrawal syndromes that are more complex to treat than opioids alone. Not long after, medetomidine overtook xylazine supplies. It, too, does not react to naloxone, causes profound sedation and cardiovascular instability, and its withdrawal syndrome—which can come on just hours after someone stops using it—can trigger extreme hypertension and tachycardia that has been connected to heart attacks in younger people. Knowing these facts could significantly alter a patient’s treatment or discharge plan. There are no point-of-care tests for either of these drugs or other alarming and novel chemicals like cychlorphine, an opioid that does not show up on fentanyl tests. Because of that, clinicians have to follow information from law-enforcement seizures, public-health alerts, and months-old fatality data rather than from tests at the bedside. These data are in siloes and not easy to access or combine. The result is a system that can identify emerging drugs in seized samples and autopsies before clinicians can identify them in living patients. There is a better approach. The FDA should establish an expedited pathway for clinical tests targeting emerging drug threats, triggered when agencies such as the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) or the Office of National Drug Control Policy identify a substance of significant and growing public-health concern. The agency already has a dual pathway through which manufacturers can seek a special clearance and a CLIA waiver together. That framework could be prioritized and adapted for emerging drugs rather than forcing each new threat through the ordinary timeline. Federal agencies could also help fund independent validation. The economics of emerging drugs are unusual: a substance can appear quickly, spread unevenly, and be replaced by something else before a manufacturer can justify the cost of extensive clinical studies. Federal and state agencies could make up this gap if the threat of the new drug is significant. A small market does not mean a small public health or public safety need. None of this requires pretending a test strip is mass spectrometry. Point-of-care testing should be explicitly treated as presumptive. Positive results can be confirmed when necessary. Clinical care rarely rests on a single perfect source of information, anyway. Physicians already make decisions by combining an imperfect history, physical examination, vital signs, laboratory results, and response to treatment. A rapid drug screen would simply be another tool in that toolbox that helps dispel the fog of drug supply changes. Further, the value extends beyond the individual patient. Better hospital data could become an early-warning system rather than a retrospective record of a crisis already underway. Emergency departments are on the front lines of the overdose crisis; they should have access to the most innovative testing tools at the bedside, when minutes can make a difference. D.C. and Virginia Join the Fight to Block Trump’s Triumphal Arch. What Happens Next?
2026-10-31

D.C. and Virginia Join the Fight to Block Trump’s Triumphal Arch. What Happens Next?

TIME | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The site of U.S. President Donald Trump's planned triumphal arch is seen from the top of the Washington Monument in Washington, D.C., on Oct. 22, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Washington, D.C., and Virginia sued the National Park Service and Interior Department on Saturday in a last-minute bid to stop construction on President Donald Trump’s proposed 250-foot triumphal arch. The Trump Administration has said that construction could begin as early as next week. The new challenge joins two existing lawsuits against the arch, adding to a series of legal battles testing the limits of Trump’s authority to remake Washington. Calling the project “flagrantly unlawful,” the lawsuit points to the Commemorative Works Act, which requires Congressional authorization for memorials on federal land in Washington and its environs. The lawsuit also alleges violations of the Administrative Procedure Act, the National Environmental Policy Act, and the National Historic Preservation Act, arguing that the Administration failed to adequately assess the project’s effects and consult as required. Read More: How National Security Became Central to Trump’s D.C. Construction Projects “In their rush to break ground on the Arch, Defendants have made only half-hearted gestures at complying with these requirements,” the lawsuit filed Virginia Attorney General Jay Jones and D.C. Attorney General Brian L. Schwalb, claims. “Perhaps recognizing that the project is legally indefensible, Defendants have made clear that they will attempt to outrun judicial review.” “The Commonwealth has a solemn duty and a profound interest in preserving the dignity of this sacred place,” Jones said in a statement to the Washington Post. “We will vigorously litigate to protect its sanctity and honor the legacy of those who made the ultimate sacrifice.” The Administration argued in a June 17 filing for Lemmon v. Trump—a separate suit filed in February—that Congress already authorized construction at the site in 1925, when it approved plans for Arlington Memorial Bridge that included two monumental columns. Those columns were never built. In Lemmon, three Vietnam War veterans and an architectural historian argue that the arch would “block historically significant reciprocal views between” the Lincoln Memorial and Arlington National Cemetery. The plaintiffs have also argued that the plan requires congressional authorization. The Arlington County Board then filed its own suit Oct. 8 arguing that the construction would disrupt roads important to emergency services and “significantly and detrimentally” affect Arlington National Cemetery. In response to those suits, and defending the urgency of the project, Trump announced last month that he had agreed, at the Defense Department’s request, to incorporate a military complex into the planned arch, including facilities for drones and snipers. In sworn declarations filed Oct. 28, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth and Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin reiterated the claim that the arch is central to national security. “The arch is a generational national defense opportunity to mitigate these evolving threats,” Hegseth wrote in his declaration. The National Capital Planning Commission (NCPC), a federal review panel, is scheduled to consider final approval of the arch’s site and building plans on Nov. 5. The commission includes several Trump appointees, and its chairman has said he plans to support the project, according to the Washington Post. Interior must also issue its final authorization and publish the required notice. The Administration has told the court that those steps could be completed on Nov. 5, as well, allowing construction to begin that same day. The Justice Department told the court on Friday that construction could begin immediately once those steps were complete. U.S. District Judge Tanya Chutkan, however, seemed skeptical of the rush. “What’s the harm to the government to wait 3 or 4 months? What’s the rush to put this arch up?” the judge asked at the hearing for Lemmon v. Trump on Friday. Chutkan observed that the issue was complex. She said that she would consider the plaintiffs' standing over the weekend and rule expeditiously. TIME has reached out to the White House about the new lawsuit. The triumphal arch is just one of several projects that Trump has introduced in his second term to remake Washington in his image, including the demolition of the White House East Wing and construction of a White House ballroom, the planned 15-month closure and massive overhaul of the historic East Potomac golf course, and plans to restore and rename the Kennedy Center. In Friday’s hearing, Deputy Assistant Attorney General Robert Stander cited the Supreme Court’s 5-4 decision in August allowing ballroom construction to continue while litigation proceeded. The majority said the Administration was likely to prevail in arguing that the preservation group had not demonstrated the concrete harm needed to sue. Chutkan, however, said that the two legal battles remain distinct in nature. “The ballroom case differs in significant respects,” she said, pointing to traffic and public safety concerns from plaintiffs in the case. Chutkan also raised skepticism around the Administration’s justification of national security and pointed out how when Trump announced the arch, its purpose was initially posited as celebrating the country’s 250th anniversary. “This is the first time the government has proffered a national security aspect to this case,” she said. The arch dispute adds to a broader debate over the Administration’s frequent use of national security to justify its policies. Beyond defending the White House ballroom and triumphal arch on national security grounds, the Trump Administration has invoked national security to justify pausing offshore wind development, barring journalists from CNN, MS NOW, and Politico from the White House, and exempting oil and gas activities in the Gulf of Mexico from certain Endangered Species Act requirements. The golf-course litigation raises a related question about congressional authority: Plaintiffs there have also argued that federal law requires Congress’s express authorization to erect structures on federal public grounds in Washington. For now, the immediate question is whether a court will intervene before construction can begin as early as the coming week.

Americans Lost Nearly $18 Billion Last Year to Cyberscams. Is Washington Doing Enough to Help Them Get It Back?
2026-10-31

Americans Lost Nearly $18 Billion Last Year to Cyberscams. Is Washington Doing Enough to Help Them Get It Back?

TIME | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings An American flag flies in front of the U.S. Capitol building on Aug. 2, 2026, in Washington, D.C. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The United States government’s response to escalating cyberscams is fragmented, leaving victims with limited options to recover lost funds, according to an investigation conducted by Democrats on Congress’s Joint Economic Committee (JEC). The investigation, led by Sen. Maggie Hassan of New Hampshire and Rep. Don Beyer of Virginia, found that federal agencies do not have a consolidated or comprehensive record of how much money has been lost to cyberfraud, how much has been seized from overseas scam compounds, or how much has been returned to victims. The report, published Tuesday, also concludes that the government does not “incentivize” private sector actors—such as financial institutions and social media companies—to “proactively detect and address abuse of the services that they provide while also protecting user privacy.” Read More: AI Safety and Cybersecurity Should Be Allies. Instead, They're at Odds Online scam operations are not new, and neither is the knowledge that the issue is escalating. The FBI released an April report showing that Americans lost nearly $18 billion in 2025, amounting to more than $2 million an hour. Overall reported internet-crime losses—a broader category—reached $20.9 billion, up 26% from 2024 and roughly triple the 2021 total. While the problem grows worse, the response to these crimes has not scaled along with it. The Congressional investigation demonstrates that only a fraction of the money lost to scams is recovered. It also points to the fact that the government fails to fully report on its existing repatriation measures. The FBI told the committee that it returned $336 million to scam victims in 2025, and the Secret Service returned more than $770 million in seized assets to fraud victims in fiscal years 2024 and 2025—though that money had come from a range of fraud schemes, not just cyber threats. Suzanne Sando, a fraud analyst at Javelin Strategy & Research, points out that “fragmentation” in the reporting system—including the differing time periods and fraud categories demonstrated above—complicates the government’s ability to grasp the full extent of the problem. “Many of the groups and entities that scam victims report their losses to operate on disparate systems,” Sando says. “The fragmented nature of these reporting entities means there’s a lack of true accounting of losses—which also means that our grasp of overall scam losses is likely less than the truth of the situation.” The Congressional report calls on the federal government to enact new legislation to address the rising issue, including the ReportScams.Gov Act, a bipartisan bill co-sponsored by Hassan and Florida Republican Sen. Rick Scott “to mandate a unified federal strategy to combat scams and establish a central location for Americans to find information and assistance regarding scams.” A more coordinated reporting system could help researchers and advocates better assess the scale of the problem. Another benefit would be helping the public stay informed about potential schemes, allowing advocacy groups to develop preventive strategies and educational materials for at-risk populations, like senior citizens and minors. According to the FBI’s 2025 Internet Crime Report, Americans ages 60 and older reported losses of about $7.7 billion, a 60% increase from 2024. They reported the highest total losses of any age group, the report showed. Individuals under 18 reported nearly $13 million in losses across 13,168 internet-crime complaints in 2025, according to the same report. But the true extent of the losses is hard to estimate, experts say. Cyberscams are underreported because victims feel embarrassed, fear judgment, and believe recovery will be impossible. The last point is salient, because a stronger and more coordinated response—reaching some of the benchmarks outlined this week by members of the JEC—could give victims the confidence to report crimes in a timely manner, in turn making enforcement efforts more effective. The report calls for legislation that would “hold accountable” countries that “enable or permit” scam compound operations, and advance federal cooperation with private sector entities like the financial institutions and social media companies at the center of these scams. Experts note that this is a growing issue whose risk is only increasing as technology improves. In alignment with the Congressional assessment, they tell TIME that a strategic, coordinated, and well-resourced response will be necessary if the government hopes to better protect Americans and help them recover funds lost to scams. Data from the FBI and Congress show that transnational criminal organizations run massive compounds—and their reach is skyrocketing. According to Sando, the growing use of artificial intelligence has been partly responsible. Specifically, she calls out the prevalence of abuse on social media and the increasing sophistication of deepfakes—images, voices, or videos that make people appear to say or do things they did not. “Criminals use social media to build trust through ongoing conversations and relationships, and AI has become a major part of criminals’ ability to manipulate situations and exploit victims,” she says. “Through AI, criminals can create more convincing and polished messages, fake social media profiles, and deepfake voices and videos with less time and effort.” The manipulation tactics at the heart of these schemes have been consistent, Sando says, but criminals now have improved tools that allow them to quickly build credibility and “commit scams at scale.” Jake Braun, the executive director of the Cyber Policy Initiative at the University of Chicago, tells TIME that highly vulnerable populations are more exposed than ever before. “You've got these, you know, super at-risk populations who just weren't online before, and now they are, and so the bad guys are taking advantage of that,” he says. “The idea that a kid had an iPhone and was on Facebook just wasn’t a reality until a few years ago.” At the same time, technology gives individuals ready access to funding streams. “They've all got Apple Pay on their phones,” Braun points out. He explains that many of these scams are run by organized criminal networks operating in Southeast Asia, where scam compounds often use trafficked workers forced to defraud people online. It can be difficult to recover stolen funds because the issue is diffuse and widespread—spanning multiple countries, financial institutions, and criminal enterprises—and it is being addressed by a small group of law enforcement agencies that lack centralized coordination. Braun says investigations are complicated because they affect “millions of victims across dozens of countries” and include large numbers of cases with relatively small individual losses. “It's just incredibly complicated,” he says. “Nobody's robbing a bank and stealing $300 million. It's $1,000 bucks from somebody's grandma here, and it's $500 bucks [there], that collectively is billions and billions. That's a really hard problem to get after.” Braun, who also served in the White House with former President Joe Biden as the acting Principal Deputy National Cyber Director, says that there are just a hundred or so top law enforcement officials working on the issue. “So they're going to focus on big fish, and try and take down big rings, but that just means that they just don't have the bandwidth to go track down hundreds or thousands of dollars that are stolen from individual people because they're trying to take down groups that are collectively stealing billions,” he explains. When reporting investigations and recovery efforts are “siloed” into different agencies, there are two immediate consequences: The process is more confusing for the victim to navigate, and it makes it difficult for government entities to coordinate quickly on an investigation and take action. The U.S. State Department told those involved with the JEC investigation that it has 60 employees in the U.S. and Asia who dedicate significant time to fighting overseas scam compounds and said that it “has the tools it needs to combat the threat internationally.” TIME reached out to the State Department for comment. Sando says that within the private sector, there is “a lack of clear ownership and responsibility,” making it difficult to provide more protection. “Scams can span multiple platforms,” she says. “Preventing and detecting scams to protect consumers requires a coordinated group effort across multiple industries—including financial services, telcos, and social media.” In a perfect world, Sando continues, the government would coordinate these efforts and set clear standards for private institutions. She describes its ideal role as “facilitating relevant information-sharing, tracking consistent scam attempts and losses, [reporting] recovery rates for victims, and [adding] measures that are working to reduce the effectiveness of scams.” Social media companies, in particular, need to be incentivized to address “fraudulent ads and impersonation profiles/accounts,” she says. Braun agrees, saying that holding the companies accountable is one of the most tangible ways that governments could more aggressively address these scams before they ever happen. “They know everything about every click that's made on their platform,” Braun says. “This problem wouldn't exist if those platforms didn't exist.” Braun says meaningful discussions around prevention are often buried in conversations about how to address losses in the U.S. “We should really think through the [cyber] grants we give to state and local governments from FEMA, [and having them include] a component where they're doing education for these at-risk communities: seniors, minors, migrants,” Braun says. “We’re focused on going after the ‘bad guys,’ when maybe we need to be focused on thinking about: ‘How do you specifically protect minors in this space? How do you specifically protect senior citizens?’” He believes that these considerations must be part of any comprehensive solution to bring down the rate of cyberscams in the U.S. Once the government has a better understanding of how specific communities are impacted by these scams, he says, it can start to “tailor” solutions to help protect each vulnerable group.

Why You Shouldn't Toss Your Old Halloween Pumpkins In The Trash
2026-10-31

Why You Shouldn't Toss Your Old Halloween Pumpkins In The Trash

TIME | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Halloween pumpkin decoration by the Navy Pier in Chicago, United States, on October 19, 2022. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The only thing scarier than Halloween? Getting rid of a sad looking jack-o’-lantern when it's over. When confronted with a rotting, deflating carved pumpkin, your first instinct might be to beeline to the trash as quickly as possible. But experts caution against doing so—warning that trashing pumpkins, and thus sending them to landfill, can release harmful greenhouse gases into the atmosphere. “Everybody throws them out from Halloween to right after Thanksgiving,” says Kay McKeen, executive director at Scarce, an environmental education non-profit. “It's a huge burst of methane gas that's going into the landfill all at one time.” When pumpkins are sent to landfill, they release methane, a potent greenhouse gas that, over a 20 year period, is around 80 times more powerful at trapping heat in the atmosphere when compared to carbon dioxide. The lack of oxygen in landfills causes pumpkins to rot “anaerobically,” which releases methane. They are also full of water, and when they mix with waste and moisture it creates leachate, a toxic sludge that can pollute the environment. Though a single pumpkin might not do much damage, if you consider how many pumpkins are trashed each autumn, it adds up. “Maybe if we had a handful of pumpkins going into that waste stream, that might be one thing,” says Chris Enroth, horticulture educator with the University of of Illinois Extension, which organizes annual “Pumpkin Smash” events to help people recycle and compost old jack-o'-lanterns and pumpkins. “But we have millions of people who buy multiple pumpkins. We're talking about hundreds of millions of pumpkins that need to be disposed of post Halloween, and it's just a massive amount of organic material that goes into the landfill system.” Composting sidesteps many of the harms that come with sending food waste, like pumpkins, to landfill. “When we compost something, it's really a whole system that we're creating that introduces both water and oxygen, [and uses] microbes, bacteria, fungi, all the little decomposers,” says Enroth. “When we introduce those things, we get that aerobic composting process, so we don't produce methane, and we're able to turn those pumpkins back into organic matter that has nutrients and can be put back out into the garden.” What’s the best way to compost? If you have a curbside compost bin or designated compost for your apartment building, you can throw it there. If not, see if there are any drop-off composting sites near you. Some areas even organize dedicated events for residents to drop off old pumpkins. Since starting “pumpkin smashes” in 2014, Scarce and Illinois Extension have saved over 1,600 tons of pumpkins from landfill. Don’t leave your pumpkin out for animals to eat or dump it in a park—they can be harmful to wildlife. While pumpkins are generally safe for most wildlife to eat, some small animals, like hedgehogs, can become sick, different animal species congregating together could increase the risk of disease, and it can attract predators to residential areas. As you’re thinking about composting, keep in mind that it’s not just pumpkins that are compostable. Food is the single most common material sent to landfills in the U.S.—and wasted food is responsible for 58% of landfill methane emissions. If you’ve been thinking about starting your own home compost, now is the best time to do it, says Enroth. “We often recommend to folks thinking about starting a compost pile, to try it in the fall because we usually have leaves, grass clippings, things to get that pile started. And you could incorporate that pumpkin into that compost pile,” he says. “It's a really good combination of materials for you to get compost out of it next year.”

Why We Like to Be Scared
2026-10-31

Why We Like to Be Scared

TIME | 2026-10-31

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In a moment marked by economic anxiety, concerns over artificial intelligence, and geopolitical instability, frightening fiction is thriving. From the page to the big screen, horror has had a massive year. In publishing, the horror sub-genre is set to break new sales records. Readers aren’t the only ones seeking a good scare. Horror films dominated the silver screen this summer, with Backrooms and Obsession grossing more than $460 million domestically and roughly $900 million globally. Both films came from young directors who cut their teeth on YouTube, with their fingers on the pulse of this generation’s anxieties and fears. Even as major franchises like Spider-Man broke box office records, audiences turned two original horror stories into blockbuster hits. What is it about a horror story that allows it to latch its claws into our minds and pull us in? Why would we seek out an experience that produces in us a feeling of fear and anxiety? After all, we spend most of our lives trying to avoid these emotions. The truth is that humans love to be scared. We spend our time sharing and consuming scary stories; we take our kids to haunted houses; we dedicate an entire season to all things spooky. Understanding why we crave fear helps explain more than horror’s hold on Hollywood: It reveals how we confront uncertainty, test our limits, and find a sense of control when the world feels frightening enough already. The prevailing wisdom has long suggested that our desire for a good scare comes from the adrenaline kick we get while watching a scary movie. While this does explain some of the appeal, especially among younger consumers, research shows that many people who like horror movies aren’t necessarily looking for the rush it provides. Curiosity, specifically about situations that can cause injury or death, is perfectly normal and common. Morbid curiosity motivates us to learn about dangers, something that all animals can benefit from. Horror also offers a laboratory for self-learning. In my own work, I’ve found that a large portion of horror fans are “white knucklers”—people who don’t necessarily enjoy fear, but rather the opportunity to practice overcoming it. Many white knucklers report that they have learned something about themselves after a scary-play experience—for example, how they react in stressful situations, or that they should trust their instincts. Another group of horror fans seems to enjoy scary stories because they offer a safe opportunity to work through feelings of dread, anxiety, and other difficult thoughts. We called this group the “dark copers.” While this may sound odd at first, horror fans and morbidly curious individuals reported higher levels of psychological resilience during the early months of the COVID-19 pandemic, even when controlling for other personality traits and demographic factors. The logic begins to make sense once we work through what a horror story really is: a sandbox for a potentially dangerous situation. Many animals show some curiosity for things that might be dangerous. Zebras will cautiously observe lions as they lounge, and minnows will swim up to a pike to keep an eye on it. However, humans are the most morbidly curious creatures of all because of our ability to ask “What if?” In horror, the human capacity for imagination really shines. We concoct detailed stories of possible encounters with all sorts of beings, some of which we know well, and others that live only in the darkest recesses of our minds. We play out scenarios imagining what might happen if we, or someone like us, encountered a terrible situation. In real life, an encounter with danger is a costly way to learn. Our minds long for safe ways to learn about dangerous situations. Because of horror’s position as a sandbox for misfortune, consumers tend to reward horror films that reflect their generation’s anxieties. For instance, the unbelievably destructive ability of atomic bombs captured the imagination of those living in the late 1940s and 1950s. Anxiety about the future of this new atomic age spawned several radiation-borne monsters, the most famous of which was the Japanese film Godzilla (1954). Around the same time, paranoia was bubbling in the U.S. about surreptitious invaders, both alien and communist. Invasion of the Body Snatchers (1956) beautifully captured these anxieties in a story about your friends and loved ones being secretly replaced by a foreign, hostile entity. In the 21st century, “elevated horror” has taken hold, with films like The Babadook (2014) and Hereditary (2018) portraying anxieties about trauma and mental health. And then there are films like Get Out (2017) and Parasite (2019), which took on the tense social issues of race and class. While the 2026 hits Backrooms and Obsession may seem to touch on fairly unrelated topics, each has a foothold in the minds of modern consumers. I would argue that the rise in interest in horror as a genre can be partly attributed to rising social anxieties. Backrooms is a masterclass in how terrifying meaninglessness can be. The endless hallways and rooms in the film are reminiscent of social media, which offers an endless tunnel of content, with mental health concerns lurking under the surface. Each room contains some variation of objects that aren’t quite right, like an AI rendering of an image. The haphazard placement gives the entire space a creepy, purposeless feel. Meaninglessness is on the minds of this generation, too. A 2025 Gallup poll reports that 39% of Gen-Z adults feel like their life mostly lacks meaning, with slightly more saying they lack a sense of purpose. Mental health outlooks are similar, with a 2024 McKinsey survey reporting that nearly 1 in 3 Gen-Z respondents in the U.S. report poor mental health. Over 20% of Gen-Z and Millennials in that same survey report poor or very poor social health, a topic that is explored into absurdity in Obsession. The basic plot is an old monkey’s paw-style tale: a story about a magical item granting a wish with unintended consequences. Director Curry Barker infused his story with modern anxieties about dating and social life. The main character, Bear, is a socially awkward twentysomething who can’t bring himself to tell his crush how he really feels. In many ways, Bear’s situation resembles men of this generation more than previous generations. One survey found that 44% of Gen-Z men did not date at all as teens, compared to 32% of Millennials and 23% of Gen-X. This may be due to a lack of confidence. A 2026 study by the Wheatley Institute and the Institute for Family Studies found that only about 1 in 3 young men express confidence in their ability to approach a potential romantic partner. In my view, this resonance is why horror has had such a good run over the past two decades. According to film producer and data maven Stephen Follows, the horror genre has grown faster in the past few decades than any other genre. While the infamous slasher era of the 1980s saw around 150 horror films per year, the 2020s have surpassed 1,500 horror films per year. Even when the film genre was suffering in 2020, horror was having a moment. As COVID-19 emptied theaters, horror held on, pushing its share of the Box Office to the highest it had ever been. Horror beat its own record for Box Office share again in 2025. To be sure, scary stories have been with us as long as any other type of story, but we seem to be in a golden age of horror, and cultural institutions are taking note. From The Substance (2024) and Sinners (2025) to Bugonia (2025) and Frankenstein (2025), dark films with horror themes are now consistently receiving critical acclaim from the Academy. The world has always been full of things that frighten us. One way we work through these fears is by simulating scenarios. We tell stories of the things we fear and indulge in the bad outcomes that the protagonists face from the safety of our seats. Humans learn quite well from the mistakes of others, and horror stories trade in poor choices. In this way, horror offers us a uniquely powerful tool to process social concerns. As our technological world enters a new area of artificial intelligence, we will unlock a whole new world of possible dangers and things to fear, and films like M3GAN (2022) will be there to help us process worries. As we enter the unknown, we will try to prepare and cope with what comes our way in the same fashion as those before us: by diving into a good horror story. I don't particularly care what the cheeto-in-chief thinks about this topic, it is obviously far above this capability. The term AGI and ASI have been around longer than that and have suffered abuse in the fast few years. EVERYONE seems to forget that a human with an IQ of 80 is for certain a natural general intelligence. Talking about super-intelligence, as in smarter than humans, is a useful term. Thankfully, he co-opted a specific term that was stupid to begin with. He can have the term "super" intelligence, as far as I'm concerned. Elon wrote the forward in Superintelligence by Nick Bostrom, published in 2014. I wanted to point this out in fairness to the "Elon bends the knee" posts, which Elon contributed to the term "SI" way before Trump's political aspirations were even a twinkle in his 2019 "down the golden escalator" eye. Joe: For the last time, I'm pretty sure what's killing the crops is this Brawndo stuff.Secretary of State: But Brawndo's got what plants crave. It's got electrolytes.Attorney General: So wait a minute. What you're saying is that you want us to put water on the crops.Joe: Yes.Attorney General: Water. Like out the toilet?Joe: Well, I mean, it doesn't have to be out of the toilet, but, yeah, that's the idea.Secretary of State: But Brawndo's got what plants crave.Attorney General: It's got electrolytes. There's simply no reason to dignify this with a response. This is something the tech industry - at least the bits of it that are not sycophants - can simply ignore and not mention at all. Articles like this are great for clicks but Slashdot and other places could simply not post them and we can all just get on with the work and having real discussions instead of this nonsense. I Escaped the Doomscroll for a Month. I See More Clearly Now What Has Been Done to All of Us.
2026-11-01

I Escaped the Doomscroll for a Month. I See More Clearly Now What Has Been Done to All of Us.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. “Hegseth is going to livestream an execution by firing squad.” I received this message while gazing out at the Atlantic Ocean parked atop a cliff in the emptiness of northwest Scotland; the first message from the outside world I’d received in 23 days. I’d chosen a place to stay with no phone reception or internet, which meant that on the rare occasion I needed to do something like alter travel arrangements or learn how to tie a spinnerbait, I needed to drive to somewhere—somewhere like this cliff, where that message awaited me like an emotional roadside IED. I never stood a chance. Here’s the thing about spending a month off grid and overseas: Your body forgets how it feels to exist in America every day. Here, you are having more conversations with sheep (mostly about getting off the road) than people; you have recently discovered that your allegedly waterproof hiking boots are rotting from the inside; you have learned how to gut a trout and reliably build a fire. You are more relaxed than you have ever been, and you have forgotten the way nearly every news story of the past decade grabs hold of your heart and squeezes, twists, strangles. That constant, low-level dread. If you are reading this, you are someone who reads the news, and if you are someone reading the news, that hand is throttling your heart right now. On some level, you know this, but you are likely so used to it that you do not really know how hard it’s squeezing, and how much it saps your strength. This is not an article about how everyone should spend a month off grid in the most beautiful place they’ve ever been. Six months ago, my wildest dreams consisted entirely of paying off my credit card debt and eating beef once in a while; I am very familiar with the seething rage such articles provoke. They functionally shift blame onto the individual for failing to thrive in a society that makes thriving almost impossible. Are you stressed all the time? Wallowing, unproductive, depressed? Maybe you should do some self-care. Have you considered taking care of yourself, you slovenly fuck? Step up, take responsibility, spend a month getting lost in the silence and beauty of nowhere. If you really cared about yourself, you’d find a way to make it happen. Less avocado toast, maybe. If I ever write that article, please shoot me on sight. No jury would convict you. I come bearing the opposite message. There are reasons why so many people are struggling right now—reasons that cannot fully be avoided or mitigated. So many of our bad habits are survival mechanisms, ways to make it through one more day. Better to eat candy than starve to death. And while it’s good to replace that metaphorical candy with something healthier, we cannot flog ourselves into not needing something sweet to survive the bitter world we live in.After getting the message about Hegseth’s ghoulish plan, I closed Signal, made my travel arrangements with shaking hands, and pulled back onto the road with my heart jackhammering in my chest. It did not matter whether or not Pete Hegseth would follow through on his sadism—the administration was once again laying groundwork for future cruelty. I suspected the administration would back down—as indeed it did. But six months from now, they’ll float the possibility again. And this time it won’t be new, and it won’t be unthinkable. And we are always, always thinking. Not only do we carry the horrors of the universe in our pockets, we spend most of our waking hours gazing into that abyss in the name of staying informed. We’ve come to see it as an end in itself: like, share, retweet. It works very well for the people who own the platforms. You are always about to make a difference, which means you can never log off—not when a West Wing–style rhetorical victory over the forces of darkness feels so close. Meanwhile, those forces perpetrate fresh atrocities that require fresh awareness, and so the cycle never stops: action and reaction, forever. “We create our own reality. And while you’re studying that reality—judiciously, as you will—we’ll act again,” an unnamed adviser told journalist Ron Suskind in 2004. “We’re history’s actors … and you, all of you, will be left to just study what we do.” I went to Scotland to try to break that cycle, for no one is more guilty of ouroboros behavior than the Very Online political journalist. I have started to feel like a sports commentator for the apocalypse—not all the time, but way too much. I pretend my chronic social media use is “for work,” and to some extent that’s true. It’s also an excuse to avoid work. Why write an entire article when I can dash off a quick thread and gain hundreds of likes? And then there’s the Skinner box effect of the internet generally, in which we keep pressing the button to generate irregular rewards, be it something truly hilarious or that adrenaline shot to the heart that comes with a terrible take or the horror of the day. I needed a detox, and I was lucky enough to be able to take one. So I packed my things and went somewhere I hadn’t been for decades: a place where the internet wasn’t. For the first week I spent in Scotland, I slept 10 hours a night. The number slowly dropped, then settled out at eight. I was well-rested for the first time in my adult life, and I felt physically good to be alive in ways I had not thought possible. It is difficult to adequately convey the difference between experiencing 16 wakeful hours per day and white-knuckling your way through a brutal 20. Suddenly, I had energy for things that actually make me happy: reading, hiking, creative writing. Replacing my anesthetizing habits took no discipline at all. The best minds of our generation—or at least, the most sociopathic ones—are spending all their time creating vicious, carefully crafted cycles to keep you activated and staring at as many screens as possible from the moment you wake up to the instant you fall asleep, and maximizing time between the two. You wake up worried about the world—what fresh horrors have transpired overnight? You reach for your phone and doomscroll, which makes you more anxious, which makes you doomscroll, and shit, you’re late for work: no time to hit the gym, even though you swore you would. You stumble through your work in a daze and finish your day exhausted: too tired to do anything fun but not too tired to doomscroll or watch short-form videos or chase the methamphetamine high of some terrible take online. Helplessness melts into righteous rage as you type some scathing retort, as loneliness gives way to the metallic taste of parasocial satisfaction as the likes and reposts flood in. Oh shit, it’s 2 a.m. Whatever. You’ll hit the gym tomorrow, you tell yourself, knowing it’s a lie. By you, I mean me. And maybe you. Certainly most of the people I know. Five days after I received the message about Pete Heseth, I returned to civilization to find that very little had changed in my absence. Our trajectory remained unaltered, we’d simply continued to travel in that direction. Donald Trump had reluctantly reversed course on Hegseth’s snuff film, but he continues to threaten election integrity in all the ways one might expect. A failed press secretary provided great amusement for a week or so. The brave people of Ukraine continued to endure murderous barrages of Russian drones while Elon Musk sabotaged their ability to fight back, and Trump agreed to buy diesel from his old friend Putin. Unspeakable horrors in Gaza continue. All of these things are horrific. Not a single one is surprising. I am not hopelessly behind after 28 days away; things are either exactly the same or worse. I am returning to a world carefully crafted to drive me insane but armed with knowledge I lacked when I left. There is a difference between burying your head in the sand and periodically reminding ourselves that the world can be beautiful and good. We have somehow become convinced that turning away even for a second is a betrayal of the most vulnerable amongst us, but constant activation paralyzes us with dread and leaves us too exhausted to take action. Hyperawareness is not a virtue, it’s a symptom of mental illness—ask your complex post-traumatic stress disorder friends about it sometime. We are not betraying the cause by turning off our phones for a few hours; we are not lazy pieces of shit who deserve to be poor when we opt for sleep over pseudoproductivity. Not everyone can fuck off to Scotland for a month because they’re “very sad” and “spent the last four months watching a loved one die horribly of cancer,” but everyone can take an awareness break once in a while. If a tree falls in a forest and you miss a post about it, it still makes a sound. It will still be on the ground in a few hours, or even a few days. You could not have stopped it falling by watching it on livestream, and you are not betraying your fellow humans by taking care of yourself instead of tree-watching 24/7. All of this is easier said than done. So many things are working against us: the obscene cruelty of our broken political system, a tech ecosystem that profits off making us perpetually aware of that cruelty, an omnipresent self-help industry that tells us that our resulting dysfunction stems from a weakness of will. Less than two weeks back in the country I am already sleep-deprived and stressed to the gills. But I have two rest days planned for next week, and I’m not upset at myself for falling into the old ways once in a while. The day I learned about Hegseth’s livestreamed execution fantasy, I’d planned to go to Durness, a lovely seaside town with charming historical caves and a gorgeous peninsula hike. Instead, I found myself driving down a bumpy single-track dirt road to Shegra, a tiny village that consists of perhaps 10 crofter’s huts, a cemetery, and (according to my guidebook) a trailhead that leads to a beach known for shipwrecks and ghosts. Dark clouds roiled above the empty moor. I felt dark and empty too. As so often happens in Scotland, the alleged trail immediately vanished into a vast stretch of bog with no obvious way forward. Two ravens circled overhead, then flew northwest, and on a whim I followed them. An hour later, I found myself on a sheer cliff face overlooking the roiling Atlantic, which I followed until I came across a promontory fenced off with an ancient stone wall, behind which a herd of sheep peacefully grazed. How long ago was this ancient stone erected? Whose sheep are these? Scotland offers very few answers, but the questions tend to be wonderful. I never did find that beach, but I wandered those ancient cliffs for hours, which were mountains as tall as the Himalayas until time and glaciers carved them down, forests until the Brits took the timber for their navy, farmland until the aristocracy expelled the crofters by force and replaced them with sheep. A few thousand years from now, it will be something else. With its rocky soil and lack of mineral wealth, it is currently the most powerful thing you can be in today’s climate: unexploitable. Scotland is nothing like heaven, but exactly like Valhalla. By the time I made it back to my car, I felt ready to fight again. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
