The Verge
HomePod Mini 2 first look: Three things I love and one disappointment
2026-11-01

HomePod Mini 2 first look: Three things I love and one disappointment

The Verge | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
The new Mini is faster, quieter, and gorgeous in green, but its upgraded Mac speaker feature didn’t work for me. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. When I first heard about the new $149 HomePod Mini 2 at Apple’s Welcome Home event last month, I was disappointed. It’s been six years since Apple launched its entry-level smart speaker, and all it got in the second gen was a chip bump, some color glowy stuff, and better AirPlay? For $50 more than the original, which launched at $99, Apple didn’t even upgrade the sound system. It felt like the company phoned this one in. Now that I’ve got my hands on a couple and set them up in my home office, I’m more enthused. The Mini 2 is so much faster than the first-gen Mini, playing music in a few seconds and responding to smart home commands just as quickly. If you’re fed up with standing over your HomePod waiting for it to finally do what you asked, then that alone is worth the upgrade. Here are my three favorite features of the new Mini 2 after spending a couple days with it, and my one big disappointment. The biggest upgrade for the Mini 2 is Siri AI, but that isn’t available yet. Apple says it’s coming “later” in a software update. However, the basic smart voice assistant has definitely improved. Siri on the Mini 2 is much snappier than on the first-gen Mini, regularly responding in under three seconds. This is thanks to a newer, faster processor (the S10, up from the S5) and Wi-Fi 6E support (up from Wi-Fi 4). When I asked Siri to play Taylor Swift, a song started in a couple of seconds. It correctly told me who won this year’s World Series in under two seconds, and turned off the light or opened the shades in under three seconds. That’s a huge improvement over the up to 10 seconds I often have to wait for Siri to get things done on the first-gen Mini. However, asking something harder, like who will win the US midterm elections, still brings up the familiar offer to look at some web results on my iPhone. As I said, Siri AI isn’t here yet. The other new behavior I am really enjoying is that when I ask Siri to perform a smart home action, such as open the shades or turn on the lights, instead of “OK, Jen,” or “One sec,” followed by a wordy confirmation of the action that I often get on the first-gen Mini, the Mini 2 just does it and doesn’t talk back. It’s not silent for every action, but it’s quiet enough to be a noticeable improvement. The green color is divine. This is the prettiest small smart speaker I’ve seen — and I’ve tried them all. Yes, Amazon’s Echo Dot Max’s purple is fun, the Google Home Speaker’s pale green is elegant, but Apple’s deep green is seriously sophisticated. It’s a color that fits in really well in various places around my home, and I’m so glad the company finally released a green option. It’s a big improvement over the slightly garish yellow and orange of the prior-gen Minis and the best of the new colors. I saw all the colors in person at Apple’s Welcome Home event, and while the pink is pretty, it’s very pale, and the blue is functionally identical to the first-gen blue, maybe a shade lighter. While a lot of folks will be happy with black (don’t get white unless you plan to dust it regularly), the green is my new go-to. Two green HomePod Mini 2s look so good on the walnut Nanoleaf monitor stand I’m testing; it’s like my office got an instant upgrade. And while Apple didn’t update the speakers in the Mini 2, it’s still a very good speaker for its size, doing a fine job with Bad Bunny’s bass and delivering clear mids and vocals. While $300 is steep for computer speakers, two as a stereo pair in my office definitely delivered on room-filling sound. A few weeks ago I fumbled for my phone on my nightstand and accidentally hit the top of my HomePod Mini, blaring music in the bedroom at 3AM. My husband was not happy. A new setting in the Home app now lets you lock the Mini’s top controls. This goes for all HomePods, old and new, and it’s a major quality-of-life upgrade. How often do you intentionally touch the top of your HomePod? I bet far less often than accidentally! The other new trick for the top of the HomePod is Sound Glow. This makes the LEDs display colors that match the album art of the song you’re playing (it’s also backward compatible with the first-gen Mini and the second-gen HomePod). It’s neat, but only works with Apple Music and Podcasts and is so subtle that I don’t feel it adds much to my music-listening experience. Beyond the speed improvement, the other new feature I was excited about is the HomePod Mini 2’s ability to be the default speaker for your Mac using what Apple calls “a responsive and persistent AirPlay connection.” This creates a wireless point-to-point connection between the computer and the HomePod. You can pair a single speaker or a stereo pair to your Mac, and the feature also works with the second-gen HomePod. (Two or four HomePod Mini 2s can also now be part of a surround sound setup using the new Apple TV 4K.) Disappointingly, I couldn’t get the Mac speaker set up to work. I have the required hardware: a Mac that supports Wi-Fi 6E, running the public release of macOS 27.2 (a Mac Mini M4), and HomePods on HomePod Software 27.2. But the Sound pane in my Mac’s settings didn’t offer an option to set a HomePod via AirPlay as the default sound output. The Mini showed up and connected like any other AirPlay speaker, with the same flaws as regular AirPlay — occasional artifacting, drop-offs, and some lag with notification sounds — along with the Mac defaulting to its built-in speakers when the computer restarts, or anytime I activate Siri on the HomePod. I also tried with a stereo pair of Minis, but they also played over regular AirPlay. The chip bump and speed improvements make this a bigger upgrade than it initially seemed According to a 9to5Mac’s hands-on, when the persistent AirPlay is enabled, the HomePod Mini should show up as a default system output device in the System Information, and there should be an option to enable it under “Play sound effects through” in the Sound pane in System Settings, but neither was there for me. I have seen other reports of the same issue. I tried resetting both HomePods completely after they updated to 27.2 out of the box (I was on the HomePod Software betas, but 27.2 is now in general release), and the feature still isn’t working. I reached out to Apple, but the company hasn’t responded to my requests yet; I’ll update this article if I hear back or find a solution. I’ve only been testing the Mini 2 for two days, and while the real test will come when it gets its Siri AI upgrade, the chip bump and speed improvements make this a bigger upgrade than it initially seemed. Apple does need to work on the Mac pairing feature; it has a lot of potential, but based on my struggles here, it feels unfinished. I plan to do a full review once Siri AI launches, and of course compare it to Apple’s big new smart home release: the upcoming $299 Apple HomeView smart display. It is also a Home hub, but the screen gives it more functionality. At $149, though, the Mini 2 is now the cheapest way to get an Apple Home hub with Thread and Matter support. MIT Technology Review
Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.
2026-10-30

Eating disorders are on the rise. GLP-1s could make things worse.

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-30

Visit Original Website
GLP-1 weight-loss drugs are one of the biggest biotech breakthroughs of the last decade. But to some clinical psychologists, they can represent something more sinister—a potential trigger for eating disorders. A new report suggests that the global incidence of anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa shot up between 1990 and 2023—by a whopping 71%. By 2023, there were an estimated 18.1 million people living with one of these two disorders. What’s worse is that the number is likely to be much higher than that once we factor in other eating disorders. And, disturbingly, those in the field are already starting to see GLP-1 weight-loss drugs exacerbating the issue. Cheri Levinson is a clinical psychologist at the University of Louisville in Kentucky who has specialized in eating disorders for almost 20 years. She says she’s now seeing more patients with more severe symptoms than at any other time in her career, and that many of them are misusing GLP-1s. “This is the worst I’ve seen things,” she says. Eating disorders are mental health conditions in which people’s eating behaviors impact their health. They include anorexia nervosa (defined by a fear of weight gain), bulimia nervosa (in which people binge and purge), binge eating disorder, and others. It’s thought that some people have a genetic predisposition to certain eating disorders, but that some external factor is what triggers their onset. That might be stress, or social pressure to be skinny. Often, simply restricting food intake can trigger an eating disorder, says Levinson. Eating disorders—along with the pressure to look thin—were already prevalent in the 1990s (the heroin chic era springs to mind). To find out how things might have changed since then, Ruhai Bai at the Children’s Hospital of Nanjing Medical University in China and his colleagues pulled together data from studies and surveys conducted on populations around the world. The team used statistical models to fill in any data gaps. That research allowed the researchers to estimate the annual incidence of two eating disorders—anorexia nervosa and bulimia nervosa—for each of 204 countries and territories between 1990 and 2023. They also estimated the impact those disorders had on global health by calculating their disability-adjusted life years (one DALY represents a year of healthy life lost). The findings, published in JAMA Psychiatry on Wednesday, are unsettling. Between 1990 and 2023, there was a 71% increase in the incidence of those two eating disorders, the team found. Those conditions had a huge impact on global health, too. In 2023, they “cost the world the equivalent of about 3.83 million years of healthy life,” says Bai. Bai and his colleagues expect these figures to continue to rise. In 2023, an estimated 18.1 million people had either anorexia nervosa or bulimia nervosa; by 2050, the team predict, the two conditions could affect 21.8 million people. It gets worse. Those two conditions represent less than half of all eating disorders, says Levinson, who was not involved in Bai’s study. The true impact of these disorders is “probably three times the rate that they’re reporting,” she says. Bai and his colleagues didn’t explore the reasons for this rise (although Bai points out that it can’t be entirely explained by population growth). So how did we get here? Social media may have played a role. “Social media comparisons, influencers, challenges, diet trends can all be precipitating … events for an eating disorder,” says Cynthia Bulik, a clinical psychologist at the University of North Carolina, who was not involved in the new study. Bulik has been studying and treating eating disorders since the early 1980s. “Between 1990 and 2023 there has clearly been an upsurge of those types of environmental exposures,” she says. Many social media users are exposed to filtered and modified images of skinny influencers who may have undergone cosmetic surgery or extreme dieting and who are promoting “thinspiration” or “fitspiration” content. But users with eating disorders appear to be more likely to engage with this content—and to be fed more of it as a result. When researchers at the University of Melbourne investigated this phenomenon a couple of years ago, they found that the algorithms of TikTok users with eating disorders delivered 4343% more “toxic eating disorder videos” than those of users who didn’t have the disorders. They also got more content focused on dieting or exercising. Many were fed over 1000 problematic videos per month. Bulik describes social media as “quicksand” for people with, or at risk of, eating disorders. The covid-19 pandemic—and the social isolation that accompanied lockdowns—also appears to have contributed to the rise in eating disorders. Research conducted in 2021 found that the number of people hospitalized with eating disorders in the US doubled between 2018 and 2020. Researchers don’t yet have good data on how trends in eating disorders might have progressed since 2023. But anecdotally, things seem to be getting even worse. “There are more eating disorders, more severe eating disorders… and we’re seeing earlier ages of onset,” says Levinson. “We’re seeing eight- and nine-year-olds with eating disorders.” “From what I’m hearing and seeing, I am not going to be surprised if that number isn’t at least doubling again,” she adds. Levinson particularly worries about the impact of GLP-1 weight-loss drugs, which have exploded in popularity in recent years. A Gallup survey conducted earlier this year suggested that 15% of Americans had used one of these drugs for weight loss. And they’re becoming more accessible—drug companies are working on new formulations that are both easier to take and more effective, and many people can buy versions of these drugs from compound pharmacies or dubious online sources. In a small study published earlier this year, Levinson and her colleagues found that 30% of people with eating disorders had used GLP-1 drugs. The drugs might benefit some of those people. But 10% appeared to have misused the drugs. And Levinson thinks that figure is also an underestimate. Levinson says she and her colleagues have seen children develop eating disorders after being prescribed GLP-1s for medical reasons. They’ve seen others—even children as young as 11—buy versions of these drugs over TikTok. “We’re very concerned that GLPs will lead to even faster and more extreme weight loss in people with anorexia nervosa, and that patients will come to us in even more advanced stages of illness that become difficult to reverse,” says Bulik. Both Bulik and Levinson are continuing to study the impacts of these drugs in eating disorders. In the meantime, both urge medical professionals to consider the risks GLP-1s might pose to people with those disorders. Bulik contributed to a set of research and clinical guidelines, published earlier this month, that recommends doctors screen for eating disorders before prescribing the drugs, for example. The guidelines also recommend more patient education and more robust monitoring of people who take GLP-1s. If you think you or someone you know might have an eating disorder, you can find information and support at the National Eating Disorders Association. The Download: AI agent tracking and climate feedback loops

The Download: AI agent tracking and climate feedback loops

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This is today's edition of The Download, our weekday newsletter that provides a daily dose of what's going on in the world of technology. —Madhulika Srikumar and Claire Leibowicz from the Partnership on AI, a nonprofit advancing best practices in AI governance. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, from hacking websites to accessing private government files. In many cases, they’ve eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent potentially devastating consequences. And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability. ​​In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. Because right now, we’re effectively flying blind when deploying these tools. Climate change itself increasingly fuels climate change. Rising greenhouse gas concentrations are driving up global temperatures, creating dangerous feedback loops that aren’t fully represented in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming. Now, a growing number of observations and studies are indicating how powerful these cascading effects could be. By extension, they’re also showing how much faster the planet may warm this century. Read more about the dangerous feedback loops accelerating global warming. This article is from The Spark, our weekly climate tech newsletter. Sign up to receive it in your inbox every Wednesday. Enter the solid-state battery, which swaps liquid for a solid ceramic or polymer. The technology promises faster charging, longer life, greater energy density and improved safety, and a new generation of companies aims to bring it to market. If their bets pan out, the tech could ease the shift away from fossil fuels. Find out how close solid-state batteries are to delivering on their promise. MIT Technology Review
We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time
2026-10-29

We urgently need to monitor AI agents’ actions in real time

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
This summer, hundreds of OpenAI agents tried to cheat on a cybersecurity test. They broke out of their test environment and successfully hacked Hugging Face, a site that they believed held clues about how they’d be graded. Around the same time, another OpenAI agent accessed private files from an Australian government website. It’s a problem that’s by no means confined to OpenAI. In the months since, Anthropic, Google, and Meta have also disclosed incidents in which their agents have gone off track. And worryingly, in many cases the agents eluded the very researchers and safety teams tasked with monitoring them. AI agents are taking unauthorized actions, and the companies building and running them are mostly relying on luck and hindsight to mitigate any harmful impacts. They must do more to ensure safety. That’s why EU regulators and US agencies are rightfully calling for agent developers and deployers to monitor agents in real time. But these rules assume that companies can see what their agents are doing and why—when in reality, they lack sufficient visibility. As agents roll out across critical sectors, from finance to government to medicine, we must be able to monitor them in real time to prevent wide-ranging and potentially devastating consequences.  And while safety depends on monitoring, monitoring requires observability: the capacity to capture what the system did, process it into signals, and present those to humans or automated oversight systems. In other words, we need to know what the agent did and why it did it. And as our recent research shows, current agent frameworks—the software that runs agents— do not reliably generate the crucial observability records we need to ensure that agents are deployed safely. Agents present new kinds of problems that generative AI alone does not. They inherit the unpredictability of the LLMs they are built on, but where a generative AI system produces a single output for a human to act on, an agent performs longer tasks—it plans, picks tools, spawns sub-agents, and takes actions. So an agent’s course can take shape in a multitude of different ways, and no tests before launch can fully anticipate the risks. This makes observing the agents while they work even more challenging, and even more important. With colleagues from academia, civil society, and industry, we studied the four most widely used agent frameworks to see what signals they actually emit. We found major gaps that made it hard to achieve key safety goals, like noticing policy violations, unauthorized actions, and even human oversight failures. Key observability requirements were missing—like Agent IDs that persist over time, and records about which humans approved agent tasks. This made it hard to tell which agents did what or assess whether they were taking unauthorized actions. The good news is that these gaps, once identified, can be closed. Doing so starts with capturing two types of records: those that reveal what happened in an agent’s run and how the agent made its decisions. The “what happened” records identify the agent (while preserving privacy) and log how humans intervened and whether agent permissions or “memory” shifted, while the “decision” records show an agent’s reasoning and levels of confidence in the actions it’s taken. Take, for example, agents working to process a bank’s mortgage loans. The system examines an application and recommends denying it, and then a human loan officer signs off on the decision. In a later audit, or perhaps even in real time, a bank or regulator needs to be able to reconstruct which agent made the call, which officer approved it, and how long they took. And the bank must also analyze records of how the agent arrived at its refusal decision in order to ensure it did not use off-limits information, like details about the neighborhood the applicant is from. Recording “what happened” and “how the agent decided” require different approaches, but both are feasible. The current lack of “what happened” records is mostly a software plumbing problem that can be addressed through OpenTelemetry, an open observability standard maintained by engineers across the industry and by the agent frameworks adopting it. OpenTelemetry has launched a working group drafting shared formats for tracing agents, but they have yet to be finalized. The group should urgently incorporate standards for maintaining persistent agent identity and for recording human interventions and changes to agents’ permission or memory. And it should be done before these systems can scale, all the while attending to the privacy implications of agent ID that links to real people. Preserving “how agents decided” records is more challenging. The issue raises open research questions for model providers and researchers: Does the step-by-step reasoning a model displays, known as chain of thought, reflect how the model actually made a decision? And do we have reliable ways to measure how confident the agent is in that decision? These questions need answers before anyone sets standards for how such observability records are used. No single organization or sector can close the observability gaps on their own, but together, they can support the infrastructure required to prevent dangerous incidents and support safety. AI developers building agent frameworks, like OpenAI, Anthropic, CrewAI, and LangChain, must integrate telemetry signals recording an agent’s persistent identity, any human interventions, and any changes to an agent’s permissions and memory. Meanwhile, enterprises adopting these agent frameworks have an influential role to play; enterprises in regulated industries, like banks and hospitals, can put pressure on the agent developers to supply these missing observability records where standards do not yet exist. Enterprises must also define policies, permissions, and roles that set out what agents can safely do, since telemetry alone cannot assess whether agents broke specific rules. Finally, regulators and AI standards institutes must specify the observability records required for high-stakes AI deployments while leaving the technical details to industry. We have already seen initial momentum here: NIST’s agent standards initiative in the US is working on agent identity, one of the most salient gaps we found in our research. No bank would let a thousand unbadged employees loose on customer accounts and hope to piece together what happened when something goes wrong. Yet that’s how we’re deploying AI agents. Making their behavior observable is the first step toward fixing this. And by building the regulatory and policy ecosystem that makes it possible to act on what we see, we can hold organizations accountable. Until then, every task we hand to an agent hands over our agency, too. Madhulika Srikumar is the Head of the AI Safety Program at the Partnership on AI, a global non-profit partnership of leading industry, academic, and civil society organizations, advancing best practices in AI governance. Claire Leibowicz is the Director of AI, Trust, and Society at the Partnership on AI and a doctoral candidate at Oxford University. MIT Technology Review
Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot
2026-10-29

Our climate models have a big, dangerous blind spot

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-29

Visit Original Website
Climate change itself is increasingly fueling climate change. Rising concentrations of greenhouse gases are driving up global temperatures, setting off dangerous feedback loops that we have not fully accounted for in our climate models—or our emissions-reduction goals. Scientists have long warned that warming would propel further warming in various ways, but a growing number of observations and studies are at last providing a sharper sense of just how powerful these cascading effects could be. And, by extension, they’re showing how much faster the planet may warm in this century. A study in Environmental Research Letters in September, synthesizing the results of previous papers, found that the additional carbon dioxide and methane released from thawing permafrost, intensifying wildfires, hotter lakes and reservoirs, and wetter, warmer wetlands alone could drive global average temperatures up 0.2 °C to 0.4 °C this century. That would come on top of the 2.6°C of warming above preindustrial levels that modelers are already expecting through 2100. The additional heat could subject many of the world’s cities to monthslong heat waves and expose hundreds of millions of people to flooding. None of the models that inform the major UN climate reports, which assess how much the planet will likely warm under various emissions scenarios, account for all these feedback effects. Many of them don’t incorporate any. That leaves a huge blind spot in our understanding of how much the world could actually warm in the coming decades—and how rapidly nations will need to slash emissions to stay within critical boundaries. A separate analysis published in One Earth in March, which examined what the feedback problem means for our international climate goals, concluded that the world may be much further off track than is widely believed. It found that just three regional sources of warming-driven emissions—thawing permafrost, increasing fires in boreal forests, and warming tropical wetlands—may shorten by as much as 25% the time before the planet consistently breaches the 1.5 °C and 2 °C warming thresholds. Our climate models and the UN’s reports clearly need to be updated to better represent these effects. Nations must also step up their efforts to reduce emissions to reflect this improving understanding of the dangers we face, says Sam Abernethy, the lead author of the paper and a climate scientist at Spark Climate Solutions, a San Francisco–based nonprofit research group. At least some of this is starting to happen. Spark kick-started an initiative, called the Warming-Induced Greenhouse Gas Emissions Model Intercomparison Project, through which more than 20 international teams are running the same set of analyses on different models to develop greater understanding of feedback effects’ potential power. Researchers involved hope that the UN will incorporate those results into its next major climate assessment, due out in 2029. That report will update estimates of the remaining emissions that can be released before the world crosses key temperature thresholds. But what is already clear is that the planet is rapidly warming and that nations haven’t done nearly enough to cut fossil-fuel pollution. And even if we did manage to halt it, warming-driven emissions could continue. So a number of the research groups involved in the recent studies have started to explore what nations could do to get feedback emissions under control. Scientists at the Woodwell Climate Research Center in Massachusetts are, for example, investigating the possibility of preventing fires or rapidly detecting them and putting them out when they threaten to burn the vegetation and soil that help insulate particularly carbon-rich wedges of permafrost (a subject I explored at length in a recent feature story). In addition, the Environmental Defense Fund’s Climate Innovation Initiative has backed lab research on the potential of adding sulfate, iron, or other compounds to wetlands to counteract fast-rising methane emissions. Several startups are also exploring possibilities. They’re looking into suppressing the lightning strikes that are igniting more and more fires in high latitudes, placing synthetic mats above permafrost to prevent it from melting, and deploying autonomous drones to quickly suppress potentially high-emissions fires before they burn out of control. Investigating all these ideas is worthwhile. But researchers are only in the earliest stages of figuring out how big a dent in emissions any such efforts could achieve across sprawling global ecosystems undergoing massive transformations. The surest path to reducing the effect of warming-driven warming remains the one we’ve known all along: breaking society’s addiction to fossil fuels. This article is from The Spark, MIT Technology Review’s weekly climate newsletter. To receive it in your inbox every Wednesday, sign up here. MIT Technology Review
Creating AI value with enterprise context
2026-10-28

Creating AI value with enterprise context

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-28

Visit Original Website
Across a business, any number of AI platforms, systems, and tools may be at work simultaneously—for example, AI-powered customer platforms, conversational assistants, and workflow and process automation tools. Each one is sophisticated, advanced, and quick to respond. But, when set to work, the outcomes and actions that emerge may be wrong, confused, or lacking a proper understanding of the business. Many organizations find themselves in this situation—and it could be one of key reasons they struggle to extract returns on their AI investments. An MIT Technology Review Insights poll finds that while every organization surveyed is using AI today to some degree, fewer than one in five (16%) are extracting transformative value from their deployments. The largest percentage of respondents (40%) say they are seeing moderate gains in specific tasks or teams, while 21% report meaningful gains, leaving 23% that are seeing minimal returns or none at all. Companies are mostly still piloting AI use cases (according to 38% of respondents) or using it to assist employees’ work with tools like co-pilots or chat assistants (35%). Just 12% of enterprises have AI agents in production and 3% say they are using AI to do most of the work in key processes. “There’s a big gap between what the models are capable of and how the models are actually being useful inside organizations,” says Prukalpa Sankar, co-founder of Atlan. “For every real-life intent and purpose, artificial general intelligence is already here. It’s just not really useful in most companies today.” The missing layer, Sankar says, is context, or how well AI understands a business. A context layer is the architectural infrastructure that sits between an organization’s business systems (for example, systems of record and data, and its AI platforms). It translates enterprise intelligence into governed, machine-usable context for AI agents or models and helps knowledge compound. “Without a context layer for AI, it’s almost like you’re hiring the smartest intern in the world, blindfolding them, and throwing them into your organization,” Sankar says. This content was produced by Insights, MIT Technology Review’s custom content arm, not its editorial staff. It was researched and written by humans, with any AI tools that may have been used limited to production processes under human oversight. Ten years after AlphaGo’s match against Go champion Lee Sedol, today’s AI still isn’t tapping into the machinery that made that win possible. NASA asks industry to rescue Hubble before it falls from orbit
2026-11-01

NASA asks industry to rescue Hubble before it falls from orbit

The Next Web | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Hubble photographed from the Space Shuttle Atlantis during its last servicing mission, in May 2009. Hubble has no engine of its own, and it is slowly sinking. NASA now wants companies to say how they could lift the 36-year-old telescope higher, service it, or steer it down safely, the agency said on Friday. The request for information went out on 29 October, and responses are due by 30 November. NASA’s analysis puts Hubble’s likely re-entry in 2033 or 2034. Any mission would need to fly by 2032. “If there are innovative and cost-effective ways to extend the life of an asset already on orbit, we owe it to the American taxpayer and the scientific community to explore them.” Jared Isaacman, NASA Administrator The request covers three options. A company could boost Hubble higher, service it to improve it, or dispose of it safely. Citing the document, Ars Technica reported that a boost must reach at least 600 km and must not harm the telescope. The company would supply the rocket, the servicing spacecraft and mission operations, plus a crew if one is needed. NASA is not committing to buy a mission, and it has no money set aside for one, Ars reported. Hubble now orbits about 470 km up. The shuttle Columbia last raised it, to 583 km, in 2002. NASA puts the chance of re-entry at 10% by September 2032, The Register reported. That rises to 50% by December 2033. Hubble is heavy enough that an uncontrolled fall carries a small risk to people on the ground. In 2022, Isaacman, then a private astronaut, offered to pay for most of a crewed Hubble mission on SpaceX’s Dragon. NASA also asked industry for ideas that year and received eight responses. In 2024 it decided not to pursue a reboost. A crewed visit is less likely now. SpaceX plans to retire Crew Dragon by 2030, and NASA has already turned to Boeing for more astronaut flights. Isaacman expects the request to draw interest from robotics companies, Ars reported. Startups are already raising money for in-orbit refuelling hardware. NASA’s smaller Swift observatory is about to burn up. Its science mission ended on 20 October. Below 275 km, drag made steady observations impossible, and NASA expects it to re-enter within weeks. NASA had hired a startup, Katalyst Space, to catch Swift and lift it higher. Its robot ran into trouble and never reached the telescope. The US Space Force now predicts Swift will come down within days, the Associated Press reported. Most of it will burn up, though a few dense tungsten parts and mirror pieces could survive. Hubble has one advantage over Swift. Astronauts fitted it with a soft-capture mechanism on the last shuttle visit in 2009, which should make docking easier. Astronomers expect no replacement for its visible and ultraviolet vision before NASA’s Habitable Worlds Observatory in the 2040s. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Google’s Suncatcher satellite runs AI in orbit and sends a haiku home

The Next Web | 2026-11-01

Visit Original Website
Google’s prototype data centre satellite has run an AI model in orbit and sent its answer back to Earth. The onboard Gemma 4 model wrote a haiku about being in space, Travis Beals, a senior director at Google, wrote in a blog post on Friday. The satellite launched on 1 October for Project Suncatcher, Google’s research effort to run AI computing in space. Google called the haiku the first AI response from a data centre class TPU, its own AI chip, in orbit. “Blue marble below / Data streams in silent grace / Starlight feeds my core.” Gemma 4, on the Suncatcher satellite, as published by Google Google sent the prompt during a pass over a ground station. The poem came back as the satellite flew over Svalbard, Norway. The team then tried space-themed limericks and prompts asking the satellite to report on its own systems. The prompt asked for a haiku written as a TPU orbiting Earth, Thomas Claburn reported for The Register. The satellite holds four TPUs and generates up to 1 kilowatt of power, Tim Fernholz reported for TechCrunch. Because of that power limit, it can run only one TPU at a time, for about 20 minutes. Testing its systems took about three weeks, and a Google spokesperson told TechCrunch the satellite was working normally. Omar Sanseviero, who works on Gemma at Google DeepMind, wrote on X that the model ran on stored solar energy. Google will now test how the TPUs cope with heat and radiation in orbit. Radiation can flip bits in a chip’s memory and corrupt data without warning. Google’s tests on the ground suggested this matters less for running models than for training them, according to TechCrunch. If the chips hold up, Google plans to launch two linked satellites in 2027. They will make more power and test laser links between satellites, TechCrunch reported. Google announced the launch in September. Startups also plan data centres in orbit, and Starcloud raised $250M in August. SpaceX has told investors its own orbital data centres may never work. Google’s plans also depend on SpaceX making its Starship rocket fully reusable, TechCrunch reported. Get the most important tech news in your inbox each week. Thanks! Gas turbines at xAI’s power plant in Southaven, Mississippi, which supplies its Colossus 2 data centre in Memphis, Tennessee. A federal judge in Mississippi has refused to throw out the NAACP’s Clean Air Act lawsuit against xAI. The case is over the gas turbines that power its Colossus 2 data centre. Judge Debra M. Brown ruled on Friday that the turbines are not exempt from air permits as “mobile sources”. She also rejected xAI’s argument that lawsuits brought by citizen groups under the Clean Air Act are unconstitutional. Earthjustice represents the NAACP with the Southern Environmental Law Center. It published the court’s opinion and a statement on the ruling. xAI is now part of SpaceX. “Because citizen suit plaintiffs sue on their own behalf, in their own name, and seek penalties that redress harm to themselves, they are not exercising the federal government’s executive power.” Judge Debra M. Brown, in her opinion The NAACP and its Mississippi State Conference sued xAI and its subsidiary MZX Tech in April. The complaint says the companies installed gas turbines at the Southaven plant between August and December 2025 without permits. By April there were at least 27 turbines, the complaint says. Together they can generate at least 495 megawatts. The NAACP says they could emit about 1,700 tons of nitrogen oxides a year, a main cause of smog. It wants a court to order pollution controls. It also seeks civil penalties of up to $124,426 a day for each violation. In July 2025, Mississippi’s environmental regulator told xAI the turbines would be exempt from permits. It called them mobile, because each sat on a trailer. It also called them temporary, because each would stay less than 12 months. The company argued it relied on that view. The judge found the regulator had “fairly notified” xAI of its position. But she said the court was not bound by it. Under the federal rules that Mississippi’s plan adopts, the plant is not a mobile source, she wrote. SpaceX has not commented publicly on the ruling. The same court ruled against the US Justice Department on 19 October. The department had asked it to dismiss the case, after it backed xAI in June. The court let the department join the case but not end it, the Southern Environmental Law Center said. The court has yet to rule on the NAACP’s request for a preliminary injunction. Sankar replied that founders lead both companies, and that each builds its strategy on what it believes. Anthropic has told investors its safety views could make it a political target.
